Chapter 4 Behaviour Therapy

In this chapter
  1. 4.1 Where Behaviour Therapy Came From and What It Assumes: explain the three origins of behaviour therapy, Eysenck's 1952 challenge, the learning model of disorder and why functional analysis comes first.
  2. 4.2 Counterconditioning and Systematic Desensitisation: describe the work of Jones and Wolpe, the principle of reciprocal inhibition, the anxiety hierarchy and the SUDS scale.
  3. 4.3 Exposure Therapies: Flooding, Implosion and Response Prevention: distinguish the forms of exposure and explain why exposure reduces fear.
  4. 4.4 Aversive Methods, the Bell and Pad, and EMDR: describe aversion therapy, covert sensitisation and the bell-and-pad method, and evaluate the EMDR debate.
  5. 4.5 Operant Techniques That Build Behaviour: apply reinforcement, shaping, chaining, prompting, the Premack principle, token economies and contingency management.
  6. 4.6 Operant Techniques That Reduce Behaviour, and Applied Behaviour Analysis: compare extinction, differential reinforcement, time-out, response cost, overcorrection and punishment, and describe the Lovaas programme for autism.
  7. 4.7 Modelling, Rehearsal, Assertiveness and Social Skills: describe Bandura's forms of modelling and the training of assertive and social behaviour.
  8. 4.8 Relaxation and Biofeedback: compare progressive muscle relaxation, autogenic training, breathing retraining, applied relaxation and the forms of biofeedback.
  9. 4.9 Habit Change and Self-Management: explain habit reversal, negative practice, thought stopping, self-monitoring, behavioural activation and the behavioural treatment of insomnia.
  10. 4.10 Paradoxical Intention, Parents and Classrooms: explain paradoxical intention, Patterson's parent management training and behavioural classroom management.
  11. 4.11 Evaluating Behaviour Therapy: judge what behaviour therapy does best, the symptom substitution debate and the ethics of aversive methods.

Weightage. 90 of the 298 questions mapped in this book (30.2%) test this chapter, 52 of them from real past papers. 5 of its 11 sections are High yield.

In the early 1920s, in a playroom of the Heckscher Foundation for Children in New York City, a little boy called Peter sat in a high chair eating food he liked. Peter was two years and ten months old, and terrified of rabbits. He was also afraid of white rats (at the first sight of one he had screamed and fallen flat on his back in a paroxysm of fear), and the fear had spread to a fur coat, a fur rug, feathers and cotton wool, but the rabbit frightened him most of all. On the far side of the room, inside a wire cage, sat a rabbit.

The young psychologist watching him was Mary Cover Jones, a graduate student who had heard John Broadus Watson lecture about Little Albert, the infant in whom Watson and Rosalie Rayner had deliberately created a fear of a white rat in 1920. Jones asked the obvious next question. If a fear can be learned, can it be unlearned? Her first approach was social. She let Peter play with three other children who happily handled the rabbit, and his fear began to fade. Then Peter caught scarlet fever and spent two months in hospital; on his way home a large dog jumped at him, and his fears came flooding back.

So Jones tried something more precise. Each day, while Peter was eating, the caged rabbit was brought a little closer: never so close that Peter stopped eating, always just close enough that he noticed it. Over the following weeks the cage crept from the far wall to the edge of the table. Then the rabbit was let out. By the end of the treatment Peter let it nibble his fingers, and his fear of the other furry objects had also faded.

Jones published the case in 1924. For almost thirty years hardly anyone took any notice. Psychoanalysis ruled the clinic, and a child eating crackers next to a rabbit did not look like psychotherapy. Yet everything in this chapter is already present in that playroom: a fear treated as learned behaviour rather than as a sign of hidden conflict; a response incompatible with fear (eating) paired with the feared object; a graded approach, one small step at a time; and other children serving as models. Keep Peter in mind. When we return to him at the end of the chapter you will be able to name every technique Jones used, and you will see why the man who later turned her method into a therapy called her the mother of behaviour therapy.

4.1 Where Behaviour Therapy Came From and What It AssumesMedium yield · 2 Qs

behaviour therapy is the application of experimentally established principles of learning (classical conditioning, operant conditioning and observational learning) to the assessment and treatment of psychological problems. Notice the two halves of that definition. The techniques come from the learning laboratory, and the therapy itself is run like an experiment: define the problem behaviour, measure it, change something, and measure it again. Before we look at the techniques, you need to know how this way of thinking reached the clinic, because the history explains both its strengths and its blind spots. (The learning principles themselves, Pavlov's dogs, Thorndike's cats and Skinner's box, are taught in the General Psychology book; here we take them as known and ask what clinicians did with them.)

Learning theory in search of a clinic

The idea that disorders are learned is older than behaviour therapy. In 1920 Watson and Rayner showed that a fear could be conditioned in an infant, and in 1924 Jones showed that one could be removed. In 1938 Orval Hobart Mowrer and his wife Willie Mae Mowrer built a bed-wetting alarm on conditioning principles (Section 4.4), and in 1932 Knight Dunlap described how a bad habit might be broken by practising it deliberately (Section 4.9). In 1950 John Dollard and Neal Miller even translated the whole of psychoanalysis into the language of learning theory in Personality and Psychotherapy. But none of this became a movement. Through the 1940s the clinic belonged to psychoanalysis, the learning laboratory belonged to experimental psychologists, and the two hardly spoke.

The spur: Eysenck's 1952 challenge

What changed? One provocative paper did a great deal. In 1952 Hans Jürgen Eysenck (1916 to 1997), a German-born psychologist working at the Maudsley Hospital in London, published "The effects of psychotherapy: an evaluation." Eysenck compared published improvement rates for neurotic patients given psychoanalysis or eclectic psychotherapy with rates for similar patients who received only custodial care or ordinary medical attention. Roughly two-thirds of neurotic patients, he concluded, improved within about two years whether or not they had psychotherapy; this improvement without treatment he called spontaneous remission. If talk therapy did no better than the passage of time, what was it for?

The paper was methodologically weak (the groups were not comparable, and "improvement" meant different things in different studies), and later meta-analyses overturned its conclusion. The details of that debate belong to Chapter 8. What matters here is its effect. Eysenck had demanded evidence, and he had an alternative ready: a therapy derived from learning theory, aimed at the symptom, and testable. In 1959 he published "Learning theory and behaviour therapy," and in 1960 an edited book, Behaviour Therapy and the Neuroses. His most quoted line states the new creed bluntly: there is no neurosis underlying the symptom, but merely the symptom itself; get rid of the symptom and you have eliminated the neurosis.

Three roots: Johannesburg, London and the United States

Behaviour therapy had not one birthplace but three, which grew up almost independently in the 1950s and merged in the 1960s (Figure 4.1 and Table 4.1).

South Africa. In Johannesburg, the psychiatrist Joseph Wolpe (1915 to 1997) grew dissatisfied with psychoanalysis while serving as an army doctor in the Second World War. His experiments on fear in cats (Section 4.2) led to the principle of reciprocal inhibition and to the technique of systematic desensitisation, published in his landmark book Psychotherapy by Reciprocal Inhibition (1958). Around him gathered younger South Africans, notably Arnold Allan Lazarus (1932 to 2013) and Stanley Rachman. Lazarus used the term "behaviour therapy" in a 1958 article in the South African Medical Journal, one of its first appearances in print. In 1960 Wolpe and Rachman published a scathing learning-theory critique of Freud's case of Little Hans, arguing that the boy's fear of horses was better explained by conditioning (he had seen a horse fall in the street) than by castration anxiety. Wolpe later moved to the United States, where he worked at Temple University in Philadelphia.

London. At the Institute of Psychiatry and the Maudsley Hospital, Eysenck supplied the theory and the polemics, while Monte Bernard Shapiro, head of clinical psychology training, supplied the method. Shapiro insisted that each patient be treated as a single-case experiment: measure the problem, form a hypothesis about what maintains it, intervene, and measure again. Rachman joined the Maudsley group, and in 1963 Eysenck founded the journal Behaviour Research and Therapy, the first journal devoted to the field.

The United States. Here the root was operant. Burrhus Frederic Skinner (1904 to 1990) had shown that behaviour is shaped by its consequences. In 1953 Skinner, Ogden Lindsley and Harry C. Solomon, studying the operant behaviour of psychotic patients at Metropolitan State Hospital in Waltham, Massachusetts, used the phrase "behavior therapy" in a research report, the earliest known use. Through the late 1950s and 1960s Skinner's students carried operant methods onto hospital wards, into schools and into homes for children with intellectual disability. This American strand usually called itself behaviour modification, and later applied behaviour analysis (ABA): the use of operant principles, with careful measurement, to change socially important behaviour. The Journal of Applied Behavior Analysis began in 1968, the same year in which Donald Baer, Montrose Wolf and Todd Risley defined the field's seven dimensions (applied, behavioural, analytic, technological, conceptually systematic, effective and showing generality). The Association for Advancement of Behavior Therapy, today the Association for Behavioral and Cognitive Therapies, was founded in 1966.

Table 4.1 The three origins of behaviour therapy.
RootKey peopleLearning baseLandmark
South Africa (Johannesburg)Joseph Wolpe, Arnold Lazarus, Stanley RachmanClassical conditioning (counterconditioning)Psychotherapy by Reciprocal Inhibition (Wolpe, 1958); Lazarus uses "behaviour therapy" in print (1958)
England (Maudsley Hospital, London)Hans Eysenck, Monte B. ShapiroHullian and Pavlovian learning theory; single-case experimentsEysenck's critique (1952); "Learning theory and behaviour therapy" (1959); Behaviour Research and Therapy (1963)
United StatesB. F. Skinner, Ogden Lindsley, Teodoro Ayllon, Nathan AzrinOperant conditioning"Behavior therapy" in a 1953 report; token economy (1968); Journal of Applied Behavior Analysis (1968)
Exam trap

"Who first used the term behaviour therapy?" has three defensible answers, so read the options. Skinner, Lindsley and Solomon (1953) used it first in an unpublished report; Lazarus (1958) used it in a published journal article; Eysenck (1959) made it famous. When "behaviour analysis" or "applied behaviour analysis" is asked about, the answer is always operant conditioning, not classical conditioning, modelling, cognitive learning or dream analysis.

The assumptions of behaviour therapy and the learning model of disorder

Strip away the techniques and behaviour therapy rests on a handful of assumptions. Every one of them was a deliberate contradiction of psychoanalysis.

  1. Most problem behaviour, normal or abnormal, is learned by the same principles, and what has been learned can be unlearned or replaced.
  2. The problem behaviour is the disorder, not a symptom of something deeper. Treatment therefore aims directly at changing the unwanted behaviour (and building better behaviour in its place), not at uncovering a hidden cause.
  3. The factors that keep a problem going now (its triggers and consequences) matter more for treatment than the history of how it started.
  4. Problems are defined in observable, measurable terms, and progress is measured, often daily.
  5. Treatment is specific: different problems call for different techniques, chosen because experiments show they work.
  6. The client is an active learner who practises between sessions, and the therapist is closer to a teacher or coach than to an interpreter of the unconscious.

Applied to anxiety, these assumptions produce the learning model of disorder: phobias, compulsions and many other problems are seen as conditioned responses kept alive by their consequences. The most influential version is the two-factor theory that Mowrer set out from 1947 onwards, shown in Figure 4.2. In the first factor, classical conditioning, a neutral stimulus is paired with something frightening and becomes a conditioned stimulus for fear: a child bitten by a dog now fears dogs. In the second factor, operant conditioning, the person learns to avoid or escape the feared stimulus, and because avoidance makes the fear drop, avoidance is negatively reinforced. Avoidance therefore keeps the phobia alive. Every time the person crosses the road to dodge a dog, the fear is relieved, the avoidance is strengthened, and the person never stays near a dog long enough to learn that dogs are mostly harmless. That last point is the whole rationale of exposure therapy (Section 4.3).

Two refinements matter for clinicians. In 1971 Martin Seligman pointed out that people develop phobias of snakes, heights and the dark far more readily than of electric sockets or cars, and proposed preparedness: evolution has primed us to learn some fears quickly. In 1977 Rachman noted that many phobic people recall no frightening encounter at all, and described three pathways to fear: direct conditioning, vicarious learning (watching someone else be afraid or hurt) and informational transmission (being told that something is dangerous). The learning model survived these refinements; it simply became broader. The operant side of the model is just as important. A child's tantrums may be kept going by the attention they win, and a patient's self-injury by the way it ends an unbearable demand. To find out which, the behaviour therapist begins every case with a functional analysis.

Functional analysis: the starting point of every case

A functional analysis (also called a behavioural analysis or functional behavioural assessment) is the identification of the observable events that trigger and maintain a specific problem behaviour, so that treatment can target those events. Its simplest form is the ABC model: the Antecedents (what happens just before the behaviour), the Behaviour itself (described so precisely that two observers would agree when it occurs) and the Consequences (what happens just after it). In 1965 Frederick Kanfer and George Saslow proposed a fuller scheme for adults, later written as SORKC: Stimulus, Organism (biological and learning history), Response, Kontingency (the schedule linking response to consequence) and Consequence.

Functional analysis starts by turning a vague complaint ("he is impossible at homework time") into a target behaviour with an operational definition ("screams, throws books or leaves the table within five minutes of being asked to begin"). The therapist then records a baseline, the level of the behaviour before treatment, using one or more measures: frequency (how often), duration (how long), latency (how soon after the trigger) and intensity. Data come from interviews, direct observation, rating scales, role-plays, physiological recording and self-monitoring. Let us push one case through.

Table 4.2 A worked ABC record: Arjun, aged seven, at homework time (one week of observation by his mother).
AntecedentBehaviourConsequenceTimes this week
Mother says "Start your maths now"Screams, throws the notebookMother takes the notebook away, says "Fine, do it later"5
Mother sits him at the table with a worksheetCries, kicks the chairSent to his room; plays with toys there3
Free play, no demandsNo tantrumNone0

Look at the pattern in Table 4.2. Tantrums happen only when a demand is made, and they reliably end the demand. The hypothesis is that Arjun's tantrums are maintained by escape from homework, that is, by negative reinforcement. Now notice what this does to treatment. The "obvious" remedy, sending him to his room (a time-out), actually rewards the tantrum, because it is one more escape from maths. A treatment that fits the function would keep the demand in place (escape extinction), break the work into small, easy chunks, reinforce compliance, and teach him an acceptable way to ask for a break. This is the logic of the whole approach: the same behaviour can serve different functions in different people, and treatment follows the function, not the form.

In 1982 Brian Iwata and his colleagues turned this logic into an experiment. They placed people who injured themselves in brief, controlled conditions (attention given after self-injury, a task demand removed after self-injury, a play condition and an alone condition) and watched which condition raised the behaviour. Their experimental functional analysis, and the many studies that later extended it (adding, for example, a condition in which a favourite item is handed back after self-injury), showed that self-injury can be maintained by social attention, by escape from demands, by access to tangible items, or by its own sensory consequences (automatic reinforcement), and that each function needs a different treatment.

One curious fact about assessment is worth knowing. When people begin to record their own behaviour (cigarettes smoked, snacks eaten, hairs pulled), the behaviour often changes before any treatment starts, usually in the desirable direction. This is reactivity: the process of observing or recording a behaviour alters the behaviour itself. Reactivity is a nuisance for measurement (the baseline is no longer a true baseline) but a gift for treatment, and self-monitoring is used as an intervention in its own right (Section 4.9).

Exam trap

"Behavioural therapies apply learning principles to..." Answer: eliminate the unwanted behaviour (and build adaptive behaviour). "Identify and treat the underlying causes" is the psychodynamic aim (Chapter 3); "improve learning and insight" describes insight therapies; "improve communication and social sensitivity" describes encounter and communication groups. And if a stem says that recording a behaviour changed it before treatment began, the answer is reactivity, not shaping, chaining or applied behaviour analysis.

Clinical link

Functional analysis is the behaviour therapist's version of case formulation (Chapter 2). In an Indian outpatient clinic you will often meet a "hysterical" adolescent whose fits multiply before examinations, or a child whose school refusal began after a minor illness. Asking "What happens just before? What happens just after? What does this behaviour get or avoid for the person?" frequently reveals an escape or attention function that no amount of advice will touch until the consequences change.

4.2 Counterconditioning and Systematic DesensitisationHigh yield · 24 Qs

Jones's treatment of Peter contained an idea that would wait thirty years for a theory: if a frightening stimulus is repeatedly paired with a pleasant or calming response, the calm can replace the fear. This section follows that idea from a New York playroom to a South African laboratory and into the most famous technique of behaviour therapy.

Counterconditioning: replacing one response with another

counterconditioning is the process of conditioning a new response that is incompatible with an existing conditioned response to the same stimulus, so that the stimulus comes to evoke the new response instead of the old one. For Peter, the old response to the rabbit was fear; the new, incompatible response was the contentment of eating. Because Jones kept the rabbit far enough away for eating to win, the rabbit gradually became a signal for calm, and the child learned, in effect, that the rabbit was no longer threatening. Jones also knew the risk: bring the rabbit too close too fast, and the fear might win and spread to the food. That single caution contains the reason every graded technique proceeds one small step at a time.

Counterconditioning is different from simple extinction. In extinction (Section 4.3) the conditioned stimulus is presented again and again without the unconditioned stimulus until the response fades; nothing new is attached to it. In counterconditioning a specific rival response is deliberately attached to the stimulus. Aversion therapy (Section 4.4) is counterconditioning in the opposite direction: an attractive stimulus such as alcohol is paired with nausea so that it comes to evoke aversion instead of craving.

Remember

Mary Cover Jones (1924) used two methods with Peter: social imitation (watching unafraid children, a form of modelling) and direct conditioning (eating while the rabbit came closer, which is counterconditioning). Joseph Wolpe later called her the mother of behaviour therapy.

Exam trap

"Counterconditioning is an exposure therapy technique that involves..." The keyed answer (RML 2022) is learning that an event or situation is no longer threatening, because a new, calm response has been attached to it. "Exposing the client to the feared stimulus" is tempting, since counterconditioning does include exposure, but it describes every exposure therapy (Section 4.3) and leaves out the incompatible response that makes counterconditioning what it is. "Learning that one negative event may be linked to another" describes how a conditioned fear is acquired in the first place, the reverse of the process. "Helping the client to see that the behaviour is counterproductive" is an insight or motivational aim (Chapters 3 and 6), not a conditioning procedure.

Wolpe's cats and the principle of reciprocal inhibition

Key study: Wolpe's experimental neurosis in cats (late 1940s)

Question. The American psychiatrist Jules Masserman had produced "experimental neuroses" in cats by making them choose between food and a blast of air, and argued that conflict was the cause. Wolpe, working in Johannesburg, asked whether conflict was necessary at all. Method. He placed cats in a small experimental cage and gave them a series of electric shocks, with no conflict involved. Result. The cats became intensely anxious in the cage, and the anxiety spread to the laboratory room and to rooms that resembled it. Even after days without food they would not eat in the cage. Wolpe then fed them in a room very unlike the laboratory, where their anxiety was slight, then in rooms progressively more like it, and finally in the cage itself. Step by step, eating returned and the anxiety disappeared. Conclusion. Anxiety is a learned response, and a response incompatible with it (here, eating), evoked while anxiety is weak, can inhibit and gradually eliminate it. Criticism. Generalising from shocked cats to human neurosis is a large leap, which is why the later controlled trials with people (described below) mattered so much.

Wolpe borrowed a term from the physiologist Charles Scott Sherrington, who had used reciprocal inhibition for the way contraction of one muscle inhibits its antagonist. Wolpe's reciprocal inhibition is the principle that two incompatible responses cannot occur at the same time, so that a response antagonistic to anxiety, evoked in the presence of an anxiety-provoking stimulus, suppresses the anxiety. In Wolpe's own (1958) formulation: if a response antagonistic to anxiety can be made to occur in the presence of anxiety-evoking stimuli so that it is accompanied by a complete or partial suppression of the anxiety responses, the bond between these stimuli and the anxiety responses will be weakened. Wolpe used several anxiety-inhibiting responses: deep muscle relaxation (the most important), assertive responses (for social anxiety, Section 4.7), sexual arousal (for sexual anxieties) and, with children, eating.

Systematic desensitisation step by step

systematic desensitisation is Wolpe's (1958) technique for reducing anxiety in which a client, while deeply relaxed, imagines (or later encounters) a graded series of anxiety-provoking situations, moving up the series only when each step can be faced without anxiety. It has three steps.

Step 1: relaxation training. The client is taught deep muscle relaxation, usually an abbreviated form of Edmund Jacobson's progressive muscle relaxation (Section 4.8), tensing and then releasing one muscle group after another. Wolpe typically spent around five or six sessions on relaxation and asked clients to practise for 10 to 15 minutes twice a day.

Step 2: constructing the anxiety hierarchy. An anxiety hierarchy is a list of situations related to the client's fear, ranked from the least to the most anxiety-provoking. Each item is rated on the subjective units of distress scale (SUDS), a scale from 0 (completely calm) to 100 (the worst anxiety imaginable) that Wolpe introduced in The Practice of Behavior Therapy (1969). Wolpe's original name was "subjective units of disturbance"; you will also see "discomfort" and "distress." A typical hierarchy has 10 to 20 items spaced roughly 5 to 10 units apart, and a client may need several hierarchies for several themes. Wolpe's case of Miss C., a 24-year-old art student who had failed examinations because of anxiety, needed four: examinations, being watched, being criticised, and seeing other people quarrel. To identify fears in the first place Wolpe and Peter J. Lang developed the Fear Survey Schedule (1964), a questionnaire listing common feared objects and situations.

Step 3: desensitisation proper. This is the step in which the client is gradually exposed to the anxiety-arousing situations. With the client relaxed (a SUDS of about 25 or less), the therapist describes the lowest item and asks the client to imagine it vividly for a few seconds. If the client feels any anxiety, he signals by raising a finger; the therapist immediately stops the scene and restores relaxation. A scene is repeated until it can be imagined with no anxiety at all, and only then does the client move to the next item. Only a few items are covered in a session. Miss C. needed 17 desensitisation sessions; four months later she sat her examinations without anxiety and passed.

Figure 4.3 shows a hierarchy for a case of the kind you might meet in a Mysuru clinic. Ravi, a 20-year-old college student, was bitten by a stray dog at the age of eight. He now takes long detours to college, will not visit friends who keep dogs, and has started to miss classes.

In session one Ravi, relaxed, imagines reading the word "dog" in the Star of Mysore. He feels nothing, so after two clear repetitions the therapist moves to the photograph of a puppy. At the third item, the video, Ravi raises a finger; the scene is stopped, relaxation is deepened, and the scene is presented again, more briefly. By the end of the session he can imagine the video calmly. Over later sessions he climbs the ladder. Notice that the client is never asked to face more than he can tolerate while relaxed; that is what makes the method systematic.

In vitro and in vivo desensitisation, and other variants

Wolpe's classic procedure is conducted in imagination, sometimes called in vitro desensitisation (Latin, "in glass," that is, in the consulting room). In vivo desensitisation ("in life") takes the client up the hierarchy in real situations: Ravi might actually stand across the road from a leashed dog, then closer, while using his relaxation skills. In vivo work is generally more powerful because nothing has to transfer from imagination to reality, but imaginal work is useful when real situations are impractical (flying, thunderstorms, examinations) or too frightening to begin with.

Several variants followed. In 1962 Lazarus and Arnold Abramovitz introduced emotive imagery for children, who find muscle relaxation hard: the child imagines a favourite hero or story in which the feared situation is woven, so that excitement and pleasure, rather than relaxation, do the inhibiting. Contact desensitisation adds the therapist's physical guidance and modelling (it overlaps with participant modelling, Section 4.7). Desensitisation has also been run in groups, by tape recording and as self-directed homework, and has been used for anger, nightmares, insomnia and some speech problems as well as phobias.

Does desensitisation work, and why?

Wolpe himself reported that roughly nine out of ten of his patients were cured or much improved, but those were uncontrolled case series. Controlled evidence came quickly. In 1963 Lang and A. David Lazovik showed that desensitisation reduced snake phobia in students compared with an untreated group. The most influential test was a comparison with insight therapy.

Key study: Paul's insight versus desensitisation (1966)

Question. Is systematic desensitisation better than traditional insight-oriented therapy, or does it work only because clients expect to improve? Method. Gordon L. Paul treated university students with severe public-speaking anxiety. Experienced therapists gave five sessions of either insight-oriented psychotherapy or systematic desensitisation; a third group received an attention-placebo (a placebo pill and a dull task presented as training in stress tolerance), and a fourth group no treatment. Anxiety was measured by self-report, physiological recording and observers' ratings during a real speech. Result. Desensitisation produced the greatest improvement on every kind of measure; insight therapy did no better than the attention-placebo, though both beat no treatment. Gains held at follow-up, with no sign of new symptoms appearing. Conclusion. A specific behavioural technique beat a non-specific talking therapy for a specific fear. Criticism. Five sessions is very short for an insight approach, so the comparison was arguably unfair to it. On the other side, the therapists were experienced insight therapists who learned desensitisation only for the study, which if anything should have favoured insight therapy.

Later "dismantling" studies took the procedure apart to see which pieces mattered, and the answer surprised Wolpe. Desensitisation worked without relaxation, and it worked without a strict hierarchy; what it could not do without was exposure to the feared stimulus. Researchers such as the Maudsley psychiatrist Isaac Marks concluded that exposure is the active ingredient, and that relaxation mainly helps clients tolerate it. Today reciprocal inhibition is regarded as Wolpe's theory of why desensitisation works rather than the established mechanism; extinction and habituation (Section 4.3) and growing self-efficacy (Section 4.7) explain the results at least as well. Because the procedure is slow, it is used less now than in the 1970s, but it remains valuable for children, for very fearful clients who refuse more intense exposure, and whenever the feared situation cannot be arranged in real life.

Exam trap

Systematic desensitisation is based on reciprocal inhibition (Wolpe), and the trap options never change: operant extinction, operant shaping, law of effect (Thorndike), habituation alone, stimulus generalisation, vicarious reinforcement, modelling, punishment, catharsis, adaptation to stress, aversive conditioning, cognitive disputation (Ellis, Chapter 5) and Mowrer's two-factor theory, which explains why a fear persists, not how desensitisation removes it. The relaxation method used in it, progressive muscle relaxation, is Jacobson's, not Wolpe's. The three steps are relaxation training, hierarchy construction and desensitisation proper; "cognitive restructuring" is not one of them. Its main use is phobias and other anxieties, never hallucinations, delusions or depression.

Exam trap

SUDS belongs to Wolpe and was built for systematic desensitisation, which is the keyed answer to "SUDS is mostly used under..." (IHBAS 2020). Be aware that every modern exposure therapy, including exposure and response prevention, also uses SUDS ratings, so pick systematic desensitisation only when it is offered. The other options in that question point elsewhere: multimodal therapy is Arnold Lazarus's later BASIC ID approach, and mindfulness-based stress reduction is Jon Kabat-Zinn's (both Chapter 5). On the rating scale itself: the Fear Survey Schedule (Wolpe and Lang) lists feared objects, the State-Trait Anxiety Inventory (Spielberger) and the Hamilton Anxiety Rating Scale (Hamilton) are standardised anxiety measures (the Assessment book); none is the 0 to 100 hierarchy scale.

Clinical link

Examination anxiety, dental fear, fear of injections before surgery and fear of dogs are common reasons for referral in Indian practice, and many clients cannot be taken into the real situation in the first session. Imaginal desensitisation lets you begin at once; then shift to in vivo steps as soon as the client is able, because real-life practice generalises better.

Scan Your Brain

  4. The A, B and C of a functional analysis stand for antecedents, behaviour and .
  5. When recording a behaviour changes it before treatment begins, the effect is called .

  7. Wolpe's principle that two incompatible responses cannot occur together is called inhibition.

Answers: (1) spontaneous remission, (2) analysis, (3) negative, (4) consequences, (5) reactivity, (6) rabbit, (7) reciprocal, (8) distress (or disturbance), (9) vitro, (10) public-speaking.

4.3 Exposure Therapies: Flooding, Implosion and Response PreventionHigh yield · 13 Qs

The dismantling studies at the end of Section 4.2 left behaviour therapists with an awkward conclusion. Wolpe's relaxation training, his carefully spaced hierarchy and his theory of reciprocal inhibition were not what made desensitisation work; contact with the feared thing was. If exposure is the active ingredient, why not go straight to it? Why spend six sessions learning to relax and then creep up a ladder of imagined scenes when the client could simply face the fear? This section answers that question. It describes the family of exposure therapies, explains the learning processes that make them work, follows their two most dramatic forms, flooding and implosion, and ends with exposure and response prevention, the treatment that turned obsessive-compulsive disorder from one of the most hopeless conditions in psychiatry into one of the most treatable.

Exposure: the common ingredient

exposure therapy is any treatment in which a person repeatedly or continuously confronts a feared object, situation, thought, memory or bodily sensation, without escaping or avoiding it, until the fear declines. Every technique in this section is a variation on that one idea. The variations differ along four dimensions, and if you can name the four you can classify any exposure technique an examiner describes.

The first dimension is intensity. In graded exposure the client works up a hierarchy, beginning with situations that provoke moderate anxiety and moving to harder ones as each becomes manageable; it is, in effect, desensitisation without the relaxation. In intense exposure the client begins at or near the top of the hierarchy, which is flooding. The second dimension is the medium. In imaginal exposure the client confronts the feared situation in vivid imagination, guided by the therapist's description or by his own spoken account; in in vivo exposure he confronts the real thing. The third is duration and spacing: sessions can be long or short, packed into consecutive days or spread over weeks. The fourth is who directs it. Exposure can be accompanied by the therapist, or it can be prescribed as homework that the client carries out alone; Isaac Marks and his colleagues at the Maudsley found that many agoraphobic patients improved almost as much with well-instructed self-exposure, guided by a manual and a daily diary, as with exposure in which a therapist went along.

As a rule in vivo exposure works faster and generalises better than imaginal exposure, because nothing has to transfer from the mind's eye to the street. Imaginal exposure remains essential in three situations: when the feared event cannot be arranged (a thunderstorm, an earthquake, an aeroplane crash), when the feared thing is itself a memory (a trauma) and when the feared thing is a consequence that will never actually happen (the obsessional patient's fear that his family will die because he did not wash). Two newer forms of exposure stretch the method to fears that cannot be met in an ordinary consulting room.

interoceptive exposure is the deliberate provocation of feared bodily sensations. In panic disorder (described in the Psychiatric Disorders book) what the person fears most is not a place but his own body: a pounding heart that might mean a heart attack, dizziness that might mean fainting, breathlessness that might mean suffocation. In the panic control treatment that David H. Barlow and Michelle Craske developed in the late 1980s, the client spins in a swivel chair to bring on dizziness, overbreathes for a minute to bring on tingling and light-headedness, breathes through a narrow drinking straw to bring on breathlessness, or runs on the spot to raise the heart rate. The sensations are produced again and again, in the safety of the clinic and then at home, until they lose their power to signal catastrophe. (The cognitive model of panic that sits beside this behavioural method is in Chapter 5.)

virtual reality exposure therapy places the client in a computer-generated environment, viewed through a headset, which changes as the client moves. In 1995 Barbara Olasov Rothbaum and her colleagues in Atlanta published the first controlled trial: students afraid of heights rode virtual glass lifts and crossed virtual bridges, and they improved far more than a waiting-list group. Virtual reality has since been used for fears of flying, driving, public speaking and spiders, and for post-traumatic stress in soldiers, who revisit simulated combat scenes. Meta-analyses find it roughly as effective as in vivo exposure for specific phobias. Its main drawback is cost, although inexpensive headsets driven by a smartphone have brought simple versions within reach of clinics in India. Table 4.3 gathers the forms of exposure taught in this section.

Table 4.3 The forms of exposure therapy.
FormWhat the client confrontsTypical useExample
Graded in vivo exposureReal situations, one step at a timeSpecific phobias, agoraphobia, social anxietyRavi stands a little nearer a leashed dog each session
Imaginal exposureVivid mental scenes or memoriesFears that cannot be staged; trauma memories; obsessional feared consequencesDescribing an aeroplane in heavy turbulence
FloodingThe most feared situation at full intensity, with no escapePhobias, agoraphobia (with the client's informed choice)Standing on a high balcony until the fear subsides
Implosive therapyExaggerated, horrific imagined scenes with psychodynamic themesPhobias and anxiety (historical; now rare)Imagining a snake coiling round the body and biting the face
Interoceptive exposureFeared bodily sensationsPanic disorderSpinning in a chair to bring on dizziness
Virtual reality exposureComputer-generated scenesHeights, flying, combat traumaRiding a virtual glass lift
Exposure and response preventionObsessional triggers, with rituals blockedObsessive-compulsive disorderTouching the floor and then eating without washing

Exposure can also be compressed. In 1989 the Swedish psychologist Lars-Göran Öst described a one-session treatment for specific phobias: a single session of up to three hours in which graded in vivo exposure and therapist modelling are combined, so that a spider-phobic client might move, in one afternoon, from looking at a spider in a closed jar to letting it walk across her hand. Most patients treated in this way are much improved, and the method is now widely used with children.

Why does exposure work?

Exposure is the best-supported psychological treatment for anxiety, yet psychologists still argue about why it works. Four answers are worth knowing, because each one has practical consequences and examiners ask about the first two by name.

Habituation. habituation is the decline in a response to a stimulus that is presented repeatedly or continuously without any significant consequence. You stop hearing the ceiling fan after a few minutes; the new resident of a house beside the railway line stops waking for the night train after a few weeks. Applied to fear, habituation means that anxiety cannot stay at its peak forever. If a person stays in a frightening situation that turns out to be harmless, the racing heart, the sweating and the urge to flee rise, reach a plateau and then begin to fall, both within a session and from one session to the next. The opposite process is sensitisation, an increase in a response after repeated exposure to a stimulus, which is most likely when the stimulus is intense and the exposure brief. Eysenck added a warning that he called incubation: a brief exposure that ends while fear is at its height may make the fear worse, because escaping at the peak is itself powerfully reinforcing. The practical rule follows directly. Exposure sessions should be long enough for anxiety to fall substantially (many therapists use the rule of thumb that the session continues until the client's SUDS rating has dropped by about half). In 1973 Richard Stern and Marks found that two hours of continuous exposure helped agoraphobic patients more than the same two hours broken into four half-hour sessions.

Extinction. extinction is the weakening of a learned response when it is no longer followed by what originally maintained it: in classical conditioning, the conditioned stimulus is presented again and again without the unconditioned stimulus; in operant conditioning, the response is no longer followed by the reinforcer. In exposure both kinds happen at once. The dog (the conditioned stimulus) appears again and again without a bite, so the conditioned fear weakens; and because the client does not escape, the avoidance response no longer earns its reward of relief. Look back at Mowrer's two-factor theory in Figure 4.2: exposure attacks the first factor by extinction and the second by preventing the negatively reinforced escape.

Key study: Solomon's avoiding dogs (1953)

Question. Why do avoidance responses, and the fears behind them, last so long after the danger has gone? Method. At Harvard, Richard Lester Solomon and his colleagues placed dogs in a shuttle box, a chamber divided by a low barrier. A signal was followed a few seconds later by a strong electric shock through the floor; by jumping the barrier the dog could escape the shock, and soon it learned to jump when the signal came, avoiding the shock altogether. Then the shock was disconnected. Result. The dogs went on jumping at the signal for hundreds of trials without ever being shocked again. Each jump was quick and apparently calm. When the experimenters put a barrier in the way, so that the dog could not jump and had to stay with the signal, the dogs were at first intensely agitated; blocking the jump in this way (in the original study, most effectively in combination with other procedures) was what finally weakened the avoidance, and later experiments with rats confirmed that preventing the avoidance response speeds extinction. Conclusion. Avoidance protects the fear from extinction, because the animal never stays long enough to discover that the danger has gone; blocking the escape (response prevention) allows extinction. Criticism. Dogs in a shuttle box are not people with phobias, and the procedure was severe. But the finding gave flooding and response prevention their rationale, and it fits the clinical picture uncannily well.

Emotional processing. In 1986 Edna B. Foa and Michael J. Kozak proposed emotional processing theory, which holds that fear is stored in memory as a network (a fear structure) linking the feared stimuli, the person's responses to them and their meaning ("dogs are dangerous; my pounding heart means I am in danger"). For exposure to change the network, two things must happen: the fear structure must be activated (the client must actually feel afraid during exposure) and information incompatible with it must be taken in (the dog does not attack; the anxiety falls on its own). Foa and Kozak named three signs that processing is happening: fear is activated at the start, it declines within the session, and its peak declines from one session to the next. This is why exposure that is done while the client is distracted, or while she secretly reassures herself, tends to work poorly.

Inhibitory learning. Extinction does not erase the original learning. Experiments by Mark E. Bouton from the 1980s onwards showed that an extinguished fear can return in at least three ways: with the mere passage of time (spontaneous recovery), when the person meets the feared stimulus in a context different from the one where extinction took place (renewal) and when the original frightening event, or anything like it, happens again (reinstatement). Extinction, on this view, lays down a new, inhibitory memory ("dogs are safe") which competes with the old excitatory one ("dogs are dangerous"), and the old one is still there. Craske and her colleagues (2008, 2014) built this inhibitory learning model into practical advice: design each exposure so that it violates the client's specific prediction ("I will faint"; ask her what she expects, then test it), vary the stimuli and the settings, drop the small safety behaviours (holding a rail, carrying a water bottle, sitting near the door) that let her believe she survived only because of them, and do not insist that anxiety must fall within every session, because the learning matters more than the curve. Remember Peter from the opening story: after months of progress, a large dog jumped at him and his fears came flooding back. That was reinstatement, and it is exactly what the inhibitory learning model predicts.

A fifth answer, that successful exposure raises the client's belief that he can cope, belongs to Albert Bandura and is described with modelling in Section 4.7. In practice all five processes probably operate together. What they agree on is the instruction a behaviour therapist gives a frightened client: do not leave, do not avoid, stay with it, and find out what really happens.

Exam trap

"In exposure therapy, habituation refers to..." The answer is reduction in anxiety through repeated encounter with the feared stimulus (IHBAS 2025). The distractors each name a different process: increasing fear over time is sensitisation (or Eysenck's incubation); avoiding feared stimuli is avoidance, the behaviour exposure is designed to stop; creating fear associations is fear conditioning, how the problem began. Keep the principles straight: systematic desensitisation rests on reciprocal inhibition (Wolpe's theory), whereas flooding and exposure and response prevention rest on habituation and extinction.

Flooding

flooding is exposure to the most feared stimulus at full intensity, for a prolonged period and with escape prevented, until the anxiety subsides; there is no hierarchy and no relaxation. It can be done in vivo or in imagination. Where desensitisation lets a frightened person into a cold river one toe at a time, flooding pushes him in and keeps him there until the water no longer feels cold.

The idea came from two directions. One was the animal laboratory: Solomon's dogs, and later studies in which rats and dogs lost their avoidance responses fastest when the escape route was blocked. The other was the clinic. In 1959 the London psychiatrist Nathaniel Malleson described treating a patient's panic by asking him to imagine, as intensely as possible, the very situation he dreaded, instead of escaping from it, until the terror subsided. Through the late 1960s and 1970s Marks and his colleagues at the Maudsley compared flooding with systematic desensitisation for agoraphobia and specific phobias and found flooding at least as effective, and usually quicker. Their trials also taught the practical lessons listed above: in vivo is better than imagination, long sessions better than short ones, and real exposure better than any amount of talking about it.

Let us push one case through. Kiran, a 31-year-old software engineer in Bengaluru, has been afraid of heights since college. His company has moved to the fourteenth floor of a glass tower, and he has started to take the stairs to avoid the lift with a view and to refuse meetings in rooms near the windows. After assessment the therapist explains the choice between graded exposure and flooding, describes how anxiety rises and then falls if one stays, and warns him honestly that the first session will be very unpleasant. Kiran chooses flooding because he wants quick results. Before they begin she asks what he expects: "I'll lose control and feel pulled over the railing." On the first morning they go together to the open terrace on the fourteenth floor. Kiran stands at the railing and looks down; his SUDS rating is 95 within a minute. The therapist does not distract him and does not let him hold the railing tightly or keep his eyes on the horizon. She asks him to describe what he sees and what he feels, and every ten minutes she asks for a rating: 95, 90, 85, 70, 60. After about an hour the rating is 35. He has not lost control or been pulled over. They return the next two mornings; the first rating on the third day is 50, and it falls to 15. By the end of the week Kiran takes the glass lift on his own.

Flooding works fast and it works well, but it asks a great deal. Clients must understand what they are choosing and give genuine informed consent, which is why, as Richard Sharf notes, behaviour therapists offer flooding as an option rather than impose it. Sessions must be long enough for the anxiety to fall; a session that ends at the peak risks sensitisation. Some clients drop out, and some therapists are reluctant to cause so much distress. It is usually avoided for people with medical conditions that make extreme arousal dangerous, such as heart disease. For most phobias today, therapists prefer graded in vivo exposure that climbs quickly, which keeps most of the speed of flooding with less of its terror.

Exam trap

"Flooding differs from systematic desensitisation mainly because flooding..." exposes the person to the most feared stimulus at full intensity, with no hierarchy and no relaxation, until the anxiety dies down (NIMHANS 2026 recall). Pairing the feared stimulus with an unpleasant stimulus such as a shock is aversion therapy (Section 4.4); pairing it with relaxation one step at a time is desensitisation; and flooding is not limited to imagination (it can be in vivo or imaginal; it is implosive therapy that is purely imaginal). The person afraid of heights who stands on a balcony for an extended period with no gradual build-up or relaxation is being flooded (Real PYQ 2024), not desensitised, modelled or cognitively restructured. Replacing irrational beliefs with rational ones is Ellis's rational emotive behaviour therapy, and rewarding successive approximations is shaping (Section 4.5).

Implosive therapy

If flooding is the realistic version of intense exposure, implosive therapy is the nightmare version. implosive therapy (implosion) is a form of imaginal flooding in which the therapist describes exaggerated, horrifying scenes built around the client's fear and around psychodynamic themes presumed to lie behind it, keeping the client's anxiety as high as possible until it extinguishes. It was developed by the American psychologist Thomas Stampfl, first described in print in 1966 and set out with his colleague Donald J. Levis in 1967 in a paper whose title shows its unusual ancestry: "Essentials of implosive therapy: a learning-theory-based psychodynamic behavioral therapy."

Stampfl and Levis accepted Mowrer's account of fear and avoidance, but they argued that the cues a frightened person avoids form a chain, the avoidance serial cue hierarchy, running from the obvious external stimulus (the snake) to deeper cues that the person avoids even thinking about. Some of these deeper cues, they suggested, are the themes psychoanalysis had described: aggression, guilt, rejection, punishment and oral, anal and sexual fears. The therapist therefore weaves them into the scenes whether or not the client agrees that they apply. A woman afraid of snakes might be asked to imagine a snake sliding over her feet, then coiling round her body, then biting her face and forcing its way into her mouth, while she is helpless to stop it. The therapist does not stop when anxiety rises; he raises it further, aiming at an "implosion" (an inward explosion) of anxiety, and continues until the scene no longer has its effect.

The research on implosion was mixed. It sometimes worked, but no better than realistic flooding, and the psychodynamic content added nothing that could be shown. Because it is so unpleasant, and because the evidence did not justify the extra horror, it is rarely used today; Sharf describes both imaginal flooding and implosive therapy as uncommon now. Its place in the exam is secure, however, because it sits so neatly beside flooding and desensitisation in option lists. Table 4.4 sets the main exposure techniques side by side.

Table 4.4 Desensitisation, flooding, implosion and exposure and response prevention compared.
FeatureSystematic desensitisationFloodingImplosive therapyExposure and response prevention
Developed byWolpe (1958)Malleson (1959); Marks's groupStampfl (1966); Stampfl and Levis (1967)Meyer (1966); refined by Foa
PrincipleReciprocal inhibition (counterconditioning)Extinction and habituationExtinctionHabituation and extinction
HierarchyYes, climbed slowlyNo: starts at the topNo: maximal scenesUsually graded, reaching the top within days
RelaxationYesNoNoNo
MediumMostly imaginationIn vivo or imaginationImagination onlyIn vivo, plus imagination of feared consequences
ScenesRealisticRealisticExaggerated, with psychodynamic themesReal triggers
