Exam trap

Implosion and flooding are both intense exposure working by extinction, but they differ in three ways: implosion is always imaginal, its scenes are exaggerated beyond reality, and it adds psychodynamic themes. Its founder is Thomas Stampfl (with Donald Levis), not Wolpe. Do not confuse implosive therapy with the "implosive layer" of Fritz Perls's Gestalt therapy, one of the five layers of neurosis (phony, phobic, impasse, implosive, explosive) taught in Chapter 6.

Exposure and response prevention for obsessive-compulsive disorder

Until the middle of the 1960s obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD) was regarded as one of the least treatable of the neuroses. Psychoanalysis had made little impression on it, and some severely ill patients were offered psychosurgery. The behavioural account of the disorder pointed to a different strategy. An obsession (an intrusive, unwanted thought, image or urge, such as "my hands are contaminated with germs that will kill my children") raises anxiety sharply. A compulsion (a repetitive act, such as washing or checking, or a mental act, such as silently repeating a prayer) brings the anxiety down within minutes. Because the compulsion ends an aversive state, it is negatively reinforced and grows stronger with every repetition. And because the person always performs it, she never discovers that the anxiety would have subsided on its own and that the disaster would not have happened. (The disorder itself is described in the Psychiatric Disorders book; here we are concerned with its treatment.)

The treatment follows from the model. exposure and response prevention (ERP) is a treatment for OCD in which the client is deliberately exposed to the situations and thoughts that trigger her obsessions and is prevented from performing the compulsions that would normally neutralise the anxiety, so that the anxiety can habituate and the feared consequences can be disconfirmed. It has two parts, and both are needed: exposure (touch the contaminated object; leave the gas tap unchecked; think the forbidden thought) and response prevention (do not wash; do not go back to check; do not seek reassurance).

Key study: Meyer's response prevention (1966)

Question. Animal studies such as Solomon's had shown that blocking an avoidance response allows the fear behind it to extinguish. Would the same hold for the rituals of OCD? Method. Victor Meyer, a psychologist at the Middlesex Hospital in London, treated two inpatients with severe, long-standing washing and checking rituals. Hospital nurses supervised the patients around the clock and stopped them from carrying out their rituals, at first completely, while the patients were deliberately brought into contact with the things they feared. Meyer called his paper "Modification of expectations in cases with obsessional rituals," because his idea was that the patients expected a catastrophe if they did not perform the ritual, and that preventing the ritual would let them find out that the catastrophe did not come. Result. Both patients improved markedly, and the improvement held when supervision was gradually withdrawn. In later work Meyer and his colleagues reported that most of a larger series of patients were much improved or free of symptoms. Conclusion. Rituals that had resisted every other treatment yielded to exposure combined with prevention of the ritual. Criticism. Two uncontrolled cases prove nothing on their own, and round-the-clock supervision was costly. The controlled trials that followed, however, confirmed the method.

Through the early 1970s Rachman, Ray Hodgson and Marks at the Maudsley refined Meyer's method into a brief outpatient treatment, and later studies by Foa and her colleagues showed why both halves are needed: exposure alone mainly reduced the anxiety the obsessions caused, response prevention alone mainly reduced the rituals, and only the combination reduced both. It was Foa, more than anyone, who turned ERP into a standard, manualised treatment and tested it in large trials; she calls it exposure and ritual prevention. In its classic intensive form, after four to six hours of assessment, the client attends daily sessions of about two hours, five days a week for three weeks, does further exposure as homework, and follows strict rules about washing (for a washer, perhaps one short shower every few days). Exposure is graded but moves quickly: in a case that Sharf reports from Foa's group, the first day's items were those rated below 60 on the SUDS, and the top of the hierarchy was reached within the first week, and the hardest items were repeated in the second. Imaginal exposure is added for the disasters the client fears but which cannot be staged ("I picture my son in hospital with an infection, and the doctors saying it was my fault"). Richard Sharf summarises the research: ERP helps more than 70 percent of patients with OCD, and the gains last. In a large trial published in 2005 Foa and her colleagues found intensive ERP more effective than the drug clomipramine, and adding the drug to ERP did little to improve on ERP alone.

Consider Meera, a 28-year-old woman in Mysuru who has been washing for up to four hours a day since a jaundice outbreak in her neighbourhood two years ago. She bathes after any visit outside the house, washes her hands until the skin cracks, and asks her husband to change his clothes at the door. Her mother-in-law has begun to rewash the vessels to calm her. The therapist and Meera build a hierarchy: touching the inside door handle (SUDS 40), the newspaper (50), the gate latch (60), her own chappals (75), the floor of a city bus (85), the door of a public toilet (95). The ground rules are agreed with the whole family: Meera will not wash her hands except before cooking and after using the toilet, will take one ten-minute bath a day, and will not ask for reassurance; her family will not rewash anything for her and will answer requests for reassurance with a kind, agreed phrase ("We decided not to answer that"). On the third day she presses her palms on the soles of her chappals, rubs her hands over her face and arms, and then eats a biscuit with those hands. Her anxiety rises to 85 within a quarter of an hour and then, as she sits with the therapist and does not wash, slowly falls. Figure 4.4 shows what she has learned.

Three practical points make the difference between ERP that works and ERP that fails. First, response prevention must cover covert rituals as well as overt ones. A client who stops washing but silently repeats a protective mantra, or who mentally reviews whether she touched anything, is still ritualising, and the anxiety never has to fall on its own. Second, reassurance-seeking is a ritual. "Are you sure the stove is off?" asked of a spouse twenty times a day neutralises anxiety just as checking does, so families are taught to stop supplying it. Third, family accommodation (relatives washing, checking or reassuring on the patient's behalf, or rearranging the household around the rituals) is common in Indian joint families, where many people share the work of keeping the patient calm. It is kindly meant but it maintains the disorder, and bringing the family into treatment, as in Meera's case, is often essential. For clients whose obsessions have no visible ritual, exposure is made to the thought itself, for example by writing a short script of the feared thought and listening to a recording of it until it loses its sting.

Exam trap

ERP is the first-line psychological treatment for obsessive-compulsive disorder (RML 2024; IHBAS 2020), and selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors are the first-line drugs. Exposure is used for social anxiety, claustrophobia, specific phobias and panic disorder too, but response prevention is central only in OCD, because only OCD is maintained by rituals. It is not the treatment for depression (behavioural activation, Section 4.9), insomnia (stimulus control, Section 4.9), schizophrenia (antipsychotic medication with psychosocial treatment) or personality disorders, and hypnotherapy, free association or "systematic desensitisation only" are not first-line for OCD. ERP rests on habituation and extinction (IHBAS 2024), not counterconditioning or reciprocal inhibition, because no incompatible response such as relaxation is trained. "Graded exposure" is tempting because ERP hierarchies are usually graded, but grading is the format, not the principle.

Exam trap

Who "operationalised" ERP? Edna Foa; Victor Meyer introduced it in 1966. The distractors in such questions are well-known names from elsewhere: Aaron Beck founded cognitive therapy, Marsha Linehan dialectical behaviour therapy (both Chapter 5), and Joseph LeDoux is the neuroscientist who mapped the fear circuits of the amygdala (Physiological Psychology book).

Prolonged exposure for post-traumatic stress

Foa carried the logic of exposure from obsessions to trauma. In post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD) the person avoids the memory of the trauma and every reminder of it, and the avoidance, as always, keeps the fear alive. prolonged exposure (PE) is the manualised treatment for PTSD developed by Foa and her colleagues from the late 1980s (Rothbaum among them), in which the client repeatedly revisits the trauma memory in imagination and gradually approaches the safe situations he has been avoiding. Treatment usually takes about 8 to 15 weekly sessions of 90 minutes and has four parts:

  1. Psychoeducation about common reactions to trauma and the rationale for facing, rather than avoiding, the memory.
  2. Breathing retraining, a slow, calm breathing exercise that gives the client one simple coping skill.
  3. In vivo exposure to situations that are objectively safe but have been avoided because they are reminders: the road where the accident happened, crowded markets, driving at night.
  4. Imaginal exposure, called revisiting: the client closes his eyes and recounts the trauma aloud, in the present tense and in detail, for 30 to 45 minutes, again and again across sessions; the account is recorded and he listens to it daily at home. Each revisiting is followed by processing, a conversation about what he noticed and what it means.

Prolonged exposure is one of the best-supported treatments for PTSD. Many therapists fear that it will retraumatise clients or drive them away, but trials have found drop-out from PE broadly similar to drop-out from other active trauma therapies. Its main rival, cognitive processing therapy, which works chiefly by challenging the beliefs the trauma left behind, was developed by Patricia Resick and is taught with the cognitive therapies in Chapter 5.

Exam trap

"Prolonged exposure for PTSD was developed primarily by..." Foa and colleagues. Cognitive processing therapy is Resick's, eye movement desensitisation and reprocessing is Francine Shapiro's (Section 4.4) and dialectical behaviour therapy is Linehan's. When a question describes a client "asked to relive the traumatic event in imagination," that is imaginal exposure, the core of prolonged exposure; it is not a description of EMDR, which adds eye movements and keeps exposure to the memory brief.

Clinical link

Exposure is the most powerful tool a clinical psychologist has for anxiety, and also the most underused. The obstacle is usually the therapist's anxiety, not the client's: it is uncomfortable to ask a frightened person to do the thing she fears, and tempting to stay with relaxation, reassurance and discussion, which are pleasant for everyone and change little. Good exposure therapy is collaborative (the client understands the rationale and chooses each step), planned (a written hierarchy and an agreed rule about rituals) and persistent (sessions long enough, practice daily). Clients forgive a therapist for discomfort far more readily than for months of treatment that does not work.

4.4 Aversive Methods, the Bell and Pad, and EMDRHigh yield · 9 Qs

Every technique so far in this chapter has used conditioning to take an unwanted emotion away: Peter's fear of the rabbit, Ravi's fear of dogs, Meera's dread of contamination. This section begins by running the same machinery in reverse. Suppose the problem is not that a person fears something harmless but that he desires something harmful: the bottle, the cigarette, a sexual interest that hurts other people. Could conditioning make the attractive thing repellent? Behaviour therapists of the 1950s and 1960s were sure that it could, and the methods they built, aversion therapy and its gentler imaginal cousin covert sensitisation, are the first topics here. Some of what was done in their name is now a source of professional shame, and a clinical psychologist needs to know exactly why. The section then turns to a far happier use of an unpleasant stimulus, the bell-and-pad alarm for bedwetting, one of the oldest and most successful treatments in the whole of clinical psychology, and ends with eye movement desensitisation and reprocessing, a trauma therapy whose story is a lesson in how to ask what really makes a treatment work.

Aversion therapy: making the attractive unpleasant

aversion therapy is a treatment in which a stimulus or behaviour that the client finds attractive but wants to give up (alcohol, cigarettes, a deviant sexual stimulus) is repeatedly paired with an unpleasant stimulus, such as nausea or a painful electric shock, so that the attractive stimulus comes to evoke discomfort instead of desire. Section 4.2 described it as counterconditioning in the opposite direction, and that is exactly right. In desensitisation a stimulus that evokes fear is paired with calm; in aversion therapy a stimulus that evokes pleasure is paired with distress. In the language of classical conditioning, the sight, smell and taste of whisky are the conditioned stimulus, the nausea-producing drug is the unconditioned stimulus, and the hoped-for result is a conditioned aversion: the whisky itself comes to make the drinker feel sick. An operant process is usually mixed in, because reaching for the drink is followed by something unpleasant (which punishes it) and turning away is followed by relief (which negatively reinforces turning away). Even so, textbooks and examiners classify aversion therapy with the classical-conditioning techniques, beside desensitisation and flooding.

The oldest form is chemical aversion, in which the unpleasant stimulus is a drug that causes nausea and vomiting. From the mid-1930s the physician Walter L. Voegtlin and his colleague Frederick Lemere ran an aversion programme for alcoholism at the Shadel Sanatorium in Seattle. The patient sat in a quiet room lined with bottles of his favourite drinks and received an injection of emetine, an alkaloid from the ipecac root that causes nausea and vomiting. Just as the nausea began he was handed a drink and told to look at it, smell it, swirl it round his mouth and swallow it. He vomited within moments, and the sequence was repeated with other drinks. A course consisted of a handful of such sessions spread over about ten days, with occasional booster sessions afterwards. Voegtlin and Lemere reported high rates of abstinence among several thousand patients, but their patients were mostly employed, married and paying privately, the kind of people who do comparatively well in any treatment, and there was no control group. In Britain the drug of choice was apomorphine, which acts on the vomiting centre of the brain; it was used for drinking and, as you will see below, for sexual behaviour.

The second form is electrical aversion (also called faradic aversion, after the physicist Michael Faraday), in which a brief, painful but harmless electric shock is delivered, usually to the forearm or fingers, at the moment the client performs or imagines the unwanted behaviour. It had an early start: around 1930 the Soviet psychiatrist N. V. Kantorovich paired alcohol with shocks in a group of alcoholic patients. Shock had obvious practical advantages over drugs. Its timing could be controlled to a fraction of a second, so that it coincided exactly with the glass touching the lips or with the appearance of a slide of the fetish object; its strength could be set precisely; and it carried none of the medical risks of repeated vomiting. Through the 1960s, at the Maudsley and elsewhere, it was used for fetishism, exhibitionism and other unwanted sexual behaviour, and for drinking.

For drinking, however, shock turned out to work poorly, and the reason lies in the learning laboratory. In 1966 John Garcia and Robert Koelling showed that rats readily learn to avoid a flavour that is followed by illness but hardly learn to link the same flavour with an electric shock; conversely, they readily link lights and sounds with shock but not with illness. This is the preparedness of Section 4.1 at work: animals, people included, are built to connect tastes and smells with nausea. Studies of alcoholic patients bore the prediction out. Nausea-based aversion produced genuine conditioned aversions to the taste and smell of alcohol (patients grimaced, reported disgust and were reluctant to drink when offered alcohol weeks later), and the patients who developed the strongest aversions tended to drink least; electrical aversion produced nothing comparable. (Taste aversion learning itself is taught in the General Psychology book.)

Key study: Raymond's case of the handbags and prams (1956)

Question. Could aversion therapy remove a sexual attraction to objects? Method. The British psychiatrist M. J. Raymond treated a married man in his thirties who was sexually aroused by handbags and prams (perambulators) and had been in repeated trouble with the police for damaging them. Over about a week, at intervals of a few hours, he was given apomorphine injections and shown handbags, prams and pictures of them while the nausea built. Result. By the end the objects had come to sicken him, and he said he had lost interest in them. Raymond reported that more than a year later he had had no further trouble with the police and his marriage had improved. Conclusion. A conditioned aversion could, apparently, suppress a long-standing fetish. Criticism. It was a single uncontrolled case, the patient's motivation was bound up with the threat of prosecution, and the procedure (days of repeated vomiting and broken sleep) would not pass an ethics committee today.

One drug is often listed beside chemical aversion, and an examiner may offer it, but strictly it works differently. disulfiram (sold as Antabuse) blocks the liver enzyme aldehyde dehydrogenase, so that if the person drinks, acetaldehyde builds up and produces a violent disulfiram-ethanol reaction: flushing, throbbing headache, nausea, vomiting, a racing heart and a fall in blood pressure. Nothing is deliberately paired in therapy sessions. The patient takes the tablet each morning precisely so that he will not drink, and the drug deters drinking by the threat of the reaction, not by conditioning a dislike of alcohol. Its weakness is obvious: a person who wants to drink simply stops taking the tablet. That is why in Indian de-addiction practice it is commonly given under supervision, with a family member watching each dose. (Medication for alcohol dependence belongs to the Psychiatric Disorders book.)

Aversion therapy is rarely used today, for four reasons. The first is ethical: it deliberately causes pain or sickness, which is defensible only with fully informed and genuinely free consent and only when no gentler treatment would work (the wider ethics of aversive methods are weighed in Section 4.11). The second is that drop-out is high; many people simply stop coming. The third is the failure of generalisation. A man who has been shocked in the clinic while lifting a glass can learn, very precisely, that drinking is unpleasant when electrodes are strapped to his arm and a psychologist is watching, and that it is perfectly pleasant in the bar on Saturday night; and a conditioned aversion extinguishes once drinking is again followed by its usual pleasure instead of by sickness. The fourth is that better treatments arrived: covert sensitisation (below), cognitive behavioural treatment with relapse prevention, motivational interviewing (Chapter 6), contingency management (Section 4.5) and medication. Chemical aversion for alcohol has survived in a few private hospitals in the United States, but the method has largely passed into history. Its most troubling chapter is the subject of the next subsection.

Exam trap

Aversion therapy works by pairing an undesirable behaviour with an unpleasant stimulus to reduce its appeal: classical conditioning (counterconditioning). Each distractor describes something else. Rewarding the behaviour intermittently would strengthen it (a variable schedule, Section 4.5); ignoring it until it dies away is extinction (Section 4.6); restructuring the belief behind it is cognitive therapy (Chapter 5). In "which is NOT a treatment developed from classical conditioning?" lists, aversion therapy, flooding and systematic desensitisation are all classical, and the odd one out is the token economy, which is operant. Do not confuse aversion therapy with flooding, which exposes a person to a feared stimulus without pairing it with anything (Section 4.3), or with punishment, the operant procedure in which an aversive consequence follows a response (Section 4.6). Disulfiram is a deterrent drug, not a conditioning procedure.

A history the profession condemns: aversion therapy and homosexuality

From the 1950s to the early 1970s homosexuality was officially classified as a mental disorder: the first two editions of the American Diagnostic and Statistical Manual (1952 and 1968) listed it among the sexual deviations, and in many countries sex between men was a crime (in England and Wales until 1967, and in India until 2018). Behaviour therapists, confident that any learned behaviour could be unlearned, offered gay men "reorientation" by aversion. Many of the men came under pressure from courts, employers, families or their own religious guilt.

The pioneer was the Czech psychiatrist Kurt Freund (1914 to 1996), who in Prague in the 1950s gave gay men nausea-inducing injections while they looked at pictures of nude men, and later showed them pictures of women after injections of testosterone. To his great credit, Freund followed his patients up for years, and what he found changed his mind. Some had married or begun relationships with women, but hardly any had lost their attraction to men. He concluded that the treatment did not change sexual orientation, abandoned it, and spent the rest of his career, later in Toronto, studying sexual arousal scientifically and arguing for the decriminalisation of homosexuality. In England in the mid-1960s M. P. Feldman and M. J. MacCulloch used a refined electrical method they called anticipatory avoidance learning: a gay client watched slides of men and received a shock unless he switched the slide off within a few seconds, and the removal of a male slide was sometimes followed by a slide of a woman, so that women would be associated with relief. Claims of success rested mostly on changes in reported behaviour over short follow-ups, not on any lasting change in whom the men desired.

By the early 1970s the work was under attack from the gay rights movement and from inside the profession. In 1973 the American Psychiatric Association voted to remove homosexuality from the DSM, and the World Health Organization removed it from the International Classification of Diseases with ICD-10, approved in 1990. In his presidential address to the Association for Advancement of Behavior Therapy in 1974, published in 1976 as "Homosexuality: the ethical challenge," Gerald C. Davison asked his colleagues to stop offering change treatments altogether, because the very availability of a "cure" told gay clients, and society, that they were sick. In 2009 a task force of the American Psychological Association reviewed the research on sexual orientation change efforts and found no sound evidence that they work and real evidence that they can harm.

Put together, there are five reasons why this use of aversion therapy is now condemned, and an interviewer may well ask you for them. First, the premise was false: homosexuality is a normal variation of human sexuality, not a disorder, so there was nothing to treat. Second, it did not work: sexual orientation was not changed. Third, it harmed: men who received these treatments have described lasting shame, depression, anxiety, damaged relationships and suicidal thoughts. Fourth, consent was compromised, because agreement given under threat of prison, dismissal or family rejection is not free. Fifth, it confused the client's distress, much of it caused by stigma and persecution, with the client's sexuality, and so treated the victim instead of the problem. Every major professional body now rejects conversion or "reparative" therapy in any form, aversive, psychological or religious.

Indian context

On 6 September 2018, in Navtej Singh Johar v. Union of India, the Supreme Court read down Section 377 of the Indian Penal Code and decriminalised consensual sexual acts between adults of the same sex. The Indian Psychiatric Society had stated in 2014, and repeated in mid-2018, shortly before the judgment, that homosexuality is not a mental illness. In 2021 the Madras High Court, in S. Sushma v. Commissioner of Police, directed the authorities to act against practitioners of conversion therapy, and in 2022 the National Medical Commission declared conversion therapy to be professional misconduct. A client who asks you to change his or her sexual orientation therefore needs careful, respectful exploration of the distress behind the request (family pressure, marriage expectations, fear of discovery, religious conflict), never an attempt at "cure."

Covert sensitisation: aversion in the imagination

If the aversive event can be imagined, why deliver it at all? That was the insight of the American psychologist Joseph R. Cautela of Boston College, who first described the method in 1966 for compulsive behaviour and set it out in full in a 1967 paper entitled simply "Covert sensitization." covert sensitisation is a form of aversion therapy carried out entirely in the imagination: the client imagines performing, or starting to perform, the unwanted behaviour and then imagines a vivid, unpleasant consequence (nausea, vomiting, humiliation, arrest), so that the behaviour and the urge to perform it become associated with aversion. "Covert" means hidden, that is, in thought; "sensitisation" here means making the person more sensitive, more averse, to the tempting stimulus.

The procedure has three parts. The client is first taught to relax, so that he can attend to imagery without distraction. The therapist then narrates aversive scenes, slowly and in the present tense, in which each step towards the unwanted behaviour brings growing nausea or distress, ending in the full aversive event at the moment of the behaviour itself. Then come escape scenes, in which the client imagines turning away from the temptation and feeling immediate relief, so that the alternative response is negatively reinforced. The two kinds of scene are alternated, the client rehearses them at home, and, most important of all, he learns to call up the aversive image the moment an urge appears in real life, when the chain of behaviour is still at its first link.

Let us push one case through. Suresh, a 44-year-old clerk in a Mysuru bank, stops every evening at a bar near the bus stand "for one peg" and reaches home after six. He wants to stop; his wife has threatened to take the children to her parents. After relaxation the therapist begins: "You leave the bank and walk towards the bus stand. You see the green sign of the bar and decide to go in. As you push open the door you notice a queasy feeling in your stomach. You walk to the counter and the feeling grows; there is a sour taste in your mouth. The barman pours your peg, and as you lift the glass the nausea rises into your throat. As the glass touches your lips you vomit, into the glass, over your hands, over the counter. The smell of whisky and vomit is everywhere, and everyone is staring at you." After a pause comes the escape scene: "You put the glass down and turn away. As soon as you step out into the fresh air the sickness begins to fade. You walk to the bus stand feeling calm, clean and proud of yourself." Over several sessions the aversive point is moved earlier in the chain, so that the nausea begins at the very thought of turning towards the bar.

For unwanted sexual behaviour the aversive consequence chosen is usually social rather than physical, because shame and the fear of discovery are what such clients dread most. In a case reported in 1974 by David Barlow and his colleagues, a man who had sexually abused his daughter repeatedly imagined being discovered at the very moment of approaching her by his wife and the family priest. In assisted covert sensitisation, introduced by Barry M. Maletzky in the 1970s, a foul-smelling chemical such as valeric acid is held under the client's nose at the climax of the scene, adding a real aversive stimulus to the imagined one.

Covert sensitisation has several advantages over its physical ancestor. It involves no pain, no drugs and no medical risk; the client controls the scenes, which makes consent more genuine; it can be practised anywhere, without equipment; and because the client carries it with him, it is more likely to generalise to the bar and the street than a shock delivered in a clinic. The controlled evidence is modest. It seems to reduce urges and behaviour in motivated clients, especially as one part of a package, and today it is used mainly within broader cognitive behavioural programmes for alcohol problems and for paraphilic disorders, alongside relapse prevention (the disorders and their medication are in the Psychiatric Disorders book). Cautela went on to build a whole family of covert conditioning methods, in which an operant or classical procedure is carried out in imagination: covert reinforcement (imagining a pleasant scene immediately after imagining the desired behaviour), covert negative reinforcement, covert extinction and covert modelling (imagining another person performing the behaviour, a form of the modelling described in Section 4.7). Table 4.5 compares covert sensitisation with chemical and electrical aversion and with disulfiram.

Table 4.5 Aversion therapy and its relatives compared.
MethodUnpleasant eventHow it is appliedMain usesStatus today
Chemical aversion (emetine, apomorphine)Drug-induced nausea and vomitingDrink tasted as nausea beginsAlcohol useRare; a few private hospitals
Electrical (faradic) aversionBrief painful shockTimed to the act or to slides of the stimulusParaphilias; drinking (poor results)Largely abandoned
Covert sensitisation (Cautela, 1966 and 1967)Imagined nausea, shame or arrestNarrated scenes plus escape scenesParaphilias, drinking, overeating, smokingOccasional, within packages
Assisted covert sensitisation (Maletzky)Imagined scene plus a real foul smellOdour at the climax of the sceneParaphiliasOccasional
Disulfiram (Antabuse)Drug-alcohol reaction, only if he drinksDaily tablet as a deterrent; no pairingAlcohol dependenceWidely used, best supervised
Exam trap

"Imagining a scene in which an undesirable behaviour is paired with an unpleasant consequence, to reduce the behaviour" is covert sensitisation (Cautela), and the distractors are all exposure methods that work on fear, not desire. Flooding exposes the person to the most feared stimulus at full intensity; implosion exposes him to exaggerated imagined horrors so that fear extinguishes; systematic desensitisation pairs feared scenes with relaxation (Sections 4.2 and 4.3). Implosion and covert sensitisation are both imaginal and both unpleasant, so ask what the scene is for: implosion aims to remove a fear, covert sensitisation aims to create an aversion. And note the two meanings of "sensitisation": in exposure theory (Section 4.3) it is an unwanted increase in fear after brief, intense exposure; in Cautela's term it is the deliberate goal.

The bell and pad for bedwetting

Few problems cause a family more quiet misery than a child who still wets the bed long after the age when other children are dry. The child is ashamed and avoids sleepovers and school trips; the parents are tired of washing sheets and often angry; and in the first half of the twentieth century the psychoanalytic view was that the wetting was a symptom of hidden conflict, which made it the child's emotional problem and the parents' fault. nocturnal enuresis is the repeated wetting of the bed during sleep by a child old enough to be expected to stay dry (the diagnostic criteria belong to the Psychiatric Disorders book). Behaviour therapy offered a radically simpler account: the child has not yet learned to respond, while asleep, to the signals from a full bladder, so the task is to teach that response.

The idea of an alarm was not new. In 1904 the Austrian paediatrician Meinhard von Pfaundler fitted a device that rang a bell when a child wet the bed, so that the nurses would know when to change the sheets, and he noticed that some of the children stopped wetting. But it was Mowrer, whom you met in Section 4.1 as the author of the two-factor theory, who turned the observation into a treatment.

Key study: the Mowrers' bell and pad (1938)

Question. If bedwetting is a failure of learning, can conditioning teach a sleeping child to respond to a full bladder? Method. Orval Hobart Mowrer and Willie Mae Mowrer, working at the New Haven Children's Center in Connecticut, built a pad of two sheets of metal gauze separated by a layer of thick cotton fabric, placed under the child's sheet and wired to a battery and a bell. The first drops of urine soaked through the fabric, completed the circuit and rang the bell; the child woke, stopped urinating, got up to finish in the toilet, and the bed was changed and the alarm reset. Result. They reported, in a 1938 paper in the American Journal of Orthopsychiatry, that all thirty children they treated became dry, none needing more than about two months, and that removing the symptom produced no new problems in its place. Conclusion. Enuresis can be treated directly as a learning problem. Criticism. The series was uncontrolled and the follow-up short; later studies showed lower success rates and frequent relapse. Yet the method survived every later test and remains a first-line treatment nearly a century on.

The bell-and-pad method (also called the urine alarm or enuresis alarm) is a conditioning treatment for nocturnal enuresis in which a moisture-sensitive pad under the child, or today a small sensor clipped into the underwear, sets off an alarm (a bell, buzzer or vibration) at the first drops of urine, waking the child so that he learns to wake, or to hold on, in response to bladder signals. Notice that the Mowrers' result also answered the psychoanalysts directly. If wetting were the symptom of a hidden conflict, removing it should have produced another symptom in its place; it did not. This was one of the earliest pieces of evidence against the symptom substitution hypothesis that is weighed in Section 4.11.

Why does the alarm work? The Mowrers' own explanation was classical conditioning, and it is still the textbook answer (Figure 4.5). The sensations of a full bladder are, at first, a neutral stimulus for the sleeping child. The alarm is an unconditioned stimulus that produces an unconditioned response: waking and a reflex contraction of the sphincter that stops the flow. Because the bladder sensations come just before the alarm night after night, they become a conditioned stimulus, and eventually they produce the conditioned response, waking or tightening, on their own, before any urine escapes.

Two facts trouble this account. First, a classically conditioned response should extinguish once the unconditioned stimulus is withdrawn, so children should start wetting again as soon as the alarm is taken away; many do not. Second, most successfully treated children do not learn to wake when the bladder is full; they learn to sleep through the night dry. In 1964 the Australian psychologist S. H. Lovibond proposed an avoidance account instead. The alarm is unpleasant, and the child learns to avoid it by contracting the pelvic muscles and holding on in response to the bladder signals. Avoidance responses, as Solomon's dogs showed (Section 4.3), are remarkably resistant to extinction, which explains why dryness can outlast the alarm. Lovibond even designed a "twin-signal" alarm, intended to make the alarm more of an unpleasant event to be avoided and less of a mere wake-up call. On top of both accounts sits ordinary operant reinforcement: dry nights earn praise and a star on the chart. Most researchers today accept that more than one process is involved, and that the child's functional bladder capacity also increases during treatment.

How well does it work? Roughly two out of three children become dry during alarm treatment, which usually takes several weeks to a few months of nightly use, and the alarm consistently does better than no treatment, than waking the child at fixed times and than most other psychological methods. Its weakness is relapse: a substantial minority of children (figures between a quarter and a half are commonly reported) start wetting again within months of the alarm being removed, although most become dry again with a second course. Two refinements reduce relapse, and both come straight from learning theory. In overlearning, training is continued beyond the point at which the skill is first mastered. In 1972 G. C. Young and R. T. T. Morgan applied it to enuresis: once a child had achieved fourteen consecutive dry nights, he was asked to drink extra fluid in the hour before bed while the alarm stayed in place, until he was again dry for a run of nights. The extra load on the bladder strengthened the new response, and relapse rates fell. The second refinement is an intermittent alarm that sounds after only some wettings; learning is slower, but, exactly as the partial reinforcement effect predicts, the response is more resistant to extinction afterwards.

In 1974 Nathan H. Azrin, T. J. Sneed and Richard Foxx combined the alarm with several operant procedures in an intensive package called dry-bed training. On the first night the child is woken every hour and taken to the toilet; after any wetting he practises the correct response repeatedly (lying in bed, counting to fifty, getting up and going to the toilet), and he changes his own wet sheets and nightclothes; dry nights earn warm praise from the whole family. The repeated practice and the sheet-changing are forms of overcorrection (positive practice and restitution), a technique taught in full in Section 4.6. Azrin is a name you will meet again in a moment, as one of the inventors of the token economy.

Today the alarm and the drug desmopressin (a synthetic version of the hormone that reduces urine production at night) are the two first-line treatments for bedwetting. The drug works faster, which makes it useful for a school camp or a wedding in the family, but wetting usually returns when it is stopped; the alarm is slower and demands more of the family, but its gains are more durable. The alarm is usually offered to school-age children who are motivated to become dry and whose families can cope with being woken at night.

Exam trap

The bell-and-pad method is Mowrer and Mowrer (1938), for nocturnal enuresis, and the textbook principle is classical conditioning (bladder distension as the conditioned stimulus, the alarm as the unconditioned stimulus); if "avoidance learning" is offered in an explanation question, Lovibond's account is the alternative. Do not credit it to Pavlov, Wolpe or Skinner, and do not confuse O. Hobart Mowrer's alarm with his two-factor theory of avoidance (Section 4.1), although it is the same Mowrer. Overcorrection is part of Azrin's dry-bed training, but when "overcorrection is used to manage..." is asked, the keyed answer is repetitive aggressive or disruptive behaviour, as in autism or intellectual disability (Section 4.6); the standard behavioural treatment for bedwetting is the alarm.

Clinical link

Before the alarm, explain to the family that bedwetting is common, involuntary and nobody's fault, and stop all punishment, which only adds shame and makes the child hide wet sheets. In many Indian homes children share a bed or a room with siblings or grandparents, so the alarm will wake others; plan who will get up with the child, keep a dry set of sheets ready, and use a simple star chart for dry nights. Medical causes (urinary infection, constipation, diabetes, or new wetting after a long dry period) should be ruled out by a paediatrician first.

Eye movement desensitisation and reprocessing: the EMDR debate

In May 1987 Francine Shapiro (1948 to 2019), then a graduate student in psychology in California, was walking in a park in Los Gatos, turning over some disturbing thoughts, when she noticed that the thoughts had lost their sting. She also noticed that her eyes had been flicking rapidly from side to side. She began to move her eyes deliberately while holding upsetting memories in mind, tried the procedure with friends and volunteers, and developed it into a treatment for traumatic memories. She first called it eye movement desensitisation (EMD); around 1990 she added "reprocessing" to the name, to reflect her view that the method works by helping the brain to process the memory.

Key study: Shapiro's first controlled study (1989)

Question. Can a single session of eye movements reduce the distress of traumatic memories? Method. Shapiro treated 22 people, aged from childhood to middle age, with distressing memories of rape, sexual abuse or combat in Vietnam. Half received the eye movement procedure; the other half, a comparison group, described their memory in detail without eye movements (and later received the eye movements too). Distress was measured on a 0 to 10 subjective units of disturbance scale and belief in a positive self-statement on a 1 to 7 scale. Result. After one session, distress in the eye movement group fell sharply and stayed low at follow-up three months later; the comparison group changed little until it, too, received the eye movements. Conclusion. Shapiro, publishing in the Journal of Traumatic Stress in 1989, claimed a rapid new treatment for traumatic memories. Criticism. Shapiro was both the therapist and the person collecting the ratings, there were no standardised measures of post-traumatic symptoms, nobody was blind to the conditions, and the dramatic one-session results were not matched by later controlled trials. The study was a promising start, not a proof.

eye movement desensitisation and reprocessing (EMDR) is a structured treatment for traumatic memories in which the client holds a disturbing memory in mind (its worst image, the negative belief and emotion attached to it and the body sensation it produces) while following the therapist's fingers as they move rapidly back and forth across the visual field, in brief sets, until the distress falls and a positive belief about the self grows stronger. The side-to-side movements are an example of bilateral stimulation, Shapiro's term for any alternating left-right stimulation used during the sets; besides eye movements, therapists may use alternate taps on the client's hands or knees, or tones played alternately into each ear. Shapiro's theory, the adaptive information processing model, holds that a traumatic memory is stored "unprocessed," frozen with the images, emotions and sensations of the original event, and that bilateral stimulation, combined with the client's "dual attention" to the memory and to the present moment, unlocks the brain's natural capacity to process it into an ordinary memory. Shapiro set out the method in her book Eye Movement Desensitization and Reprocessing: Basic Principles, Protocols, and Procedures (1995), as a standard protocol of eight phases (Table 4.6).

Table 4.6 The eight phases of EMDR (Shapiro's standard protocol).
PhaseNameWhat happens
1History taking and treatment planningThe therapist takes a history, chooses target memories and judges whether the client can tolerate the distress the work may bring.
2PreparationThe method is explained and the client learns self-calming skills, such as imagining a "safe place," for use during and between sessions.
3AssessmentThe client selects the worst image of the target memory, the negative belief ("I am helpless"), a desired positive belief ("I survived; I am safe now"), the emotion and its body location. Baselines are taken: distress on a 0 to 10 SUD scale and the believability of the positive belief on the 1 to 7 validity of cognition (VoC) scale.
4DesensitisationThe longest phase. The client holds the memory while following sets of bilateral eye movements; after each set he lets go, breathes and reports what he notices. Sets continue until the SUD rating falls to 0 or 1.
5InstallationThe positive belief is linked to the memory with further sets until it feels fully true (VoC of 6 or 7).
6Body scanThe client scans the body for leftover tension, which is targeted with more sets.
7ClosureThe client is returned to calm before leaving and asked to keep a log of any disturbing thoughts, images or dreams between sessions.
8Re-evaluationAt the next session the therapist checks whether the gains have held and what still needs work.

EMDR spread with astonishing speed. Within little more than a decade tens of thousands of clinicians had paid for training through Shapiro's institute, and the procedure was being applied to phobias, panic, grief, performance anxiety and much else. The speed of its spread, and the boldness of its early claims, provoked one of the sharpest debates in modern clinical psychology. The debate has two parts, and it is important to keep them apart.

Does EMDR work? Broadly, yes. Many randomised controlled trials have found it better than no treatment, waiting lists and non-specific therapies for post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), and a meta-analysis of 38 trials by Jonathan Bisson and colleagues in 2007 found that EMDR and trauma-focused cognitive behavioural therapy were both effective, both did better than non-trauma-focused "other therapies," and both showed some advantage over stress management a few months after treatment. On that evidence the World Health Organization's 2013 guidelines recommend EMDR, alongside trauma-focused cognitive behavioural therapy, as a first-line psychological treatment for PTSD (Chapter 5 sets these trauma treatments side by side). Some reviews find exposure therapy slightly superior, and the American Psychological Association's 2017 guideline gave EMDR only a conditional recommendation, but EMDR is now an established trauma treatment. Its main use, then and now, is PTSD.

Do the eye movements matter? This is the real question, and it was answered by dismantling studies, which take a treatment package apart and compare the full package with the same package minus one component. If the eye movements were the active ingredient, EMDR with eye movements should clearly beat the identical procedure carried out with the eyes fixed on a stationary point, or with the hands simply resting. In study after study through the 1990s it did not. In 2001 Paul Davidson and Kevin Parker pooled 34 studies in a meta-analysis and found that EMDR was better than no treatment and than non-specific therapies, no better than other exposure-based treatments, and that adding the eye movements made no reliable difference to outcome.

If the eye movements add little, what does the work? Look again at Table 4.6. A client in EMDR repeatedly calls up the traumatic memory, with its images, emotions and sensations, while staying in the room instead of avoiding: that is imaginal exposure (Section 4.3), even though each exposure is brief. He identifies a negative belief and works towards a more realistic positive one: that is cognitive restructuring (Chapter 5). He learns a calming exercise, has a warm and confident therapist and a convincing rationale: those are the common factors of all psychotherapy (Chapter 8). Every one of these ingredients was known before 1987. Hence the famous verdict of the Harvard psychologist Richard J. McNally in 1999: what is effective in EMDR is not new, and what is new is not effective. McNally compared EMDR with the animal magnetism of Franz Anton Mesmer in the eighteenth century, which produced real cures through suggestion while crediting them to an invisible fluid. In 2000 James Herbert, Scott Lilienfeld and their colleagues went further, using EMDR as a case study of the warning signs of pseudoscience in clinical psychology: claims of success that ran ahead of the evidence, a theory too vague to be tested, training restricted and trademarked, and a habit of dismissing negative studies on the grounds that the protocol had not been followed faithfully.

The story has a final twist, which a fair-minded clinician should know. From the late 1990s laboratory researchers, among them Jackie Andrade and later Marcel van den Hout and his colleagues in the Netherlands, offered a more testable explanation of the eye movements: the working memory account. Holding a vivid memory in mind while doing a demanding task (tracking a moving finger, or even doing mental arithmetic) competes for the limited capacity of working memory, so the memory is experienced as less vivid and less emotional, and may be stored again in that weaker form. Laboratory studies support this, and a 2013 meta-analysis by Christopher Lee and Pim Cuijpers found that eye movements did add a small-to-moderate effect beyond the memory work alone (larger in laboratory studies than in clinical trials). The honest verdict today is therefore threefold. EMDR is an effective treatment for PTSD; most of its benefit comes from exposure to the memory and from cognitive processing, which it shares with other trauma therapies; and the eye movements may add a small effect, probably by taxing working memory, but nothing like the unique, rapid effect originally claimed. Some writers class EMDR as a behaviour therapy because its active ingredient is exposure; Shapiro saw it as an integrative therapy in its own right.

Exam trap

EMDR was developed by Francine Shapiro, who noticed the effect in 1987 and published her first study in 1989 (books give either year; both are right for different events). It was designed for, and is used primarily for, post-traumatic stress disorder, not phobia, OCD, eating disorders, borderline personality disorder, autism, dissociative disorders, schizophrenia or bipolar disorder. Its trademark is bilateral stimulation: the client focuses on the memory, emotions and thoughts of the trauma while making therapist-guided eye movements (or receiving alternating taps or tones). "Reliving the trauma in imagination" describes the imaginal exposure of Foa's prolonged exposure; administering an antidepressant, relaxation training alone, and hypnosis to recover repressed memories are not EMDR. The rival names in option lists: Edna Foa (prolonged exposure), Patricia Resick (cognitive processing therapy), Marsha Linehan (dialectical behaviour therapy), Aaron Beck (cognitive therapy), Bessel van der Kolk (a psychiatrist known for research on the body and trauma and for The Body Keeps the Score) and Pat Ogden (founder of sensorimotor psychotherapy). Do not confuse Francine Shapiro with Monte B. Shapiro of the Maudsley (Section 4.1). EMDR's SUD scale runs from 0 to 10; Wolpe's SUDS runs from 0 to 100.

Clinical link

The EMDR debate is the kind of question an interview panel likes, because it tests whether you can separate "this treatment helps" from "this treatment helps for the reason its founder gives." A good answer acknowledges both: EMDR is a guideline-recommended treatment for PTSD that many clients find tolerable because each exposure is brief, and its distinctive ingredient has turned out to contribute much less than its name suggests. The general lesson, which applies equally to Wolpe's relaxation in Section 4.2, is that dismantling studies are how a field finds out what its treatments are really doing.

4.5 Operant Techniques That Build BehaviourHigh yield · 19 Qs

Every technique in Sections 4.2 to 4.4 works by changing what a stimulus means: a dog that once signalled danger comes to signal safety, a glass of whisky that once signalled pleasure comes to signal nausea. But much of what clinicians are asked to change is not an emotion at all. It is an action, or more often the absence of one: the long-stay patient who has not dressed himself in years, the child with autism who does not speak, the adolescent who will not open a schoolbook, the cocaine user who cannot stay clean for a week. For problems like these behaviour therapists turned to the operant conditioning of B. F. Skinner, in which behaviour is changed by its consequences. The laws themselves (Thorndike's law of effect, the Skinner box, the schedules of reinforcement) are taught in the General Psychology book. This section is about what clinicians built from them: the techniques that create and strengthen behaviour. Section 4.6 covers their mirror image, the operant techniques that reduce behaviour, and applied behaviour analysis for autism. Remember, too, that every operant programme begins with the functional analysis of Section 4.1 (taught in full in Chapter 2): you cannot change the consequences of a behaviour until you know what they are.

Reinforcement: the engine of operant therapy

reinforcement is any consequence that increases the future probability of the behaviour it follows, and a reinforcer is the stimulus or event that has this effect. The definition is functional: a consequence counts as a reinforcer because of what it does, not because of what anyone intends. A teacher who scolds a boy for clowning in class, and finds that the clowning increases, has been reinforcing it, however angry she felt; a mother who praises her daughter's drawings, and finds that the girl draws less, has not. This is why a behaviour therapist watches what actually happens before deciding what counts as a reward.

There are two ways a consequence can strengthen behaviour. In positive reinforcement a stimulus is added after the behaviour, and the behaviour increases: a child is praised for finishing her homework and does homework more often. In negative reinforcement a stimulus, usually an unpleasant one, is removed, reduced or avoided after the behaviour, and the behaviour increases: you take a paracetamol, the headache goes, and next time you reach for the tablet sooner. "Positive" and "negative" here mean plus and minus, adding and subtracting; they do not mean good and bad. Negative reinforcement is everywhere in clinical work, and usually it is maintaining the problem: the washing that removes obsessional anxiety (Section 4.3), the escape that relieves phobic fear (Figure 4.2), Arjun's tantrums that remove the homework (Table 4.2), and the exhausted parent who gives in to a screaming child and is herself negatively reinforced by the silence that follows. Both kinds of reinforcement strengthen behaviour. The procedures that weaken it are punishment and extinction, and the four possible consequences fit the simple grid of Table 4.7.

Table 4.7 The four kinds of consequence. Reinforcement always increases behaviour and punishment always decreases it; "positive" means a stimulus is added and "negative" means one is removed.
What happens after the behaviourBehaviour increases: reinforcementBehaviour decreases: punishment (Section 4.6)
A stimulus is addedPositive reinforcement: praise after homework is finishedPositive punishment: a reprimand after hitting a sibling
A stimulus is removedNegative reinforcement: the nagging stops once the room is tidiedNegative punishment: loss of phone time (response cost) or of time with others (time-out) after hitting

Reinforcers also differ in where their power comes from. A primary reinforcer (unconditioned reinforcer) works without any learning, because it satisfies a biological need: food, water, warmth, sleep, sexual contact, relief from pain. A secondary reinforcer (conditioned reinforcer) is a stimulus that was once neutral and has acquired its power by being paired with primary or other established reinforcers: a smile, the word "good," a grade, a coin. A generalised reinforcer (generalised conditioned reinforcer) is a secondary reinforcer that has been paired with many different reinforcers, so that it works whatever the person happens to want at the moment and rarely loses its value through satiation. Money is the classic example; so are attention, approval and the tokens of a token economy. Skinner pointed out that attention is one of the most powerful generalised reinforcers of all, because almost every other reinforcer a young child receives comes through an adult's attention, and that is exactly why attention so often maintains problem behaviour.

In practice therapists draw on six kinds of reinforcer: edible (a biscuit, a piece of fruit), tangible (stickers, small toys, a new pencil), activity (playing cricket, watching cartoons, the Premack principle below), social (praise, a smile, a pat on the back), token (points, stars, coupons exchangeable for other reinforcers) and sensory (music, a spinning light, which can matter a great deal to some children with autism). Social reinforcers are the goal, because they are free, natural and available everywhere. Edible and tangible ones are used when social reinforcement does not yet work, and they are always delivered together with praise, so that the praise gradually acquires value of its own and the tangible reward can later be withdrawn.

What reinforces one person may bore another, so reinforcers have to be found, not assumed. The therapist can ask (reinforcer surveys and questionnaires list activities and items for the client or parent to rate), observe (what does the person do when free to choose?), and test: in a preference assessment, used widely with children who cannot say what they like, items are offered in pairs or in arrays and the choices recorded, so that the most preferred can be used as reinforcers. Teodoro Ayllon and Nathan Azrin added a useful rule, reinforcer sampling: let the person try a new reinforcer once without having to earn it, because nobody will work for something he has never tasted.

Seven rules decide whether reinforcement works.

  1. Immediacy. Deliver the reinforcer within seconds of the behaviour; delay weakens it, especially for young children and people with intellectual disability, who may not connect a reward at bedtime with something done after school.
  2. Contingency. Give it only after the target behaviour. A reinforcer that arrives regardless of behaviour strengthens nothing in particular.
  3. Size. Make it large enough to be worth the effort but small enough that the person does not quickly have enough of it.
  4. Deprivation and satiation. A reinforcer is most effective when the person has gone without it for a while (sweets lose much of their power straight after lunch). Behaviour analysts call such changes in a reinforcer's value motivating operations.
  5. Specificity. Name the behaviour being reinforced ("You put all your blocks back in the box, well done!"), which is called labelled or descriptive praise.
  6. Consistency. Every adult in the setting follows the same rules; a behaviour reinforced by the father and ignored by the mother is being reinforced intermittently, which, as the next rule explains, makes it stronger.
  7. Schedule. Reinforce continuously at first, then intermittently.

That last rule needs a word of explanation, though the details belong to the General Psychology book. Continuous reinforcement, reinforcing every correct response, produces the fastest learning, but behaviour learned that way also extinguishes fastest once reinforcement stops. Intermittent (partial) reinforcement, on one of the four basic schedules (fixed ratio, variable ratio, fixed interval and variable interval), makes behaviour far more durable; this is the partial reinforcement effect. The variable-ratio schedule, on which a reinforcer arrives after an unpredictable number of responses, produces the highest, steadiest rates of responding and the greatest resistance to extinction, which is why slot machines and lottery tickets are so hard to give up. The clinical rule follows directly: reinforce every occurrence while a behaviour is being learned, then gradually make reinforcement less frequent and less predictable, a process called schedule thinning, so that the behaviour survives once nobody is counting.

Exam trap

Negative reinforcement is not punishment. Both kinds of reinforcement increase behaviour; negative reinforcement does it by removing something unpleasant. Time-out and response cost remove something pleasant in order to decrease behaviour, so they are negative punishment (Section 4.6); a reprimand is positive punishment. Among the schedules, variable ratio gives the highest rate and the greatest resistance to extinction, while continuous reinforcement gives the least, so an option claiming that continuous reinforcement is most resistant to extinction is false. Food is a primary reinforcer; praise, money and tokens are secondary, and money and tokens are generalised reinforcers. And when a question asks which is NOT part of parent management training (Section 4.10), "negative reinforcement" is the odd one out: parents are taught to attend to and reward good behaviour and ignore minor misbehaviour, and the unwitting negative reinforcement of tantrums (giving in to end the screaming) is what the training sets out to stop.

Shaping: building behaviour that does not yet exist

Reinforcement can only strengthen a behaviour that occurs. What if it never does? A man who has not spoken for nineteen years cannot be reinforced for speaking. The answer is shaping: the reinforcement of successive approximations to a target behaviour, that is, of responses that resemble the target more and more closely, until the target itself appears. Shaping combines two processes you already know. Each approximation is reinforced until it occurs reliably; then reinforcement is withheld from it (extinction) and given only for a slightly closer approximation. Because behaviour always varies a little from one occasion to the next, some responses will be closer to the target than the current standard, and those are the ones that are reinforced. Reinforcing some forms of a response and not others is called differential reinforcement, and shaping is differential reinforcement with a moving standard. Skinner used shaping to teach pigeons to turn in circles and even to play a version of ping-pong (General Psychology book). Clinicians used it on problems that had defeated every other approach.

Key study: Isaacs, Thomas and Goldiamond's chewing gum (1960)

Question. Can shaping restore speech in a patient who has been mute for many years? Method. Wayne Isaacs, James Thomas and Israel Goldiamond worked with a 40-year-old man in an American state hospital who had a diagnosis of catatonic schizophrenia and had not spoken a word for nineteen years. During a group session a packet of chewing gum fell from the experimenter's pocket and the man's eyes moved towards it, the first sign of interest in anything that anyone had seen. In individual sessions the experimenter held up a stick of gum and gave it to him whenever his eyes moved towards it. Once that was reliable, gum was given only when he moved his lips; then only when he made a sound; then only when the sound came closer and closer to "gum." Result. In the sixth week the man suddenly said, "Gum, please," and soon he was answering simple questions about his name and age. Conclusion. A behaviour absent for nineteen years could be rebuilt from tiny approximations, reinforced one step at a time. Criticism. At first the man spoke only to the experimenter and only in the treatment room; his speech spread to the ward only when the nursing staff, too, began to require him to ask for what he wanted. That lesson about generalisation is taken up at the end of this section.

Four years later Montrose Wolf, Todd Risley and Hayden Mees used shaping with Dicky, a three-year-old boy with autism who had had operations for cataracts and needed to wear glasses to save his sight, but threw every pair across the room. With small bites of food as reinforcers, the therapists reinforced him first for picking up empty glasses frames, then for carrying them, then for bringing them towards his face, then for putting them on, and then for wearing glasses with lenses for longer and longer periods, until he was wearing them for most of his waking day. The same 1964 study used time-out for Dicky's tantrums (Section 4.6), and it is one of the founding papers of applied behaviour analysis for autism.

Shaping follows a sequence of steps that is the same whether the learner is a pigeon, a child or a patient on a ward.

  1. Define the target behaviour precisely.
  2. Choose a starting behaviour that the person already performs at least occasionally and that resembles the target in some way.
  3. Plan the steps between them: small enough that each can be achieved, large enough that progress is made.
  4. Reinforce the current approximation immediately, every time, until it occurs reliably.
  5. Raise the standard: reinforce only the next, closer approximation, and stop reinforcing the old one.
  6. If the behaviour breaks down, drop back to the previous step, then move forward again in smaller steps. Moving too fast loses the behaviour; moving too slowly lets it settle at an intermediate step.
  7. When the target is reached, reinforce it consistently, then thin the schedule.

Shaping can work on any measurable dimension of a behaviour: its form (from a grunt to a word), its frequency, its duration (from two minutes at a task to thirty), its latency (how quickly a child starts work after an instruction), its speed, and its force or loudness. And because it needs no words, it works with learners who have no language at all: infants, people with profound intellectual disability and, of course, animals, which is where it was discovered. Consider Kavya, an eight-year-old in a Mysuru primary school who answers the teacher only in a whisper that nobody can hear. The school psychologist and the teacher agree a plan. In the first week Kavya earns praise and a sticker whenever she answers loudly enough to be heard by the girl sitting beside her; in the second week, by the girl two seats away; in the third, by the front row; in the fourth, by the whole class. When a cold drags her voice down in the fifth week, the teacher goes back a step for a few days instead of withdrawing the stickers. By the end of term Kavya answers so that everyone can hear, and the stickers have been replaced by the ordinary praise every child gets.

Exam trap

Shaping is reinforcing successive approximations of a target behaviour, so that simple behaviours are reinforced in sequence until a more complex one is learned. Its look-alikes: chaining links several steps into a sequence; prompting adds a cue before the response; fading gradually removes that cue. Other distractors describe different things: "a pigeon in a Skinner box trained to press a key for food" is plain operant conditioning; "any behaviour with pleasant consequences is likely to be repeated" is Thorndike's law of effect; "removing a reinforcer to weaken behaviour" is extinction or negative punishment; "pairing two neutral stimuli" is a classical procedure; "children are taught the difference between right and wrong" describes moral training, not a conditioning procedure; "punishing every incorrect response" is not shaping at all, because shaping simply withholds reinforcement from responses that do not meet the current standard. One real item (RML 2024) asked on which of "speed, power, non-verbal, performance" shaping is based and keyed "speed, power and non-verbal." The item is poorly worded (those four words are the names of kinds of psychological test, taught in the Assessment book), and the only sensible reading of the key is the point made above: shaping can change the speed and force of a response and needs no verbal instruction. Know the real principle, successive approximation by differential reinforcement, and recognise the key if it appears again.

Chaining: linking steps into a skill

Many of the skills that matter most for independent living are not single responses but sequences: washing your hands, dressing, brushing your teeth, making a cup of tea, catching the right bus. A behaviour chain is a sequence of responses in which each response produces the conditions for the next, and the whole sequence ends in a reinforcer. In a chain every step does two jobs at once. Completing it is a conditioned reinforcer for that step, and the situation it creates is the discriminative stimulus, the cue, for the next step. Turning on the tap is reinforced by the sight of running water, and running water is the cue to put your hands under it. chaining is the procedure of teaching such a sequence by reinforcing its steps in a planned order until the learner performs the whole chain.

Chaining begins with a task analysis: breaking a skill into small, teachable steps, each described as an observable action, usually worked out by watching competent people perform it or by doing it oneself. For hand-washing an able learner might need five steps (turn on the tap, wet the hands, rub on soap, scrub and rinse, dry on the towel); a learner who needs smaller steps might need fifteen. Performance is recorded step by step, so that you know exactly where the chain breaks. There are then three ways of teaching it, shown side by side in Figure 4.6.

In forward chaining the first step is taught first. When the learner can turn on the tap reliably, he is taught to turn on the tap and wet his hands, and so on, with the trainer completing or guiding him through the remaining steps each time; reinforcement follows the newly mastered portion. In backward chaining the last step is taught first. The trainer completes every step except the final one, the learner completes that one and is reinforced at once; then the learner does the last two steps, then the last three, until he performs the whole chain. Its great advantage is that every trial ends with the learner finishing the task himself and meeting the natural reinforcer at the end of the chain (clean hands, being dressed, the cup of tea), and each newly taught step leads straight into steps he has already mastered. Dressing is the classic case: the parent pulls the child's trousers up to the hips and the child pulls them up the last few centimetres; then from the knees; then from the ankles; and finally the child puts his feet in as well. In total task presentation (total task chaining) the learner attempts every step on every trial, with prompts on whichever steps need them, and the prompts are faded one step at a time. It suits learners who can already do some of the steps and chains that are not too long. Studies comparing the three have found all of them effective, without a consistent winner, so the choice depends on the learner and the task.

Let us push one case through. Rohit, a ten-year-old with moderate intellectual disability at a special school in Bengaluru, cannot wash his hands before the midday meal without an adult doing it for him. His teacher records his baseline on the five-step task analysis: he does none of the steps without help. Because the midday meal itself is a powerful reinforcer, she chooses backward chaining. For the first week she guides him through the first four steps and lets him dry his hands on the towel himself, which he can nearly do already; she praises him and he goes straight to lunch. Once he dries his hands reliably, he also scrubs and rinses, then rubs on the soap, and so on. Within six weeks he washes his hands alone when the lunch bell rings, and the teacher's help has been faded to a single reminder at the door, then to nothing.

Shaping and chaining are easily confused, so keep the difference clear. Shaping changes one response gradually until it takes on a new form; chaining links several responses, each of which the learner can already make or can be prompted to make, into a sequence. In practice the two are often combined: a step of a chain that the learner cannot yet perform at all may itself have to be shaped. Chains matter for reducing behaviour too. A binge, a cigarette or a drinking session is usually the last link of a long chain (leaving work, walking past the shop, buying, unwrapping), and a chain is easiest to break at its earliest links, which is why covert sensitisation moves the aversive image to the start of the chain and why the self-management programmes of Section 4.9 target the first link.

Exam trap

In backward chaining the learner is taught the last step first, while the trainer does all the earlier ones; it does not mean performing the task in reverse order. Forward chaining teaches the first step first, and total task presentation has the learner attempt every step every time. A task analysis is the breakdown of the skill into steps that comes before any of them. And if a stem describes reinforcing closer and closer approximations to a single response, the answer is shaping, not chaining; chaining is about linking a sequence of steps.

Prompting and fading

Shaping waits for a closer approximation to occur by chance, which can take a long time. With many learners it is faster to help the right response happen and then withdraw the help. A prompt is an extra antecedent stimulus, given before or during a response, that makes the correct response more likely; it is temporary help, meant to be removed once the behaviour is controlled by the natural cue. Prompts come in two families. Response prompts act on the learner's behaviour: verbal prompts (an instruction or a hint, "Now the soap"), gestural prompts (pointing at the soap), modelling prompts (the teacher demonstrates rubbing her own hands) and physical prompts (full hand-over-hand guidance, or a light touch on the elbow). Stimulus prompts change the materials: a within-stimulus prompt exaggerates the correct choice itself (the correct picture card is placed nearer or printed larger), and an extra-stimulus prompt adds a separate cue (a red dot inside the right shoe).

fading is the gradual removal of prompts, so that the behaviour comes to occur in response to the natural cue alone: the instruction "Wash your hands," the sound of the lunch bell, the question "What is your name?" Prompts can be faded in two directions. In most-to-least prompting the therapist starts with the most intrusive prompt, full physical guidance, and steps down through partial guidance and gestures to nothing; the learner makes few errors, which suits new and difficult skills. In least-to-most prompting (the system of least prompts) the therapist waits for the learner to respond, gives the least intrusive prompt only if he does not, and moves to more intrusive prompts only as needed; the learner is more independent but makes more mistakes. In time-delay prompting the cue and the prompt are given together at first, and then the therapist waits a little longer each time (two seconds, then four) before prompting, giving the learner the chance to answer before help arrives. In graduated guidance physical help is faded moment by moment, the therapist's hand moving from the child's hand to the wrist, the elbow and the shoulder, and finally just shadowing the movement.

Fading the prompt for an answer shows how smoothly this works. A therapist teaching a child with autism to answer "What is your name?" first says the whole answer for him to echo ("Ravi"); then only "Ra..."; then only the shape of the first sound on her lips; then nothing. The danger at every stage is prompt dependence: a learner who waits for the therapist's nod before each step has learned to respond to the nod, not to the task. The remedy is to plan the fading from the start, fade as quickly as performance allows, and reinforce unprompted correct responses more richly than prompted ones.

Prompting and fading lie behind one of the most elegant findings in learning research. In 1963 Herbert S. Terrace taught pigeons to peck a red key and not a green one almost without mistakes, by introducing the green key at first so briefly and so dimly that the birds hardly responded to it, and then fading it in to full strength. Pigeons trained the usual way made many errors and showed signs of emotional upset when they were not reinforced. errorless learning, arranging teaching so that the learner makes few or no errors, is now standard with learners who become frustrated by failure or who tend to repeat their own errors: children with autism or intellectual disability, and adults with memory impairment after brain injury (Chapter 9). Table 4.8 sets the confusable procedures of this section side by side.

Table 4.8 Five operant procedures that are easily confused.
ProcedureWhat changesWhat it is forExample
ShapingThe standard a response must meet to be reinforcedCreating a response that does not yet occurWhisper, then soft voice, then a voice the whole class can hear
ChainingThe number of linked steps the learner performsTeaching a sequence of responsesBackward chaining of hand-washing
PromptingExtra help added before or during the responseGetting the correct response to occurPointing to the soap
FadingThe prompt, gradually removedTransferring control to the natural cue"Ravi," then "Ra...," then nothing
