Exam trap

A prompt comes before or during the response and helps it happen; a reinforcer comes after it and strengthens it. Fading removes prompts gradually; it is not extinction, which removes reinforcement, and not schedule thinning, which reduces how often reinforcement is given. Modelling can be used as a prompt within operant training, but modelling as a therapy in its own right (live, symbolic and participant modelling) is Bandura's and is described in Section 4.7.

The Premack principle: using preferred activities as reinforcers

Aarav, ten years old, rarely does his homework, but if he is allowed he will play video games for two hours every evening. What should his parents use to reinforce homework? Sweets, money and praise are all possible, but the most powerful reinforcer is already in front of them: the video games themselves. The idea that an activity can reinforce another activity was set out in 1959 by the American psychologist David Premack (1925 to 2015), who argued that reinforcers are best thought of not as things but as behaviours (eating, not food; playing, not the toy), and that their power is relative. The Premack principle states that a more probable behaviour will reinforce a less probable behaviour when access to the more probable one is made contingent on performing the less probable one. The probability of a behaviour is judged by how much time a person spends on it when free to choose. Put simply, what a person likes to do can be used to reward what he is reluctant to do, but not the other way round.

Premack's best-known demonstration used first-grade children. He gave them free access to two activities, a pinball machine and a dispenser of chocolate sweets, and recorded which each child spent more time on: some were "manipulators," who preferred the pinball, and others "eaters." Then he arranged contingencies. When children had to eat sweets in order to be allowed to play pinball, the manipulators ate far more sweets than before; when children had to play pinball in order to get sweets, the eaters played more pinball. In each case the preferred activity reinforced the less preferred one, whichever it happened to be for that child. The principle had long been known to parents as "Grandma's rule" ("First finish your vegetables, then you can have the ice cream"). An early classroom use was reported in 1963 by Lloyd Homme and his colleagues, who used the favourite activities of a group of three-year-olds in a nursery school (running round the room, screaming, pushing chairs) to reinforce a much less popular one (sitting quietly and looking at the blackboard). After a few minutes of quiet attention a bell rang and the children were told to run and scream; soon they sat quietly when asked.

Later research refined the principle. In 1974 William Timberlake and James Allison showed that even a less preferred activity can act as a reinforcer if a person has been restricted to less of it than he would normally choose; what matters is deprivation relative to the person's usual level, an idea called the response deprivation hypothesis. For clinical work Premack's simple version is the one to remember, along with four rules for using it. First, measure what the person actually does often, instead of assuming: a ward patient's most probable behaviour may be sitting in one particular chair, and Ayllon and Azrin used exactly such observations to choose the privileges in their token economy. Second, the low-probability task always comes first and the preferred activity after it, every time. Third, the preferred activity must not be freely available at other times, or it reinforces nothing. Fourth, start with a small amount of the reluctant behaviour and increase it gradually, which is shaping again. For Aarav, twenty minutes of homework earns twenty minutes of gaming, and over the following weeks the homework period lengthens. The same logic runs through activity scheduling for depression (Section 4.9) and through self-management: the student who allows himself to open Instagram only after an hour of revision is using the Premack principle on himself.

Exam trap

The Premack principle says that a high-probability (preferred, frequent) behaviour can reinforce a low-probability one: video games after homework, not homework after video games. The reversed option ("a low-probability behaviour can reinforce a high-probability one") is the standard bait, and the principle is about reinforcement, not punishment or extinction. Among the remedies for a child who rarely does homework, only "video games allowed after homework is done" is Premack; verbal praise is a social reinforcer and a payment per assignment is a tangible one, and neither uses a preferred activity, while pairing homework with drinking water uses nothing preferred at all. In "the Premack principle explains..." questions, the answer is a technique of behaviour modification, used especially with children, not interpersonal relationships, language development or the impact of parenting. The language distractor has a reason: the same David Premack also taught the chimpanzee Sarah to use plastic tokens as words, and in 1978, with Guy Woodruff, asked whether the chimpanzee has a "theory of mind," coining the term (Developmental Psychology book). Other stray options in these questions ("continuous reinforcement is most resistant to extinction," "punishment must follow immediately," "two unconditioned stimuli cannot be paired") are not the Premack principle, and the first is simply false.

Token economies

Imagine a ward of the kind that filled the large state hospitals of the 1950s. Most of the women on it have been there for many years, most with a diagnosis of schizophrenia. They sit for hours doing nothing, many do not wash or dress unless nurses do it for them, and the staff have long since stopped expecting change. This was the setting for the most famous experiment in the history of operant therapy.

Teodoro Ayllon had already shown, with Jack Michael at a hospital in Saskatchewan, Canada, in a 1959 paper called "The psychiatric nurse as a behavioral engineer," that nurses who changed their responses to patients could change the patients' behaviour. From 1961, at Anna State Hospital in Illinois, Ayllon and Nathan Azrin turned a whole ward into an economy. A token economy is a programme in which clients earn tokens, which are generalised conditioned reinforcers, for clearly specified target behaviours, and exchange them for backup reinforcers, the goods, activities and privileges that give the tokens their value. On the Anna ward the women earned tokens for self-care (bathing at the set time, brushing their teeth, making their beds, combing their hair, dressing properly) and more tokens, the number depending on the job, for work in the kitchen or laundry, cleaning the ward, running errands or taking other patients for walks. They could spend them on sweets, cigarettes, lipstick, gloves and newspapers, a film on the ward, a walk in the grounds, a better room, or more privacy. The most expensive privileges, at 100 tokens, were an escorted trip into town and a private meeting with the social worker; a private meeting with the psychologist cost only 20. The backup reinforcers were chosen partly by Premack's rule: Ayllon and Azrin watched what the patients did often when left alone (sitting in a particular chair, staying in their rooms) and made those things available for tokens. They described the programme in their 1968 book The Token Economy: A Motivational System for Therapy and Rehabilitation.

Key study: Ayllon and Azrin's reversal experiment (1965)

Question. When patients on the token ward worked, was it really because of the tokens, or because they liked the jobs, the attention or the new atmosphere? Method. Ayllon and Azrin used a reversal design (an ABA design): measure the behaviour under the programme (A), withdraw the active ingredient (B), then restore it (A again). After a period in which tokens were earned by work, the patients went on receiving the same number of tokens, but now free each day, whether they worked or not. Then tokens were again made contingent on work. In another experiment patients who had chosen a favourite job were told that it would no longer pay tokens while a less popular job would. Result. When tokens no longer depended on work, the total hours worked by the ward collapsed within days to almost nothing; when the contingency returned, so did the work (Figure 4.7). When the favourite job stopped paying, most patients switched to the job that paid. Conclusion. The behaviour was controlled by the contingency between work and tokens, not by the tokens themselves, the staff's attention or liking for the job. Criticism. The target behaviours were largely ward chores that also suited the institution, which raised the question of whose interests the programme served; and the study showed control of behaviour on the ward, not recovery that would last after discharge.

Every token economy, on a ward, in a classroom or in a home, has the same working parts, and a programme that skimps on any of them tends to fail.

  1. Target behaviours, defined so precisely that any staff member would agree whether they occurred ("bed made with the sheet tucked in by 8 a.m.," not "takes responsibility").
  2. Tokens that are cheap, portable, hard to forge and quick to give: plastic chips, stamps on a card, points on a chart, stars. They are delivered immediately after the behaviour, always with praise, so that praise gradually becomes reinforcing in its own right.
  3. Backup reinforcers that are varied and genuinely wanted, chosen by asking, by observation and by reinforcer sampling, so that the tokens stay valuable whatever a person wants that day.
  4. Exchange rules: how many tokens each behaviour earns, what each backup reinforcer costs, and when and where the "shop" opens.
  5. Fines for specified misbehaviour in some programmes, a form of response cost (Section 4.6).
  6. Records of tokens earned and spent, so that the programme can be evaluated and adjusted.
  7. Staff training and consistency, because a token given by one nurse and not by another teaches nothing but confusion.
  8. A plan for fading: tokens are exchanged less often, a "levels" system moves clients up to privileges that need fewer tokens, and natural reinforcers (praise, wages, freedom, friends) gradually take over.

Table 4.9 shows what such a programme might look like in a rehabilitation day centre attached to an Indian psychiatric hospital.

Table 4.9 A worked example: an illustrative token programme in a psychiatric rehabilitation day centre.
Target behaviourTokens earnedBackup reinforcerPrice in tokens
Arriving by 9.30 a.m.2Extra cup of tea and biscuits3
Bathed, hair combed, clean clothes2Choosing the television channel for an hour5
Each hour in the vocational unit (tailoring, candle making)3Five-minute telephone call home5
Helping to serve lunch2New soap or hair oil8
Speaking up once in the group session2Escorted outing to the market25

The token economy's most rigorous test came a decade after Anna State. Gordon Paul, whose study of public-speaking anxiety you met in Section 4.2, and Robert J. Lentz spent more than four years at a mental health centre in Illinois comparing three regimes for severely disabled long-stay patients, most with chronic schizophrenia: a social learning programme built on a token economy together with skills training and a full timetable of activities; milieu therapy, a therapeutic community in the tradition of Maxwell Jones (Chapter 7), in which the ward is organised around group responsibility, open communication and the expectation that patients will behave as responsible members of a community; and routine hospital care. In their 1977 book, Psychosocial Treatment of Chronic Mental Patients: Milieu versus Social-Learning Programs, they reported that the social learning programme was the clear winner: its patients gained most in self-care, social and vocational skills, showed the largest reductions in bizarre behaviour, and far more of them were discharged and stayed in the community. Milieu therapy came second and routine care a poor third. (The study's place in the rehabilitation of severe mental illness is discussed in Chapter 9.)

Token economies spread rapidly in the 1960s and 1970s, to schools and classrooms (Section 4.10), to programmes for children with autism and intellectual disability, to prisons and to homes for young offenders. At Achievement Place in Kansas, opened in 1967, Montrose Wolf and his colleagues Elery and Elaine Phillips ran a family-style home for boys in trouble with the law in which points earned for schoolwork, chores and social skills bought privileges; this "teaching-family" model was later copied widely. Three problems then slowed the movement down. The first was generalisation: behaviour maintained by tokens tends to fade once the person leaves the setting in which tokens exist, unless the programme deliberately hands control over to natural reinforcers. The second was staff: a token economy is only as good as the consistency of the people running it, and consistency is hard to sustain on understaffed wards. The third was ethics and law. In Wyatt v. Stickney (1971 to 1972) a United States federal court in Alabama ruled that psychiatric patients have a right to basic amenities, including adequate meals, a bed, privacy, clothing and access to recreation, so these could no longer be withheld and sold back for tokens; backup reinforcers had to be genuine extras. Add the closure of the long-stay wards with deinstitutionalisation, and the large hospital token economy became rare. Its principles survive everywhere: in classroom point systems, star charts at home and the incentive schemes of rehabilitation units.

Exam trap

The token economy was developed as a therapeutic technique by Teodoro Ayllon and Nathan Azrin (Anna State Hospital, from 1961; book in 1968) and rests on operant conditioning, using tokens as secondary (generalised) reinforcers. It is the standard answer to "which is NOT a treatment developed from classical conditioning?" (flooding, aversion therapy and desensitisation are classical). One real key (Amity 2023) gives B. F. Skinner as the "proponent of the token economy as a psychosocial therapeutic technique," with Ayllon and Azrin also offered. Skinner supplied the principles, but the people who developed and named the technique were Ayllon and Azrin, so choose them; pick Skinner only if Ayllon and Azrin are not among the options. Do not confuse the token economy with Maxwell Jones's therapeutic community (Chapter 7), which was the milieu condition that the social learning programme beat in Paul and Lentz's comparison (1977).

Contingency management and contingency contracting

A contingency is simply an if-then relation between a behaviour and its consequence: if you do this, that follows. contingency management is the systematic arrangement of consequences, usually positive reinforcement, for objectively verified target behaviour; in the field of addiction, where the term is now mostly used, it means giving tangible rewards for verified abstinence. Its modern form was developed in the early 1990s by Stephen T. Higgins and his colleagues at the University of Vermont, working with people dependent on cocaine, a drug for which there was (and still is) no effective medication.

Higgins's patients gave urine samples three times a week. Every sample that tested negative for cocaine earned a voucher with a money value, which could not be cashed but could be exchanged, through the clinic staff, for goods and services that fitted the patient's treatment goals: cinema tickets, sports equipment, fees for a class, clothes for a job interview. Two features made the scheme powerful. The value of the vouchers escalated with each consecutive negative sample (in the early trials the first was worth 2.50 US dollars, and each one after it a little more, with a bonus for every three in a row), so that a long run of abstinence became increasingly valuable; and a positive or missed sample reset the value to the starting level, so that a single lapse cost the patient a great deal but did not end the programme. Over twelve weeks a patient who stayed abstinent could earn vouchers worth about a thousand dollars. The vouchers were combined with the community reinforcement approach that George Hunt and Azrin had developed for alcoholism in 1973 (Chapter 9), which rearranges a person's work, family and leisure so that sober life is more rewarding than drug use. In Higgins's trials more patients stayed in treatment and more achieved long periods of abstinence than with standard drug counselling based on the twelve-step approach.

Vouchers are expensive, and in 2000 Nancy M. Petry and her colleagues found a cheaper way in a paper whose title says it all: "Give them prizes and they will come." In prize-based contingency management each negative sample earns a draw from a fishbowl of slips. About half say "Good job!"; most of the rest win small prizes, a few win larger ones, and a single slip in the bowl wins a jumbo prize. Because the patient never knows which draw will pay, this is a variable-ratio schedule, the most powerful schedule in Section 4.5.1 and the one that keeps people buying lottery tickets. Contingency management of one kind or another is now among the best-supported psychosocial treatments for stimulant use, and it has been used for nicotine (including smoking in pregnancy, verified by carbon monoxide in the breath), alcohol, opioids (with take-home doses of methadone as the reinforcer) and attendance at treatment. Its limitations are cost, a common objection that patients are being "paid to do what they should do anyway," the need for reliable testing, and the fact that its effects tend to fade once the contingencies stop, which is why the best programmes thin the schedule gradually and build up natural reinforcers for sober living before they end.

A contingency contract (behavioural contract) is a written agreement that specifies the target behaviours, the consequences that will follow them, who will deliver the consequences, how the behaviour will be checked and for how long the contract runs, signed by everyone concerned. Lloyd Homme popularised contracts for schools in his 1970 book How to Use Contingency Contracting in the Classroom, and Richard B. Stuart used them from the late 1960s with married couples and with the families of delinquent adolescents (contracts between partners are discussed in Chapter 7). A good contract is clear (it names behaviours, not attitudes), fair to both sides, phrased positively (what will be earned, not only what will be lost), honest, and generous early on, rewarding small steps quickly so that everyone sees it work. Most contracts add a bonus clause for consistent performance, a penalty clause for failures, a record sheet and a date for renegotiation.

Consider Aditya, fifteen, in Pune, whose parents and he argue every evening about homework and his phone. The psychologist helps them write a contract. Aditya agrees to spend an hour on homework between 6 and 8 p.m. on school days, with his mother initialling the record sheet. Each initialled evening earns forty-five minutes of phone time after 8 p.m.; five initialled evenings in a week earn a bonus, a Sunday cricket match with friends without any questions asked; an evening without homework means no phone that night, and nothing more. His parents, for their part, agree not to remind him about homework more than once and not to comment on his marks for four weeks. All three sign, and they agree to meet the psychologist again in a fortnight to adjust the terms. Notice what the contract does. It turns a daily argument into a clear rule, it uses a high-probability behaviour (phone time) to reinforce a low-probability one (the Premack principle again), and it binds the parents as well as the boy, because their nagging was part of the problem.

Exam trap

Contingency management (Higgins: vouchers for verified abstinence; Petry: prizes) arranges reinforcement for an objectively checked behaviour; a contingency contract is a written, negotiated agreement that spells out behaviours and consequences; the community reinforcement approach (Hunt and Azrin, 1973) rearranges the person's whole environment so that sobriety is rewarding. All three are operant. Do not confuse contingency management with aversion therapy (classical, Section 4.4) or with disulfiram, which deters drinking by the threat of a reaction instead of rewarding abstinence.

Clinical link

Indian de-addiction services rarely have money for vouchers, but the principle travels well. Families can make privileges that matter (use of the scooter, pocket money, an outing, a visit to the in-laws' village) contingent on objectively checked sobriety, such as an evening breath test or a clean urine screen, and can be taught to reinforce sober behaviour warmly instead of attending to the person only when he drinks. Clinics can reinforce attendance itself, with small, immediate rewards and public recognition at each milestone of abstinence.

Stimulus control, generalisation and maintenance

So far this section has been about consequences. But Skinner's three-term contingency has an antecedent as well: behaviour is reinforced in some situations and not in others, and people learn the difference. A discriminative stimulus (SD) is a stimulus in whose presence a behaviour has been reinforced, so that the behaviour becomes more likely when that stimulus is present; a stimulus in whose presence the behaviour has not been reinforced is an S-delta. stimulus control is the state of affairs in which a behaviour occurs reliably in the presence of a particular stimulus and not in its absence, and, as a therapeutic technique, the deliberate rearrangement of antecedents to change behaviour. The A of the ABC model in Section 4.1 is exactly this.

Clinicians use stimulus control in two directions. The first is to narrow the stimulus control of a problem behaviour, so that fewer and fewer situations set it off. In a 1967 study of overweight women, Richard Stuart had his clients eat only at the dining table, at set times and doing nothing else while they ate (no reading, no television), so that the sofa, the television and the newspaper stopped being cues for snacking; with these and other self-control procedures his clients lost weight steadily over a year. A smoker can be asked to smoke only in one uncomfortable chair on the balcony, doing nothing else, so that coffee, telephone calls and the end of a meal stop triggering cigarettes. The second direction is to strengthen the stimulus control of a desirable behaviour. In 1965 Israel Goldiamond described a student who could not study at her desk because it was also where she did many other things, from writing letters to daydreaming. He had her use the desk for studying only; when she began to daydream she was to get up and leave the desk, so that the desk came to signal studying and nothing else. Any Indian student preparing for an entrance examination can use the same trick with a particular table and lamp. The best-known clinical use of stimulus control, Richard Bootzin's treatment for insomnia, which makes the bed a cue for sleep alone, is described in Section 4.9.

Stimulus control has a shadow side, and it is the most important practical problem in all of operant therapy. Behaviour that comes under the control of the clinic, the therapist and the token board may stay there. Isaacs's patient spoke only to the experimenter; token-economy gains faded after discharge. Three terms describe what therapists want instead. stimulus generalisation is the occurrence of a learned behaviour in situations other than the one in which it was learned (Kavya speaks up at home and at the temple, not only in class). response generalisation is the spread of change to related behaviours that were not trained (a child taught to say "please" when asking for juice also begins to say "thank you"). maintenance is the persistence of the behaviour over time, after the programme has ended.

For years therapists relied on what Trevor F. Stokes and Donald Baer, in a famous 1977 paper, called "train and hope": teach the behaviour in one setting and hope that it would appear elsewhere. Stokes and Baer argued that generalisation must be programmed, not hoped for, and listed the techniques for doing so (Table 4.10). The most elegant is the behavioural trap, a term Baer and Montrose Wolf had introduced in 1970: like a mouse trap that needs only a little bait to catch the mouse, a behavioural trap uses a small, contrived reinforcer to get a behaviour started, after which the natural reinforcers of the environment take over and keep it going. A withdrawn child who is coaxed with stickers into joining a game of cricket will soon be kept there by the game itself and by the other children.

Table 4.10 Programming generalisation and maintenance (after Stokes and Baer, 1977, and later work).
StrategyWhat the therapist doesExample
Use natural contingencies (behavioural trap)Teach behaviours that the everyday environment will reinforceTeaching play skills that peers then reward
Train sufficient examplesTeach with several people, settings and materialsHand-washing taught at school, at home and at a relative's house
Train looselyVary the instructions, times and surroundings during trainingDifferent teachers asking the same question in different words
Use indiscriminable contingenciesMake reinforcement intermittent and delayed, so the learner cannot tell when it will comeRandom checks of homework instead of daily ones
Programme common stimuliBring features of the real setting into trainingPractising with the actual school bag and bus pass
Mediate generalisationTeach the learner to prompt, monitor and reward himselfA self-monitoring card carried to class (Section 4.9)
Sequential modificationIf generalisation fails, run the programme in each setting in turnExtending a classroom token system to the home

Maintenance needs its own plan, and the techniques of this section supply it. Thin the schedule from continuous to intermittent reinforcement, and lengthen the delay between behaviour and reward; shift from contrived reinforcers (sweets, tokens, vouchers) to natural ones (praise, grades, wages, the pleasure of the activity, the approval of friends); train families, teachers and nurses to keep reinforcing the new behaviour; teach the client to monitor and reward himself (Section 4.9); and schedule booster sessions and relapse prevention (Chapter 9) for the months after treatment. The cliché that behaviour therapy "only treats symptoms in the clinic" is answered by exactly this work: a behaviour that has been generalised and maintained is, for practical purposes, part of the person's life.

Exam trap

Stimulus generalisation is the same response in new situations; response generalisation is new, related responses in the same kind of situation; discrimination is responding differently to different stimuli, the opposite of generalisation; maintenance is persistence over time. A discriminative stimulus signals that reinforcement is available; it does not itself reinforce. Stimulus control for insomnia is Bootzin's (Section 4.9); stimulus control for overeating was used by Stuart (1967) and for studying by Goldiamond (1965). "Train and hope" is the practice that Stokes and Baer (1977) criticised, not a technique they recommended.

4.6 Operant Techniques That Reduce Behaviour, and Applied Behaviour AnalysisHigh yield · 11 Qs

Section 4.5 was about building behaviour: reinforcing it, shaping it one step at a time, linking it into chains and paying for it with tokens. Yet a great many referrals to a behaviour therapist are about behaviour that there is too much of. A four-year-old screams for forty minutes whenever the mobile phone is taken away; an adolescent with intellectual disability hits his classmates; a woman on a long-stay ward hoards towels; a child with autism bangs her head on the floor when asked to stop playing. Common sense has one answer to all of these, which is to punish. Behaviour therapy has a whole family of answers, and punishment comes last in it, not first. This section sets out that family in order, from the gentlest technique to the most intrusive, and explains why the order matters. It then describes the discipline that turned these techniques into an applied science, applied behaviour analysis, and its most famous and most argued-about programme, Ivar Lovaas's treatment for young children with autism.

Four ways to make a behaviour less likely

Every operant technique rests on one fact from the learning laboratory: behaviour is controlled by its consequences. (Operant conditioning itself, from Thorndike's law of effect to Skinner's schedules of reinforcement, is taught in the General Psychology book.) A consequence can do one of two things to the behaviour it follows, strengthen it or weaken it, and it can do so in one of two ways, by adding something or by taking something away. Crossing these two questions gives the four consequences in Table 4.11. Learn them so well that you could draw the table half asleep; no distinction in behaviour therapy is confused more often.

In this vocabulary positive and negative do not mean good and bad. They mean added and removed, as in arithmetic. Reinforcement always means that the behaviour becomes more frequent; punishment always means that it becomes less frequent. So negative reinforcement is the strengthening of a behaviour because it removes or prevents something unpleasant: you take a paracetamol for a headache, the headache goes, and next time you reach for the paracetamol sooner. positive punishment is the weakening of a behaviour because it is followed by the addition of something unpleasant (a scolding, a smack, a squirt of lemon juice, an effortful chore). negative punishment is the weakening of a behaviour because it is followed by the removal of something pleasant (the cricket match switched off, a fine deducted from the pocket money).

Negative reinforcement comes in two forms. In escape conditioning (operant escape) the behaviour ends an unpleasant situation that is already present: Arjun's tantrums in Section 4.1 ended his mother's demand that he do his maths, so the tantrums grew stronger. In avoidance conditioning (operant avoidance) the behaviour prevents or postpones the unpleasant situation before it arrives: the child who develops a stomach ache every school morning avoids school altogether, and the phobic person who crosses the road avoids meeting the dog (the second factor of Mowrer's theory, Figure 4.2). Both escape and avoidance are reinforcement. They make a behaviour more frequent, so neither can be the name of a technique for discouraging one.

A fifth possibility sits outside the four boxes: the consequence that used to follow the behaviour simply stops. That is extinction, and it too makes behaviour less frequent. A behaviour therapist who wants to reduce a behaviour therefore has four levers. She can stop paying for it (extinction); pay for something else instead (differential reinforcement); take something good away (negative punishment, whose two clinical forms are time-out and response cost); or add something unpleasant (positive punishment, including overcorrection). A fifth, rarer lever acts on the reward itself, by giving so much of it that it loses its value (satiation). We take them in that order, which is also, roughly, the order from least to most intrusive.

Table 4.11 The operant consequences that change how often a behaviour occurs.
ProcedureWhat follows the behaviourEffect on the behaviourClinical example
Positive reinforcementSomething pleasant is addedIncreasesPraise when a child asks politely for a turn
Negative reinforcement (escape and avoidance)Something unpleasant is removed or preventedIncreasesA tantrum ends the homework demand (escape); a stomach ache keeps the child home from school (avoidance)
Positive punishmentSomething unpleasant is addedDecreasesA sharp reprimand; overcorrection
Negative punishment (time-out, response cost)Something pleasant is removedDecreasesTwo minutes away from play; loss of tokens
ExtinctionThe usual reinforcer no longer followsDecreases, after a burstParents stop attending to tantrums
Exam trap

Negative reinforcement is not punishment. Ask two questions in order. Did the behaviour go up or down? (Up is reinforcement, down is punishment.) Was something added or removed? (Added is positive, removed is negative.) Time-out removes access to play and attention and makes the outburst less frequent, so it is negative punishment (Real PYQ 2024, and a repeated NIMHANS item); it is not positive punishment, which adds something, and it is certainly not negative reinforcement, which would make the outburst more frequent. Operant escape and operant avoidance are both kinds of negative reinforcement, so they strengthen behaviour.

Extinction, the extinction burst and spontaneous recovery

Section 4.3 defined extinction as the weakening of a learned response when it is no longer followed by what originally maintained it, and showed how exposure therapy extinguishes conditioned fear. In operant work the procedure is just as simple to state: find the reinforcer that keeps a behaviour going and stop delivering it. A rat that has learned to press a lever for food presses less and less when the food dispenser is disconnected; a child who has learned that whining gets biscuits whines less when it stops getting biscuits. Extinction, in a phrase, is habit weakening through the withdrawal of reward.

The word "find" in that instruction is doing most of the work. Extinction means withholding the particular reinforcer that maintains the behaviour, so the therapist must first know what that reinforcer is, which is why every case begins with a functional analysis (Section 4.1; the full method is in Section 2.8). If a tantrum is maintained by attention, extinction means planned ignoring: no eye contact, no talking, no scolding, no comforting, for as long as the tantrum lasts, while the child is kept safe. If the tantrum is maintained by escape from a demand, ignoring is useless and may even help the child escape; extinction then means escape extinction, keeping the demand in place (gently, with as much help as needed) so that the tantrum no longer ends it. If the behaviour produces its own sensory reward, as when a child spins plates on a table for the sound, extinction means removing that sensory consequence. In 1978 Arnold Rincover called this sensory extinction: in one of his cases, carpeting the table top took the sound out of plate-spinning, and the spinning faded. In the 1990s Brian Iwata's group showed what this implies in practice: an extinction procedure works when it matches the function of the behaviour and fails when it does not.

Key study: Williams's bedtime tantrums (1959)

Question. Could a distressing childhood problem be removed simply by withdrawing the attention that maintained it? Method. The American psychologist Carl D. Williams described a boy of 21 months who had been seriously ill for much of his first eighteen months and had received a great deal of special care. He now threw a screaming tantrum whenever he was put to bed if a parent left the room, so a parent stayed beside him, sometimes for up to two hours, until he slept. On Williams's advice the parents put him to bed in a relaxed, affectionate way, said good night, closed the door and did not go back in, however much he cried. Result. On the first night he screamed for 45 minutes. On the second he did not cry at all, and by about the tenth night he no longer even whimpered; he smiled as his parents left. About a week later, when an aunt put him to bed, he screamed again, and the aunt stayed with him until he fell asleep. The tantrums returned, and the parents had to extinguish them a second time, which they did. In a follow-up about two years later there had been no further bedtime tantrums. Conclusion. A tantrum maintained by adult attention can be eliminated by consistently withholding that attention. Criticism. It is a single case without a control, but it shows in miniature all the features of extinction that clinicians must plan for: the reappearance of the behaviour after a pause, and how easily one well-meaning adult can undo the work.

Extinction was carried into psychiatric wards at almost the same time. In 1959 Teodoro Ayllon (whose token economy appears in Section 4.5) and Jack Michael published "The psychiatric nurse as a behavioral engineer," in which they showed that nurses could reduce problems such as a patient's constant intrusions into the nurses' office, or another's endless psychotic talk, simply by ceasing to reward them with attention, while attending to more sensible behaviour instead. Notice the second half of that sentence; it will matter in the next subsection.

Three features of extinction must be explained to every parent or nurse before it begins, because each of them looks like failure and tempts the carers to give up.

  1. The extinction burst. An extinction burst is a temporary increase in the frequency, duration, intensity or variety of a behaviour when the reinforcer that used to follow it is first withheld. A person who presses the lift button and gets no lift does not stop pressing; he presses harder and faster first. A child whose tantrums no longer bring attention screams louder and longer for a few days, may try new behaviours (kicking the door, throwing things) and may become aggressive; this extinction-induced aggression is well documented in animals and people. The burst is in fact a sign that the reinforcer has been correctly identified and that extinction has begun.
  2. Gradual decline. Unlike punishment, extinction does not stop a behaviour at once. It fades over days or weeks, and how long it takes depends largely on the schedule on which the behaviour was reinforced. Behaviour that was rewarded every time extinguishes quickly; behaviour that was rewarded only sometimes, and unpredictably, is far more resistant to extinction (the partial reinforcement effect, and the reason a variable ratio schedule is the most resistant of all, taught in the General Psychology book). This is why the parent who gives in "only occasionally" produces the most persistent tantrums of all.
  3. Spontaneous recovery. spontaneous recovery is the reappearance of an extinguished behaviour after a period of rest, without any new reinforcement. Section 4.3 described it for conditioned fear; operant behaviour shows it too, as in Williams's case. The recovered behaviour is usually weaker and briefer than before, and it fades quickly if it is still not reinforced. If it is reinforced, as by Williams's kindly aunt, it quickly regains its strength, and it has now been reinforced intermittently, which makes it harder to extinguish next time.

Let us push one case through, and watch all three features appear (Figure 4.8). Diya, aged four, lives with her parents, her grandparents and an uncle's family in a joint household in Mysuru. For six months she has thrown a tantrum, lying on the floor and screaming, whenever the mobile phone is taken away from her (at meals, at bath time, at bedtime), about eight or nine times a day in all. The ABC record kept for one week shows the pattern: the tantrum usually ends when her grandmother, wanting peace, hands the phone back. The function is access to a tangible reinforcer (the phone) together with a good deal of adult attention. The therapist meets the whole family, because extinction fails if a single adult keeps reinforcing. All of them agree to the same plan: when the phone time is over it is put on a high shelf; if Diya screams, everyone carries on eating and talking to each other, and nobody looks at her, argues, pleads or hands her the phone; she is kept safe if she flails; and the moment she is calm, an adult turns to her warmly and offers a choice of two play activities. The therapist warns them that things will get worse before they get better, and gives them a chart on which to count the tantrums.

On the second day of the plan Diya has her worst tantrum ever, nearly forty minutes, and throws a steel tumbler across the room. This is the extinction burst. The family holds firm (the grandmother leaves the room when she cannot bear it, which is allowed). By the end of the second week the tantrums have nearly stopped. Then the family spends a weekend at a cousin's house, and on the Monday evening Diya throws tantrums again. This is spontaneous recovery; because nobody gives in, the tantrums are shorter than before and fade within a few days.

Extinction has real limits. It cannot be used alone for behaviour that is dangerous (head-banging, biting, running into the road), because nobody can ignore it safely through a burst. It is impossible when the therapist cannot control the reinforcer: a classroom clown is reinforced by his classmates' laughter, not by the teacher, and a child who rocks for its own sensory pleasure is reinforced by the rocking itself. It is slow, and it teaches nothing new. For all these reasons extinction is almost never used on its own; it is combined with reinforcement for a better behaviour, which is the subject of the next subsection.

Exam trap

"A parent deliberately ignores a child's temper tantrums in an attempt to discourage them" describes extinction (Amity 2023). Some printed keys give "operant escape", and the site originally followed that key; that key is wrong. It has since been corrected to extinction, and extinction is the right answer. The tantrum was reinforced by attention, and the attention is now withheld, so the behaviour weakens. Operant escape (a response that ends something unpleasant) and operant avoidance (a response that prevents it) are both negative reinforcement, which strengthens behaviour; negative reinforcement is not a way of discouraging anything. In the same family of items: "habit weakening through the elimination of any reward" (RML 2024) and "a previously reinforced behaviour is no longer reinforced and gradually fades" (IHBAS 2024) are both extinction. The distractors are learning terms with other meanings: generalisation (responding to stimuli similar to the original), discrimination (responding differently to different stimuli), shaping (reinforcing successive approximations, Section 4.5), reciprocal inhibition (Wolpe's principle, Section 4.2) and flooding (intense exposure, Section 4.3).

Clinical link

Most extinction programmes that fail in practice fail during the burst, when an exhausted parent gives in after thirty minutes of screaming. That single surrender teaches the child the worst possible lesson: that a longer, louder tantrum works. Warn families about the burst in advance, agree beforehand what each adult will do, plan for safety, and give them something positive to do as well (attend warmly the moment the child is calm). In Indian joint families, bring grandparents into the plan from the first session; otherwise the most loving adult in the house becomes the intermittent reinforcer.

Differential reinforcement: DRO, DRI, DRA and DRL

differential reinforcement means reinforcing some responses and not others. Used to reduce a problem behaviour, it has two parts: the problem behaviour is no longer reinforced (it is put on extinction), and reinforcement is delivered instead for something else, such as the absence of the problem, a behaviour that cannot occur at the same time, a useful alternative or a lower rate of the problem itself. Because it reduces a behaviour without any punishment and builds something in its place, it is the first choice among reduction techniques. Behaviour analysts distinguish four procedures, and their abbreviations are worth knowing (Table 4.12).

differential reinforcement of other behaviour (DRO) delivers a reinforcer at the end of an interval in which the target behaviour has not occurred. "Other" means anything at all except the target, which is why DRO is also called differential reinforcement of zero rates of responding, or omission training: what is reinforced is the absence of the behaviour. Rahul, nine, picks at the skin on his fingers until they bleed. His therapist sets a kitchen timer for ten minutes; if the timer rings and he has not picked, he earns a sticker, and if he picks, the timer is quietly reset. As picking-free intervals become common the interval is stretched to fifteen minutes, then thirty, then the whole homework hour. DRO is simple and often effective, but it has a weakness. Since it reinforces whatever else the child happened to be doing, it may reinforce another problem behaviour, and it teaches nothing positive. Ogden Lindsley's "dead man test" puts the objection memorably: if a dead man could earn the reward, you are not reinforcing any behaviour, and a dead man never picks his skin.

differential reinforcement of incompatible behaviour (DRI) reinforces a behaviour that is physically incompatible with the target, so that the two cannot happen at once. A boy who bites his hand is reinforced for holding a toy with both hands; a girl who wanders round the classroom is reinforced for sitting in her seat; a child who flaps his hands at the table is reinforced for keeping them on the table or in his lap. The more the child does the incompatible behaviour, the less room there is for the problem.

differential reinforcement of alternative behaviour (DRA) reinforces a specific, desirable alternative behaviour that need not be physically incompatible with the target, but ideally serves the same function. A child who screams to get attention is taught to tap her mother's arm and say "Amma, look", and that, rather than screaming, is what now gets attention. DRA is the most constructive of the four, because it replaces the problem with a socially acceptable way of getting the same thing. Its most important clinical form was introduced in 1985 by Edward G. Carr and V. Mark Durand. In functional communication training (FCT), the therapist first identifies the function of a problem behaviour, then teaches the person a communicative response that produces the same reinforcer: a child whose tantrums are maintained by escape from difficult tasks is taught to ask for help or for a break, by words, a sign or a picture card, and asking is then reliably honoured while tantrums no longer end the task. Picture cards are often taken from the Picture Exchange Communication System (PECS), devised in 1985 by Andrew Bondy and Lori Frost for children with autism who have little or no speech.

differential reinforcement of low rates (DRL) reinforces the behaviour when it occurs at or below a set, low rate. It is used for behaviour that is acceptable in moderation but a problem in excess: the student who asks the teacher twenty questions a lesson, the patient who telephones the ward ten times a day, the child who asks for a biscuit every five minutes. In 1973 Deitz and Repp used DRL in classrooms, rewarding pupils when the number of talk-outs or other disruptions in a lesson stayed at or below a set criterion, which could then be lowered step by step. DRL aims to reduce the behaviour to an acceptable level, not to zero. (Its mirror image, differential reinforcement of high rates, increases a behaviour, and belongs with the techniques of Section 4.5.)

Table 4.12 The four differential reinforcement procedures for reducing behaviour.
ProcedureWhat earns the reinforcerExampleBest suited to
DRO (other behaviour; zero rates; omission training)A set interval passes without the target behaviourA sticker for every ten minutes without skin-pickingReducing a behaviour to zero when no single replacement is obvious
DRI (incompatible behaviour)A behaviour that cannot physically occur with the targetPraise for hands in lap instead of hand-bitingBehaviour with a clear physical opposite
DRA (alternative behaviour)A desirable alternative, ideally serving the same functionAsking for a break with a picture card instead of a tantrum (functional communication training)Behaviour whose function is known; the most constructive choice
DRL (low rates)The behaviour occurring at or below a low criterion rateA reward if the child talks out five times or fewer in a lessonBehaviour acceptable in moderation but excessive
Exam trap

Keep the four letters apart by what is reinforced. DRO: the absence of the target for an interval (time-based; "zero rates"). DRI: a behaviour that is topographically incompatible with the target (it cannot happen at the same moment). DRA: any desirable alternative, which need not be incompatible (functional communication training is DRA). DRL: the target itself, at a low rate, so the aim is reduction, not elimination. A child with autism "taught to request a break using a picture card instead of engaging in a tantrum" is receiving functional communication training, a core procedure of applied behaviour analysis (Real PYQ 2024), not CBT, play therapy or psychoanalytic therapy.

Time-out and response cost

When extinction and differential reinforcement are not enough, the next step is negative punishment: taking away something the person values, contingent on the problem behaviour. It has two clinical forms. One takes away the opportunity to earn reinforcement for a short time; the other takes away a quantity of reinforcement the person already has.

time-out (in full, time-out from positive reinforcement) is the brief removal of a person's access to reinforcement, contingent on a problem behaviour. The term comes from the animal laboratory, where a "time out" was a period during which no reinforcer could be earned, and it entered clinical work through one of the founding cases of applied behaviour analysis. In 1964 Montrose Wolf, Todd Risley and Hayden Mees described Dicky, a three-and-a-half-year-old boy with autism who had had cataracts removed and needed to wear glasses if his sight was to be saved. He refused them, and his tantrums included banging his head, slapping his face and pulling his hair. The team shaped glasses-wearing with small bites of food (Section 4.5 explains shaping), and they treated the tantrums with time-out: at each tantrum Dicky was put in his room with the door shut, and the door was opened only when the tantrum had stopped. His tantrums declined sharply, and he learned to wear his glasses for most of the day. One of the case's lessons is in the detail of the release rule. Dicky left time-out when he was quiet, never while he was screaming, so that screaming was never followed by the end of time-out.

Time-out comes in degrees of restrictiveness, and you should be able to name them.

  1. non-exclusionary time-out: the person stays where the activity is but loses access to reinforcement for a few minutes. Planned ignoring (withdrawing adult attention for a few minutes) is the mildest version; it overlaps with extinction when attention is what maintains the behaviour. In the time-out ribbon procedure described by Richard M. Foxx and Steven Shapiro in 1978, each child in a class wore a ribbon and received frequent praise and treats while wearing it; after misbehaviour the child's ribbon was removed and the child received no reinforcement for a few minutes. In contingent observation the child sits at the edge of the group and watches the others play without joining in.
  2. exclusionary time-out: the person is removed from the activity to another part of the room or to a nearby area, such as a time-out chair facing away from the group or the corridor outside the classroom.
  3. Seclusion (isolation) time-out: the person is placed alone in a separate room. This is the most restrictive form, open to abuse, and in many services it is now forbidden or allowed only under strict legal safeguards (the Indian position is given in the Indian context box later in this section).

Whatever the form, time-out works only if some conditions are met. The most important is that the environment the person is taken out of (the "time-in") must be rich in reinforcement; time-out from a boring or unpleasant situation is not punishment at all. Look back at Arjun in Section 4.1. His tantrums were maintained by escape from maths, so sending him to his room, where his toys were, was a reward, not a time-out; for him it was negative reinforcement of the tantrum. Time-out should also be brief (usually one to five minutes; a popular rule of thumb is about one minute per year of age, and longer periods add little), immediate, consistent and delivered calmly in a few words ("You hit. Time-out."). Release should depend on a short period of calm, and time-out should always sit inside a programme that richly reinforces good behaviour. Its everyday use by parents, as part of parent management training, is described in Section 4.10.

response cost is the loss of a specified amount of a reinforcer that the person already has, contingent on a problem behaviour. A traffic fine, a penalty in a football match and the deduction of ten minutes of screen time for each instance of rudeness are all response cost. Its natural home is the token economy (Section 4.5): tokens earned for good behaviour can be lost for problem behaviour. In many school programmes for children with attention deficits, the child begins the lesson with a number of points and loses one for each instance of off-task behaviour, and whatever remains is exchanged for a reward. Response cost is easy to administer, does not interrupt the activity, and has fewer side effects than positive punishment. Two rules keep it fair and effective. The person must have reinforcers to lose (some programmes give a free allowance at the start, a version called bonus response cost), and fines must not be so large that the person goes "bankrupt", because a child who has already lost everything has nothing left to work for and nothing left to lose.

It is worth being precise about how response cost, time-out and extinction differ, because all three involve "not getting" something and all three reduce behaviour. Extinction withholds the particular reinforcer that maintains the behaviour (the attention a tantrum used to win). Response cost removes a different reinforcer the person already possesses (tokens, money, minutes). Time-out removes the opportunity to obtain any reinforcement for a period of time.

Exam trap

"Removing a desirable stimulus following an undesirable behaviour in order to reduce the frequency of that behaviour" is response cost (Real PYQ 2025), a form of negative punishment. The distractors fail one test each. Negative reinforcement increases behaviour; positive punishment adds an unpleasant stimulus; extinction stops delivering the reinforcer that used to follow the behaviour but does not take away something the person already has. Time-out is also negative punishment, but the thing removed is the opportunity to earn reinforcement, for a few minutes. If the "time-out" is from an unpleasant task, it rewards the behaviour by escape and is negative reinforcement in disguise.

Overcorrection

Time-out and response cost tell a person what not to do, but they teach nothing about what to do instead. In the early 1970s two behaviour analysts working in Illinois with adults and children who had severe intellectual disability looked for a consequence that would also educate. Richard Foxx and Nathan Azrin (whose other work appears in Sections 4.5 and 4.9) described their method in a 1972 paper, "Restitution: a method of eliminating aggressive-disruptive behavior of retarded and brain damaged patients," and extended it in 1973 to the self-stimulatory behaviour of children with autism and intellectual disability. They called it overcorrection.

overcorrection is a behaviour-reduction procedure in which, after each instance of a problem behaviour, the person is required to correct its effects beyond their original state, to practise the correct form of the behaviour repeatedly, or both. It has two components.

  1. restitutional overcorrection requires the person to restore the environment damaged or disturbed by the behaviour to a condition better than it was before. A patient who overturns a table must set it upright and then straighten and dust every table in the room; a child who throws food must clean up the food and then wipe the whole of the dining table; a child who scribbles on a wall must wash off the scribble and then clean the rest of the wall.
  2. positive practice overcorrection requires the person to practise an appropriate form of the behaviour, or an incompatible one, repeatedly in the situation where the problem occurred. A child who runs down the school corridor must go back and walk it properly several times; a child who slams a door must close it gently ten times. In Foxx and Azrin's 1973 study, a child's head-weaving was followed by several minutes of guided practice in holding the head still and moving it only on instruction, and mouthing of objects was followed by brushing the teeth and gums with a mild antiseptic solution.

Foxx and Azrin laid down rules that distinguish overcorrection from simply making someone do chores. The required activity should be directly related to the misbehaviour (so that it teaches the right behaviour in the right place), it should follow the misbehaviour immediately, it should last for an extended period (several minutes or more), it should be carried out briskly with no reinforcement during it, and it is enforced by graduated guidance: the trainer gives only as much physical help as needed, moving from full manual guidance to a light touch to merely shadowing the person's hands, and removes the help as soon as the person begins to comply.

Push a case through. Manoj, fourteen, with moderate intellectual disability, attends a special school in Mysuru. When a task frustrates him he sweeps everything off his desk with his arm, several times a day. A functional assessment shows that the outburst often ends the task (escape), so the programme has three parts. First, functional communication training: Manoj is taught to hand his teacher a "help" card, and help is given at once. Second, the task stays in place after an outburst (escape extinction). Third, overcorrection: after each sweep he must pick up every item and arrange it neatly, then tidy the two neighbouring desks as well (restitution), and then place his materials gently on the desk five times in a row (positive practice), with graduated guidance if he refuses. Within three weeks the sweeping has nearly stopped and the help card is in daily use.

Overcorrection has been used for aggression, destruction of property, self-stimulation, toileting accidents and some forms of self-injury, mainly with people with intellectual disability and autism. Azrin and Foxx built elements of it into their rapid toilet-training method (1971), and Azrin's dry-bed training for bed-wetting (1974) included positive practice in getting up to use the toilet and cleanliness training, in which the child changes his own wet sheets. But it has real drawbacks. Technically it is positive punishment, because it adds an effortful, unwanted activity after the behaviour, and its "educative" value has been debated; the procedure requires a lot of staff time; physical guidance of a resisting adolescent can turn into a struggle and can provoke aggression; and its effects often do not generalise beyond the setting in which it was applied. It is therefore treated as a restrictive procedure, used only when milder ones have failed and under supervision.

Exam trap

"Over-correction is a behavioural technique to manage..." The keyed answer is repetitive aggressive (and disruptive or self-stimulatory) behaviour in autistic children and others with developmental disability (IHBAS 2020), because Foxx and Azrin developed it for exactly that population. Bed-wetting is treated chiefly with the bell-and-pad alarm (Section 4.4), although Azrin's dry-bed training borrowed overcorrection components; ordinary temper tantrums in children are handled with extinction and time-out; "alienation in adolescence" is not a target behaviour at all. Do not confuse overcorrection with negative practice (Knight Dunlap: deliberately performing the unwanted habit itself over and over, Section 4.9) or with the competing response of habit reversal (Azrin and Nunn, Section 4.9). The two kinds are restitutional (restore the environment to better than before) and positive practice (repeat the correct behaviour).

Punishment: its side effects, its ethics and the least restrictive alternative

We now reach the technique that common sense puts first. In behaviour analysis, punishment is defined by its effect: any consequence of a behaviour that reduces how often the behaviour occurs in future. It is not defined by intention or by how unpleasant it looks. A teacher who shouts at a boy for clowning, and finds that the clowning increases, has not punished the clowning; for that boy, the shouting is attention and has reinforced it. Conversely, a consequence that looks mild, such as a quiet "no", is a punisher if it reliably reduces the behaviour. Positive punishment adds something unpleasant (a reprimand, a smack, an aversive taste, an electric shock, an effortful activity), and negative punishment removes something pleasant (time-out, response cost), as Table 4.11 showed.

Is punishment effective? Skinner thought not, or not for long. In The Behavior of Organisms (1938) he reported that rats whose paws were slapped by the lever when they first pressed it in extinction pressed less for a while, but eventually made as many responses in total as unpunished rats; punishment had only suppressed the behaviour temporarily. In 1944 his student William Kaye Estes (1919 to 2011) reached a similar conclusion with mild shock. This view, repeated in Science and Human Behavior (1953), made a generation of behaviour therapists suspicious of punishment. Later laboratory research painted a more complicated picture. In a classic 1966 review Nathan Azrin and William Holz showed that punishment can suppress behaviour strongly and lastingly, but only under strict conditions: it must follow the behaviour immediately and every time; it must be intense enough from the start, because a punisher that is increased gradually loses its effect as the person adapts to it; escape from it must be impossible; it must not be associated with reinforcement (as when a child is scolded and then cuddled); and an alternative response that earns reinforcement must be available. Few parents, teachers or ward staff can meet those conditions, which is one reason punishment so often fails in daily life.

The other reason is its side effects. They are the heart of the case against punishment, and you should be able to list them.

  1. Emotional reactions. Punishment produces fear, crying, anger and anxiety, which interfere with learning and can become conditioned to the person who punishes and to the place where it happens.
  2. Escape and avoidance. The punished person learns to avoid the punisher: the child lies, hides the report card, avoids the teacher and eventually the school. These avoidance behaviours are themselves negatively reinforced.
  3. Aggression. Pain provokes attack. In 1962 Roger Ulrich and Azrin showed that pairs of rats given foot-shock fight each other; children who are punished harshly are more aggressive towards others.
  4. Modelling. The punisher models the very behaviour he wants to stop. A parent who hits a child for hitting his sister teaches, by example, that hitting is how one deals with people who annoy you (Bandura's work on modelling, Section 4.7).
  5. Narrow and temporary suppression. The behaviour often stops only in the presence of the punisher and returns when he is absent, because the person learns to discriminate when punishment is likely.
  6. It teaches nothing new. Punishment tells the person what not to do, never what to do instead.
  7. It reinforces the punisher. When a smack stops a child's whining, the parent's smacking is negatively reinforced. Punishment therefore tends to escalate, in frequency and in severity, which is how ordinary discipline slides into abuse.

Why, then, is punishment ever used in therapy? Because some behaviour is so dangerous that it must stop quickly. In 1974 Thomas Sajwaj, Julian Libet and Stewart Agras treated a six-month-old infant whose chronic rumination (bringing up and re-chewing food) had brought her close to death from malnutrition, by squirting a little lemon juice into her mouth each time she began to ruminate; the rumination stopped and she gained weight. In the 1960s Ivar Lovaas used contingent electric shock with children with autism who were injuring themselves severely, and from the 1980s devices delivering electric shocks were used in a few American programmes for severe self-injury. They remain deeply controversial: in 2020 the United States Food and Drug Administration banned electrical stimulation devices for self-injurious or aggressive behaviour, a ban that was then challenged in the courts. In 2021 a federal appeals court struck it down, ruling that the agency could not ban a device for one particular use only; a law passed at the end of 2022 gave the agency that power, and in 2024 it proposed the ban afresh, so the dispute has continued since. Most behaviour analysts today regard physical aversives as almost never justified, because functional assessment and function-based treatment (extinction plus functional communication training) now succeed with most of the people for whom aversives were once used.

The ethical rule that governs all of this is the least restrictive alternative: a client should receive the least intrusive and least restrictive intervention that is likely to be effective, and more restrictive procedures should be used only when less restrictive ones have been tried and have failed, or when the danger is too great to allow them to be tried first. The principle came from law. American courts in the 1960s and early 1970s, notably in Wyatt v. Stickney (Alabama, 1972), held that people in institutions have a right to treatment in the least restrictive conditions necessary. In 1988 Ron Van Houten and his colleagues, writing for the Association for Behavior Analysis, added an important counterweight, the right to effective treatment: a client should not be left for months on a mild but useless procedure merely because it is mild. The two principles together point to the least restrictive effective treatment.

Teachers and behaviour analysts often organise the choice as a hierarchy, set out by Paul Alberto and Anne Troutman in their textbook on applied behaviour analysis for teachers (Figure 4.9). Level I is differential reinforcement; Level II is extinction; Level III is the removal of desirable stimuli (response cost and time-out, non-exclusionary before exclusionary); Level IV is the presentation of aversive stimuli (overcorrection, reprimands and, very rarely, physical aversives). Whenever a procedure above Level I is used, good practice requires a functional assessment first, a written plan, informed consent from the client or guardian, review by a peer or human rights committee for the most intrusive procedures, continuous data to show whether it is working, a time limit, and, always, reinforcement of an alternative behaviour alongside it. A punishment procedure that is not reducing the behaviour within a short, agreed period should be stopped, not intensified.

Indian context

Indian law has settled some of these questions. The Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act, 2009, in Section 17, forbids subjecting any child to physical punishment or mental harassment in school, so corporal punishment cannot be part of any school behaviour programme. The Mental Healthcare Act, 2017, forbids chaining people with mental illness in any manner and forbids solitary confinement, and it allows physical restraint only under strict conditions; seclusion time-out and physical aversives therefore have no accepted place in Indian mental health services. Physical punishment of children at home nevertheless remains common, and much of a child clinician's work with parents consists of replacing smacking with the techniques in Levels I to III.

Exam trap

Two pairs are confused again and again. First, punishment versus aversion therapy: punishment is operant (an unpleasant consequence follows a response and makes the response less frequent), whereas aversion therapy (Section 4.4) is classical conditioning (an attractive stimulus such as alcohol is paired with nausea so that the stimulus itself becomes unpleasant). Second, punishment versus negative reinforcement: both may involve something unpleasant, but punishment decreases a behaviour and negative reinforcement increases it. On ethics, the guiding principle is the least restrictive alternative (balanced by the right to effective treatment), and the classic side effects of punishment are emotional upset, escape and avoidance, aggression, modelling of aggression, temporary and situation-specific suppression, and no new learning. The wider debate on the ethics of aversive methods is in Section 4.11.

Satiation

Every technique so far has worked on the link between a behaviour and its consequence. Satiation works on the consequence itself. A reinforcer is effective only while the person wants it: food is a powerful reinforcer before lunch and a weak one after a feast. satiation is the reduction of a reinforcer's power by giving the person so much of it, freely, that it loses its value, so that the behaviour it used to maintain declines. The classic clinical demonstration is one of the most memorable case reports in behaviour therapy.

Key study: Ayllon's towel hoarder (1963)

Question. Could a long-standing psychotic habit be removed not by withholding a reinforcer but by flooding the patient with it? Method. Teodoro Ayllon worked with a 47-year-old woman with chronic schizophrenia who had been in hospital for nine years. Among her problems was that she collected towels from all over the ward and stored them in her room, where she usually kept twenty or more; the nurses spent part of every week taking them back. Ayllon told the nurses to stop retrieving the towels and instead to bring her towels several times a day and simply hand them to her without comment. They started with about seven a day and increased the number until, by the third week, they were giving her about sixty a day. Result. At first she was delighted and folded and stacked them. As the pile grew past six hundred towels, her pleasure turned to complaint, and she began asking the nurses to stop and then carrying the towels out of her room herself. The nurses stopped bringing them. Over the following year the number of towels in her room averaged only one or two, and the hoarding did not return. Conclusion. Hoarding towels had been reinforced by the towels themselves; giving her more than she could possibly want removed their reinforcing value. Criticism. It was a single case, and an expensive one in laundry. Ayllon's report also treated the same patient's food stealing and her habit of wearing many layers of clothing with other operant procedures, so it shows how several problems in one patient can need several different techniques.

Ayllon called his procedure stimulus satiation, because the patient was saturated with the reinforcing stimulus. It should be kept distinct from response satiation, in which the person is made to perform the unwanted response itself over and over until it becomes tiresome. Knight Dunlap's negative (massed) practice for tics and habits is the classic example, and it is taught with habit change in Section 4.9. The two are often lumped together under "satiation", so read the stem carefully: towels given is stimulus satiation; a tic repeated is negative practice. A version of response satiation was also used in the treatment of sexual offenders by the Canadian psychologist William Marshall, whose satiation therapy (1979) had men repeat their deviant fantasies aloud for long periods until the fantasies became boring.

Satiation is rarely used today. Its effect is often temporary, because deprivation restores a reinforcer's value; it can require enormous quantities of the reinforcer; and it is unthinkable for reinforcers that are harmful in large amounts, such as food for an overeater or drugs for an addict. Its importance is conceptual. It shows that the power of a reinforcer is not fixed but depends on the person's state of deprivation or satiation, which is why behaviour analysts check before every session whether the chosen reinforcer is still wanted. Table 4.13 draws the reduction techniques of this section together.

Table 4.13 Operant techniques that reduce behaviour, compared.
TechniqueWhat the therapist doesTypeMain drawback
ExtinctionStops delivering the reinforcer that maintains the behaviourExtinction (no consequence)Extinction burst; slow; unsafe for dangerous behaviour
Differential reinforcement (DRO, DRI, DRA, DRL)Reinforces absence, an incompatible or alternative behaviour, or a low rateReinforcement of other behaviour plus extinctionSlower than punishment; DRO teaches nothing new
Time-outRemoves access to reinforcement for a few minutesNegative punishmentUseless if time-in is not rewarding; rewards escape-maintained behaviour
Response costRemoves a set amount of a reinforcer the person hasNegative punishmentThe person may go "bankrupt" and stop trying
OvercorrectionRequires restitution and repeated practice of the correct behaviourPositive punishment with a teaching elementStaff time; physical guidance may provoke struggle
Positive punishmentAdds an aversive consequence (reprimand, aversive taste, shock)Positive punishmentFear, avoidance, aggression, modelling; ethically restricted
SatiationGives the reinforcer in excess until it loses its valueAbolishes the reinforcer's effectOften temporary; impossible for harmful reinforcers
Exam trap

Ayllon's towel case is stimulus satiation: the patient is given the reinforcer in excess. It is not extinction (in extinction the reinforcer is withheld, the opposite of what Ayllon did), not flooding (flooding is exposure to a feared stimulus, Section 4.3) and not negative practice (repeating the unwanted response, Section 4.9). The Ayllon of the towels is the same Teodoro Ayllon who, with Nathan Azrin, ran the first token economy (at Anna State Hospital from 1961; their book on it appeared in 1968); keep the two contributions apart.

Applied behaviour analysis and its seven dimensions

Section 4.1 introduced applied behaviour analysis (ABA) as the American, operant root of behaviour therapy, and named the seven dimensions that defined it. Now that you have seen its techniques, you can see what made ABA a discipline and not just a bag of tricks. In 1968, in the first volume of the new Journal of Applied Behavior Analysis, Donald Baer, Montrose Wolf and Todd Risley, three young psychologists then at the University of Kansas, published a short paper called "Some current dimensions of applied behavior analysis." It set out what a piece of work had to be to count as ABA, and it is probably the most cited paper in the field. Table 4.14 explains the seven dimensions; the mnemonic "GET A CAB" (generality, effective, technological, applied, conceptually systematic, analytic, behavioural) helps you recall them.

Table 4.14 The seven dimensions of applied behaviour analysis (Baer, Wolf and Risley, 1968).
DimensionWhat it requiresExample
AppliedThe behaviour changed must be socially important to the person and those around himToileting, speech, self-injury, reading; not lever presses
BehaviouralThe target is observable behaviour, measured precisely, not what people say about itCounting head-bangs per hour, not "seems calmer"
AnalyticA believable demonstration that the intervention, and not something else, caused the changeReversal (ABAB) or multiple-baseline designs
TechnologicalProcedures described so completely that another person could repeat them exactlyA written protocol for every step of time-out
Conceptually systematicProcedures are derived from, and explained by, basic behavioural principles"Differential reinforcement", not "a trick that works"
EffectiveThe change is large enough to matter in the person's life, not merely statistically significantSelf-injury falls from 50 to 2 a day, not from 50 to 45
GeneralityThe change lasts over time, appears in other settings and spreads to related behavioursA child who asks for help at school also asks at home

Two of these dimensions shaped how ABA does research. "Analytic" means that ABA demonstrates its effects with single-case experimental designs rather than group averages. In a reversal (ABAB) design the behaviour is measured at baseline (A), during treatment (B), after treatment is withdrawn (A) and when it is reinstated (B); if the behaviour changes each time the treatment does, the treatment is very probably responsible. In a multiple-baseline design, treatment is introduced at different times across several behaviours, settings or people, and the change should follow the treatment each time. (These designs, the American counterpart of M. B. Shapiro's single-case method at the Maudsley, are taught in the Research Methodology book.) "Effective" led, ten years later, to the idea of social validity: in 1978 Wolf argued that the people affected by a programme (clients, parents, teachers) should be asked whether its goals are worthwhile, its procedures acceptable and its results important to them. Social validity is the dimension on which modern critics of ABA have pressed hardest, as you will see below.

From the late 1980s a movement within ABA, led by Robert Horner, Edward Carr and their colleagues, pushed the field away from aversive procedures and towards prevention, functional assessment, the teaching of replacement skills and changes to the environment that make problem behaviour unnecessary. It came to be called positive behaviour support, and it is now the standard framework in services for people with intellectual disability and autism in many countries. ABA itself has spread far beyond autism, into special and mainstream education, brain-injury rehabilitation, organisational behaviour management in workplaces, road safety, sport and health behaviour. Practitioners are certified by the Behavior Analyst Certification Board, established in the United States in 1998, whose best-known credential is the Board Certified Behavior Analyst.

Exam trap

"Behaviour analysis is based upon the principles of..." operant conditioning (RML 2021, RML 2022). Not classical conditioning (the root of systematic desensitisation and aversion therapy), not modelling or cognitive learning theories (Bandura and the cognitive therapists), and certainly not dream analysis (psychoanalysis). The seven dimensions are Baer, Wolf and Risley's (1968), not Skinner's; Skinner supplied the principles, his successors the applied discipline. A technique such as teaching a child to request a break with a picture card is ABA (functional communication training, a form of DRA).

The Lovaas programme and early intensive behavioural intervention

No application of ABA has been more celebrated, or more fiercely attacked, than its use with young children with autism.
