(Autism itself, its criteria and causes, is described in the Psychiatric Disorders book, and its assessment in the Assessment book; Chapter 10 covers clinical work with children.) The man at the centre of the story is Ole Ivar Lovaas (1927 to 2010), a Norwegian-born psychologist who spent most of his career at the University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA).

In the early 1960s, when autism was still widely blamed on cold, "refrigerator" mothers and treated, if at all, with psychoanalysis, Lovaas began to apply operant principles to children with autism who had been given up as untreatable. He taught speech by reinforcing imitation of sounds, then words, then phrases, one tiny step at a time, and he reduced severe self-injury, in some cases with contingent electric shock. A 1965 Life magazine feature on his work, "Screams, Slaps and Love," made him famous and controversial in equal measure. A follow-up he published with Robert Koegel and others in 1973 taught him two lessons. Children who went home to parents who had been trained in the methods kept their gains and went on improving, whereas children discharged to institutions lost what they had learned. And Lovaas reasoned that treatment would achieve more if it began much earlier in childhood. From 1970 his UCLA Young Autism Project therefore treated very young children, at home, with their parents as co-therapists, for most of their waking hours.

Key study: Lovaas's Young Autism Project (1987)

Question. Could very intensive behavioural treatment, begun before the age of four, allow some children with autism to reach normal intellectual and educational functioning? Method. Lovaas reported on children diagnosed with autism before about the age of four. Nineteen children in the intensive group received about 40 hours a week of one-to-one behavioural treatment, at home, at school and in the community, for two years or more, delivered by trained student therapists and the parents. A control group of 19 children received the same kind of treatment for 10 hours a week or less, and a second control group of 21 children was treated elsewhere. Children were assigned according to whether enough therapists were available, not at random. Treatment began with reducing aggressive and self-stimulatory behaviour and building compliance and imitation, moved to language and play, and then to preparing the child for an ordinary school class. Contingent aversives, such as a loud "no" or a slap on the thigh, were part of the original procedure. Result. At about seven years of age, 9 of the 19 intensively treated children (47 percent) had passed first grade in an ordinary class and scored in the normal range on intelligence tests, compared with 1 of the 40 control children (2 percent). Eight more children in the intensive group had made substantial but smaller gains. Conclusion. Lovaas concluded that intensive early behavioural treatment could produce normal functioning in almost half of young children with autism. In 1993 John McEachin, Tristram Smith and Lovaas reported that eight of the nine best-outcome children were still functioning in the normal range in late childhood. Criticism. See the paragraph that follows: assignment was not random, the outcome measures changed between assessments, the intensive group may have been more able at the start, and "normal functioning" was defined narrowly.

The 47 percent figure travelled round the world, and parents began demanding "Lovaas therapy" for their children. Critics were equally quick. In 1989 Eric Schopler (the founder of TEACCH, a structured-teaching programme for autism in North Carolina) and his colleagues argued that the sample had been selected for higher-functioning children, that the groups were not randomly assigned, that different intelligence tests were used before and after treatment, and that passing first grade in an ordinary class might reflect the school's willingness to accept a child rather than the child's recovery. In the late 1990s Frank Gresham and Donald MacMillan added that "recovery" had never been properly measured, since the children's social behaviour and autistic features had not been assessed in detail. Later studies, including a small randomised trial by Tristram Smith and his colleagues in 2000, have generally found real benefits of intensive behavioural treatment, but much smaller ones than in 1987, with very large differences between children. The method has never been abandoned, but the claim of "recovery" in nearly half of children is not accepted today.

Lovaas's method lives on as early intensive behavioural intervention (EIBI): one-to-one behavioural teaching for 20 to 40 hours a week, begun before about the age of five and continued for one to three years, following a structured curriculum and involving the parents. Its core teaching format is discrete trial training (DTT), in which a skill is broken into small units and each unit is taught in a series of short, repeated trials, each with five parts:

  1. The discriminative stimulus: a brief, clear instruction or cue ("Touch cup", with a cup, a ball and a spoon on the table).
  2. A prompt if needed: help that makes the correct response likely, such as guiding the child's hand to the cup, which is faded over later trials (prompting and fading are described in Section 4.5).
  3. The child's response.
  4. The consequence: an immediate reinforcer for a correct response (praise and a few seconds with a favourite toy), or a neutral correction ("Let's try again") for an incorrect one.
  5. A short intertrial interval of a few seconds before the next trial begins.

Discrete trial training is efficient: many learning opportunities can be packed into an hour, and progress can be measured trial by trial. Its weakness is generality. Skills learned at a table with an adult giving crisp instructions often fail to appear in the playground, at home or with a different adult. Since the 1990s, therefore, many programmes have shifted towards teaching in natural settings, during play and everyday routines, following the child's own interests. Approaches of this kind, such as Pivotal Response Treatment (developed by Robert Koegel and Lynn Kern Koegel) and the Early Start Denver Model (developed by Sally Rogers and Geraldine Dawson), combine behavioural principles with developmental ones and are now called naturalistic developmental behavioural interventions. A Cochrane review of EIBI in 2012 (updated in 2018) found evidence that it improves adaptive behaviour and intelligence test scores in some children, but judged the evidence to be of low quality, because most studies were small and not randomised.

The sharpest criticism today comes from autistic people themselves. The idea of neurodiversity, a term usually credited to the Australian sociologist Judy Singer in the late 1990s, holds that autism and other neurological differences are natural variations in human brains, to be accommodated and supported, not diseases to be cured. Many autistic self-advocates argue that ABA as historically practised aimed at making children "indistinguishable" from their peers (the word Lovaas's team used for its best outcomes), trained compliance above everything, suppressed harmless behaviour such as hand-flapping or rocking that helps them regulate themselves, taught them to hide their autism at great personal cost, and in its early years used slaps and shocks. Lovaas's 1970s collaboration with George Rekers on "treating" feminine behaviour in young boys is now cited as an example of behavioural methods used to enforce social conformity. Defenders reply that modern ABA has abandoned aversives, targets dangerous behaviour and functional skills such as communication rather than harmless differences, seeks the child's assent and the family's goals, and is the best-evidenced help available for many children with severe disability. The fair conclusion for a clinician is that the question is not "ABA or not" but "which goals, chosen by whom, and taught how": this is Wolf's social validity again, now asked by the people on the receiving end.

Consider how these ideas come together for one child. Aarav, three and a half, lives in Bengaluru and was diagnosed with autism six months ago. He has about five words, flaps his hands when excited, and screams and bangs his head on the floor when he is asked to stop spinning his top to come for lunch or to do a puzzle. A functional assessment suggests two functions: escape from demands and access to the top. The team agrees on goals with his parents, and leaves the hand-flapping alone, because it harms nobody and seems to help him. The head-banging is a priority because it is dangerous: a cushion is kept nearby and his head is gently blocked, and he is taught, by functional communication training, to hand over a "break" picture card and a "top please" card, each of which is honoured at once at first and then with gradually longer waits. Demands are made easier and shorter, and they stay in place after an outburst (escape extinction). Short discrete trial sessions teach him to identify objects and to imitate actions, and his parents are coached to use the same cues and reinforcers during bath time, meals and play, so that learning spreads beyond the table. Three months later the head-banging has fallen from about twelve episodes a day to one or two, he uses the cards many times a day, and he has twenty new words.

Clinical link

In India very few families can obtain, or pay for, forty hours a week of one-to-one therapy from trained professionals, and trained behaviour analysts are few. The realistic model is parent-mediated: a clinician assesses the child, sets a small number of socially valid goals with the family, teaches the parents to use reinforcement, prompting, functional communication and natural-setting teaching during daily routines, and reviews progress with data. Be wary of centres that promise "recovery" from autism; the honest message, based on the research above, is that intensive, well-planned behavioural teaching helps many children a great deal, unpredictably, and cures none.

Exam trap

The "Lovaas programme" uses behaviour modification for autism (RML 2022), not aphasia (a language disorder after brain injury, treated by speech and language therapy and neuropsychological rehabilitation, Section 9.9), acute depression (behavioural activation, Section 4.9) or alcoholism (aversion therapy, Section 4.4). Lovaas's 1987 paper claimed that 47 percent of intensively treated children (9 of 19) reached normal functioning, versus 2 percent of controls; its core method is discrete trial training at about 40 hours a week, and it is the ancestor of early intensive behavioural intervention. Do not confuse Lovaas with Leo Kanner, who first described infantile autism in 1943, or with Eric Schopler's TEACCH, a structured-teaching approach that was one of Lovaas's chief rivals and critics.

4.7 Modelling, Rehearsal, Assertiveness and Social SkillsLower yield · 1 Q

Peter, in the opening story, began to lose his fear of rabbits before Mary Cover Jones ever fed him crackers. He watched three other children play happily with the rabbit, and his fear started to fade. Jones called this method social imitation, and for thirty years it was a footnote. Classical and operant conditioning both require the learner to do something and experience its consequences; a great deal of human learning, though, happens by watching someone else. This section describes how behaviour therapists turned that ordinary fact into a set of techniques. It begins with modelling, moves to the rehearsal of new behaviour in the consulting room, and ends with the two training programmes built from modelling and rehearsal: assertiveness training and social skills training.

Learning by watching: modelling and its effects

The psychologist who made observation central to learning theory was Albert Bandura (1925 to 2021), a Canadian who joined Stanford University in 1953 and spent the rest of his career there. In the Bobo doll studies of the 1960s, children who watched an adult punch and kick an inflatable doll later attacked it in the same ways, although they had never been reinforced for doing so; and when the adult model was rewarded the children imitated more, and when the model was punished they imitated less. From experiments of this kind Bandura built social learning theory, later renamed social cognitive theory. The details belong to the General Psychology and Personality books; here you need only its outline. modelling is the process by which a person acquires or changes behaviour by observing the behaviour of another person, the model, and its consequences. Seeing a model rewarded makes the observer more likely to copy the behaviour (vicarious reinforcement); seeing a model punished makes it less likely (vicarious punishment). Bandura described four processes that observational learning requires: attention (the observer must notice the model), retention (he must remember what he saw), motor reproduction (he must be able to perform it) and motivation (he must have a reason to do so).

In his Principles of Behavior Modification (1969) Bandura distinguished three effects that a model can have, and each corresponds to a clinical use.

  1. The observational learning effect: the observer acquires a new behaviour he could not perform before, as when a child with autism learns to wave goodbye by watching the therapist, or a shy student learns how to start a conversation.
  2. Inhibitory and disinhibitory effects: watching a model's consequences strengthens or weakens the observer's inhibitions about a behaviour he can already perform. A child afraid of dogs who watches another child pat a dog safely is disinhibited: his avoidance weakens and his approach increases. This is the effect used in the treatment of fear.
  3. The response facilitation effect: a model prompts the observer to do something he already knows how to do and has no inhibition about, as when one person in a queue opening an umbrella prompts others to open theirs.

Behaviour therapists list five practical functions of modelling (the scheme is Michael Spiegler and David Guevremont's, quoted by Sharf): teaching new behaviour, prompting behaviour already learned, motivating it (by showing that it pays), reducing anxiety (by showing that the feared thing is safe) and discouraging behaviour (by showing its bad consequences, as an anti-smoking film does). Research has also identified what makes a model effective. Models who are similar to the observer in age, sex and background are copied more than dissimilar ones; models who are seen as competent and warm are copied more than incompetent or cold ones; several different models work better than one; and a model whose behaviour is seen to be rewarded is copied more than one whose behaviour goes unrewarded.

Models pass on standards as well as actions. In 1964 Bandura and Kupers had children watch an adult or a child model play a bowling game and reward their own scores, with sweets and self-praise, either only when the scores were high or also when they were modest. When the children then played the game themselves, with the sweets freely available, they rewarded themselves by the same standard their model had used. In 1967 Bandura and Perloff found that children who set their own standards on a dull wheel-turning task, and rewarded themselves, worked about as hard as children whose rewards were set by an adult, and many of them chose demanding standards. People, in other words, do hold themselves to standards, and they learn those standards largely by watching others; this is the foundation of the self-reinforcement procedures of self-management (Section 4.9).

One finding cuts against intuition. You might expect a fearless, perfect model to be the most persuasive. In 1971 Donald Meichenbaum compared mastery models, who perform a feared task calmly and flawlessly from the start, with coping models, who begin anxious and hesitant, talk themselves through their fear and gradually succeed. With people who avoided snakes, coping models reduced avoidance more. A coping model shows the observer not only what success looks like but how to get there from where the observer actually is, frightened. Section 5.5 shows how Meichenbaum built this insight into self-instructional training and stress inoculation.

Exam trap

A coping model starts anxious and overcomes the difficulty; a mastery model performs perfectly throughout; for anxious clients coping models usually work better (Meichenbaum, 1971). Bandura's four processes of observational learning are attention, retention, motor reproduction and motivation, in that order. "Modelling" appears as a distractor in many desensitisation and flooding items (Real PYQ 2024): it is learning by observing someone else, and it involves no relaxation, no hierarchy and no full-intensity exposure. Reciprocal inhibition is Wolpe's; reciprocal determinism (behaviour, person and environment influencing each other) is Bandura's.

Live, symbolic, participant, covert and self-modelling

Modelling techniques differ mainly in how the model is presented to the client. Five forms are recognised, and Table 4.15 summarises them.

In live modelling the client watches a real person, often the therapist, perform the target behaviour in front of him. Jones's use of other children with Peter was live modelling. In 1967 Bandura, Joan Grusec and Frances Menlove gave nursery-school children who were afraid of dogs a few brief sessions in which they watched a fearless four-year-old boy approach and play with a dog, in a cheerful party setting. The children became far more willing to approach and handle dogs themselves, and the gains were still present a month later.

In symbolic modelling the model is presented indirectly, on film or video, in a book, a comic, a puppet play or an audio recording. Its great advantage is that a carefully made film can be shown to hundreds of clients. In 1968 Bandura and Menlove found that dog-phobic children who watched films of several different children with several different dogs improved more than children who watched a single model with a single dog. The best-known clinical example is Ethan Has an Operation, a film used in 1975 by Barbara G. Melamed and Lawrence J. Siegel. Children aged about four to twelve who were waiting for elective surgery watched a 16-minute film in which a seven-year-old boy, admitted to hospital for a hernia operation, goes through admission, blood tests and the operating theatre, describing his worries and how he copes with them. Compared with children who saw an unrelated film, they showed less anxiety before and after surgery, on self-report, on observers' ratings and on a physiological measure of palmar sweating. Many children's hospitals now prepare children in this way.

In participant modelling (also called guided participation, and close to the contact desensitisation that Brunhilde Ritter used with snake-fearing children in 1968) the therapist first models the behaviour and then helps the client to perform it, step by step, with physical support and encouraging guidance, withdrawing the support as the client gains confidence. Bandura described its stages: the therapist demonstrates; the client performs the action jointly with the therapist, using "response induction aids" (the therapist's hand on the snake's head, protective gloves, shorter exposures, a nearer escape route); the aids are faded; and finally the client practises alone in varied situations (self-directed mastery). Sharf's example is a client afraid of climbing ladders: the therapist climbs a ladder, then climbs an adjoining ladder beside the client, offering encouragement and a steadying hand when needed.

In covert modelling, introduced by Joseph Cautela in the early 1970s, the client imagines a model performing the behaviour, guided by the therapist's description. In a series of studies in the 1970s Alan Kazdin showed that clients who imagined a model behaving assertively became more assertive, especially when they imagined several models and when the imagined model was rewarded. Covert modelling is useful when real models are not available or the situation cannot be staged. Sharf describes its use with a seven-year-old deaf girl who, after sexual abuse, had begun touching men inappropriately and having tantrums: she imagined another little girl, "Sara", who chose to play nicely instead of throwing tantrums and felt good about it.

In self-modelling, described by Peter Dowrick, the client watches a video of himself performing the target behaviour successfully, usually edited so that his mistakes are cut out. A child who rarely plays with others is filmed during a few moments of good play; a man who stammers watches a recording edited to show only his fluent speech. Seeing oneself succeed is a particularly persuasive kind of model, because no model could be more similar to the observer.

Table 4.15 The forms of modelling used in behaviour therapy.
FormHow the model is presentedClassic example
Live modellingA real person performs the behaviour in front of the clientBandura, Grusec and Menlove (1967): a fearless boy plays with a dog
Symbolic modellingFilm, video, book, puppets or recordingMelamed and Siegel (1975): Ethan Has an Operation
Participant modelling (guided participation)Therapist models, then guides the client through the behaviour, fading supportBandura, Blanchard and Ritter (1969): snake phobia
Covert modellingThe client imagines a modelCautela; Kazdin's studies of assertiveness
Self-modellingThe client watches an edited video of his own successDowrick's video self-modelling

Which form works best? The most famous comparison answered that question for fear, and it also set Bandura on the road to his most influential idea.

Key study: Bandura, Blanchard and Ritter's snake phobia study (1969)

Question. How do modelling and systematic desensitisation compare as treatments for a phobia? Method. Bandura, Edward B. Blanchard and Ritter recruited adolescents and adults whose fear of snakes restricted their lives. Each was first tested on a graded series of approach tasks, from looking at a harmless snake in a glass cage to the final task: letting it crawl over their lap while they held their hands passively at their sides. They were then assigned to one of four conditions. In symbolic modelling they watched a film of children, adolescents and adults interacting more and more closely with a snake, while practising relaxation; they could stop and rewind the film whenever they became anxious. In systematic desensitisation they received Wolpe's standard procedure in imagination. In live modelling with guided participation they watched the experimenter handle the snake and were then guided, step by step, to touch and hold it themselves, at first with the experimenter's help and later alone. A control group received only the assessments. Result. Live modelling with guided participation was by far the most powerful treatment: it eliminated the phobic behaviour in 92 percent of participants, who completed even the final task. Symbolic modelling and desensitisation both reduced avoidance substantially but less completely, and the controls barely changed. When the controls and the participants who had not fully recovered were later given participant modelling, they too improved markedly. The treatments also reduced fear of snakes in general and changed attitudes towards them, and some participants reported fewer fears of other kinds. Conclusion. Doing the feared thing, with a model's help, is more effective than watching or imagining it. Criticism. Snake phobias in volunteers are milder and more circumscribed than most clinical fears, and the participant-modelling condition contained more real exposure than the others, so exposure as well as modelling may explain its success.

Why did doing beat watching and imagining? Bandura's answer, published in 1977, was self-efficacy, a person's belief that he can successfully carry out the behaviour a situation requires. He distinguished it from an outcome expectation, the belief that a behaviour will lead to a given outcome: a phobic person may know perfectly well that touching a harmless snake will not hurt him (outcome expectation) and still believe that he cannot make himself do it (low self-efficacy). Bandura proposed four sources of self-efficacy, in order of strength: performance accomplishments (succeeding oneself), vicarious experience (seeing a similar person succeed), verbal persuasion (being told one can succeed) and emotional arousal (interpreting one's calm or agitation). Participant modelling supplies the strongest source, real success, along with the second, while symbolic modelling supplies only the second and desensitisation mainly reduces arousal. In 1977 Bandura, Nancy Adams and Janice Beyer showed that participant modelling raised snake-phobic clients' self-efficacy more than modelling alone, and that a client's self-efficacy rating predicted how close he would come, after treatment, to a different snake he had never seen. This is the "fifth answer" to why exposure works that Section 4.3 promised: successful exposure teaches the client that he can cope. (Self-efficacy as a general construct, with its role in cognitive therapy, is in Section 5.1.)

Exam trap

Participant modelling (guided participation) is Bandura's, and in Bandura, Blanchard and Ritter (1969) it beat both symbolic modelling and systematic desensitisation for snake phobia. Bandura did not develop systematic desensitisation (Wolpe did; Amity 2023), although he compared it with modelling. Joseph Cautela gave his name to two "covert" techniques that are easily confused: covert modelling (imagining a model performing a desired behaviour) and covert sensitisation (imagining an unwanted behaviour followed by an unpleasant consequence, an aversive technique, Section 4.4). The strongest source of self-efficacy is performance accomplishment (mastery experience), not vicarious experience or persuasion.

Behavioural rehearsal

Watching a model is rarely enough. A client who has seen exactly how to refuse an unreasonable request will still freeze when his manager asks, unless he has actually practised saying the words. behavioural rehearsal (behaviour rehearsal) is the practice of a new behaviour in a simulated situation, usually a role play in the consulting room, with modelling, coaching and feedback from the therapist, until the client can perform it well enough to try it in real life. Wolpe used it from the 1950s as part of assertive training, and the term was made popular by Wolpe and Lazarus in Behavior Therapy Techniques (1966).

Lazarus tested it in the same year. He took patients whose main complaints were specific social or interpersonal problems (difficulty refusing requests, dealing with a domineering parent, asking for a pay rise) and gave each a few sessions of one of three treatments: behaviour rehearsal, direct advice about what to do, or non-directive reflection and interpretation. Judged by whether patients actually handled the problem situation in real life, behaviour rehearsal succeeded in 92 percent of cases, advice in 44 percent and reflection-interpretation in 32 percent. The study was small and, by modern standards, loosely controlled (there was no untreated group), but its message has held up: for social problems, practising beats talking.

A typical rehearsal runs through a cycle. The client describes a specific problem situation (who, where, what was said) and what he would like to achieve. The therapist plays the other person and the client plays himself; or, in role reversal, the client plays the other person while the therapist plays the client, which both models a better response and lets the client feel what it is like to be on the receiving end. After each attempt the therapist gives feedback that is specific and begins with what went well ("Good, you looked at me and you said 'no' in the first sentence"), then suggests one or two changes ("This time, don't apologise three times"). The scene is repeated, often with coaching during the role play, until the response is fluent, and then made harder (the "manager" becomes more insistent). Many therapists record the role play on a phone so that the client can see his own posture and hear his own voice. The final step is always homework: the client tries the new behaviour in the real situation, records what happened, and brings the record to the next session. When a situation cannot be enacted, clients can rehearse in imagination (covert rehearsal).

Take Imran, a 23-year-old with social anxiety, who has been unable for three weeks to take a faulty mobile phone back to the shop where he bought it. In the first role play he mumbles, looks at the floor and accepts the first "no". The therapist models the exchange, Imran tries again, receives feedback on eye contact and on stating his request in one clear sentence ("This phone stopped charging after a week, and I'd like a replacement"), and repeats it four times against an increasingly unhelpful "shopkeeper". That evening he goes to the shop, and comes back with a new phone and the beginning of a different belief about himself.

Role play itself is older than behaviour therapy. Jacob Moreno's psychodrama (Chapter 7) used it, and role reversal is one of psychodrama's signature techniques; George Kelly's fixed role therapy (Section 5.5) asks clients to live out a written character; Gestalt therapy's empty chair (Chapter 6) asks the client to speak to an imagined person. What makes behavioural rehearsal distinctive is its aim. It is not catharsis, insight or the exploration of feelings, but the step-by-step acquisition of a skill, practised to a criterion and tested in real life.

Exam trap

Modelling, role play (behavioural rehearsal) and homework are signature techniques of behaviour therapy, and so are reinforcement and shaping; when "reinforcement, modelling, shaping" or a similar list appears as an option in a question about Carl Rogers's core conditions, it is a distractor, because Rogers's conditions are empathy, unconditional positive regard and congruence (Chapter 6). Behavioural rehearsal is associated with Wolpe and Lazarus; role reversal and psychodrama with Moreno; fixed role therapy with Kelly; the empty chair with Gestalt therapy (Perls).

Assertiveness training

Some clients cannot say no. They agree to extra work they resent, swallow criticism they think unfair, never ask for what they want and apologise for things that are not their fault, and they feel anxious, used and quietly angry. Others overshoot, and every disagreement becomes a shouting match. Both groups lack the same skill. assertiveness is the direct, honest and appropriate expression of one's feelings, opinions, wishes and rights, in a way that respects the feelings and rights of others. assertiveness training is a behavioural programme that teaches this skill, mainly through modelling, behavioural rehearsal, feedback and graded real-life practice.

Its history has three names, and the order in which they appear matters. The precursor was Andrew Salter (1914 to 1996), a New York therapist and a fierce critic of psychoanalysis (he also wrote The Case Against Psychoanalysis, 1952). In Conditioned Reflex Therapy (1949) Salter borrowed Pavlov's concepts of excitation and inhibition and argued that neurotic people suffer from too much inhibition: they have learned to hold back their feelings, and the cure is to become more "excitatory". He prescribed six exercises that every client was to practise in daily life: feeling talk (saying aloud what one feels: "I like this", "That annoys me"); facial talk (letting the face show emotion); contradict and attack (disagreeing openly when one disagrees, instead of pretending to agree); the deliberate use of the word "I" as often as possible; expressing agreement when praised (accepting a compliment instead of brushing it off); and improvisation (acting spontaneously instead of planning every move). Salter's own word was excitation rather than assertion, but his excitatory exercises are its ancestor.

Joseph Wolpe gave assertion its theory and its name. In Psychotherapy by Reciprocal Inhibition (1958) he treated assertive responses as one of the responses that inhibit anxiety (Section 4.2): a person cannot be anxious and assertive at the same moment, so practising assertion in situations that used to provoke anxiety weakens the anxiety. He defined assertive behaviour as the proper expression of any emotion other than anxiety towards another person, which includes affection and praise as well as anger and refusal. Wolpe and Lazarus set out "assertive training" in Behavior Therapy Techniques (1966), with behavioural rehearsal as its core. Arnold Lazarus later objected that assertion had come to mean only "standing up for your rights", and in 1973 he described four separate classes of assertive behaviour: the ability to say no; the ability to ask for favours and make requests; the ability to express positive and negative feelings; and the ability to initiate, continue and end general conversations.

The movement reached the public through Your Perfect Right (1970) by the American psychologists Robert E. Alberti and Michael L. Emmons, which went through many editions. Alberti and Emmons defined assertive behaviour as behaviour that promotes equality in human relationships, enabling a person to act in his own best interests, to stand up for himself without undue anxiety, to express honest feelings comfortably and to exercise his own rights without denying the rights of others. Their most useful contribution was to distinguish three styles of behaviour. passive behaviour (also called nonassertive behaviour) gives up one's own rights and wishes to avoid conflict: the person stays silent, gives in, apologises and hopes others will guess what he wants. aggressive behaviour pursues one's own rights at the expense of other people's: the person blames, threatens, shouts, insults and wins by making the other lose. Assertive behaviour respects both. Many trainers add a fourth style, passive-aggressive behaviour, which looks compliant on the surface but expresses hostility indirectly, through sulking, sarcasm, lateness or "forgetting". Figure 4.10 shows how the four styles fit together, and Table 4.16 shows how to recognise them.

Table 4.16 Recognising passive, assertive and aggressive behaviour.
FeaturePassiveAssertiveAggressive
WordsVague, apologetic, hedging ("I'm sorry, it's probably nothing, but...")Clear, direct, "I" statements ("I'd like...", "I feel...")Blaming, "you" statements, threats, insults
VoiceSoft, hesitant, trailing offSteady, audible, calmLoud, harsh, sarcastic
Eyes and postureLooks away; hunched; fidgetsComfortable eye contact; upright, relaxedStares; leans in; points
Underlying message"Your needs matter, mine do not""We both matter""My needs matter, yours do not"
Usual resultResentment, anxiety, low self-esteemRespect; problems solvedShort-term win, damaged relationships

A course of assertiveness training, whether individual or in a group (groups are especially suitable, because members can model for, role-play with and reinforce each other), usually follows five steps. First comes assessment: the therapist identifies the situations and people with whom the client is unassertive, sometimes using a questionnaire such as the 30-item Rathus Assertiveness Schedule (1973; measurement belongs to the Assessment book). Second, the client learns to discriminate the three styles, by watching examples and judging them. Third, the beliefs that block assertion are examined ("If I refuse, they will stop liking me"; "It is selfish to ask for anything"), and clients are taught that they have rights, such as the right to say no, to change their mind, to make mistakes and to ask for what they want. Fourth, and most important, the client practises assertive responses through modelling and behavioural rehearsal, working up a hierarchy from easy situations (sending back cold tea in a restaurant) to hard ones (refusing a manager), with attention to non-verbal elements (eye contact, posture, tone of voice, distance and timing) as well as words. Fifth, each rehearsed response is tried out in real life as homework and reviewed.

The trainers who followed Salter, Wolpe and Lazarus gave names to specific assertive techniques, and you should know who named which. The best-known come from the American psychologist Manuel J. Smith, whose systematic assertive therapy was set out in When I Say No, I Feel Guilty (1975). Smith's book opens with a "bill of assertive rights", the first of which is the right to judge one's own behaviour, thoughts and emotions and to take responsibility for their consequences. His named techniques are listed in Table 4.17. The two best known are the broken record (calmly repeating one's point or request again and again, in the same words, without getting angry, raising one's voice or being drawn into argument) and fogging (calmly agreeing with whatever is true, or might be true, in a criticism, without accepting the whole of it and without counter-attacking, so that the criticism is absorbed like a stone thrown into fog). In 1976 Arthur Lange and Patricia Jakubowski, in Responsible Assertive Behavior, distinguished several kinds of assertion, of which empathic assertion (first acknowledging the other person's feelings or position, then stating one's own) and escalating assertion (beginning with a minimal assertive response and becoming firmer only if the other person persists) are the most useful in practice.

Table 4.17 Named assertiveness techniques and who named them.
TechniqueWhat the person doesExampleSource
Broken recordRepeats the request or refusal calmly, without argument"I understand. I still can't lend the money."Manuel J. Smith (1975)
FoggingAgrees with any truth in a criticism without giving in"You may be right that I could have called earlier."Smith (1975)
Negative assertionOpenly admits a real mistake without anxiety or excess apology"Yes, I forgot. That was careless of me."Smith (1975)
Negative inquiryAsks for more detail about a criticism"What is it about my report that bothers you?"Smith (1975)
Free information and self-disclosurePicks up what others reveal, and reveals something oneself, to keep a conversation going"You mentioned Hampi; I've always wanted to go."Smith (1975)
Workable compromiseOffers a middle way when self-respect is not at stake"I can't do the weekend, but I'll stay late on Friday."Smith (1975)
Empathic and escalating assertionAcknowledges the other's position first; becomes firmer only if needed"I know you're under pressure, and I still can't."Lange and Jakubowski (1976)
I-messagesStates the behaviour, one's feeling and its effect, without blame"When the music is loud at night, I can't sleep."Thomas Gordon (1970)
DESC scriptDescribe, Express, Specify, ConsequencesA planned four-part request to a landlordSharon and Gordon Bower (1976)

Let us push a case through. Ananya, 27, is a junior accountant in Mysuru who lives with her husband's family. At work her manager regularly gives her other people's tasks on Friday evenings, and she always agrees, then works all weekend and cries on Sunday night. At home she cannot tell her mother-in-law that she needs an hour of rest after work before helping with dinner. Assessment shows a passive style with authority figures and elders, high anxiety in these situations and the belief that refusing would be disrespectful and selfish. In therapy she first watches the therapist model a passive, an aggressive and an assertive reply to the Friday request, like those in Figure 4.10, and names each style. Then she rehearses the assertive reply. In the first attempt her voice drops and she adds three apologies; in the fourth, she looks at the "manager", says "I can't take this on this weekend; I can finish it by Monday afternoon", and holds to it with a broken record when the manager pushes ("I understand it's urgent. I can have it by Monday afternoon"). When the "manager" says she is not a team player, she practises fogging ("You may be right that I haven't been taking on as much lately") without giving in. The next Friday she uses the reply at work; the manager grumbles, then accepts Monday. At home the therapist and Ananya agree that a broken record would be unwise with an elder in her family's culture. Instead she rehearses an empathic assertion, chosen and worded by her: "Amma, I know there's a lot to do in the evening, and I want to help. If I rest for half an hour first, I'll be much more use to you." Her mother-in-law agrees, a little surprised. Notice that assertiveness here was not a single Western script. It was a set of skills that Ananya learned to use flexibly, in a form that fitted each relationship.

Assertiveness training has been used for social anxiety, depression (where passivity cuts the person off from reward), anger problems (where aggressive clients learn assertion as an alternative to attack), the refusal of drinks and drugs in relapse prevention, the refusal of unsafe sex, workplace stress and caregiver burden, and it is a component of many cognitive-behavioural packages, including stress inoculation (Section 5.5) and the interpersonal effectiveness skills of dialectical behaviour therapy, whose DEAR MAN script includes a broken record (Section 5.8).

Exam trap

Attributions: Andrew Salter (1949, Conditioned Reflex Therapy, excitatory exercises) is the precursor; Joseph Wolpe (1958) gave assertion its rationale as an anxiety-inhibiting response under reciprocal inhibition and named "assertive training"; Arnold Lazarus (1966 with Wolpe; 1973 four response classes) broadened it; Alberti and Emmons (1970, Your Perfect Right) popularised the passive, assertive and aggressive distinction. The broken record and fogging were named by Manuel J. Smith (1975), not by Wolpe, Lazarus or Beck. Assertive is not a polite word for aggressive: assertion respects the other person's rights, aggression does not. And do not confuse assertiveness training with assertive community treatment, the team-based community care model for severe mental illness developed by Stein and Test (Chapter 9).

Clinical link

In collectivist settings, including many Indian families and workplaces, direct refusal of an elder or a senior can be seen as disrespect, and a therapist who teaches the broken record without thought may set a client up for conflict at home. Assess what the client wants and what her setting will tolerate; choose the form of assertion with her (empathic assertion, workable compromise, a request framed around shared goals); and rehearse the likely replies. The aim is not to make clients behave like characters in an American self-help book, but to give them a choice where they previously had none.

Social skills training

Assertion is one social skill among many. Starting a conversation, keeping it going, listening, taking turns, reading other people's faces, giving a compliment, asking for help, making a friend, handling a job interview, dealing with a doctor who is in a hurry: all of these are skills, and like any skill they can be missing, rusty or blocked by anxiety. social skills training (SST) is a structured behavioural method for teaching the verbal and non-verbal behaviours needed for effective social interaction, using instructions, modelling, behavioural rehearsal, feedback, reinforcement and homework.

Its theoretical foundation came from the Oxford social psychologist Michael Argyle (1925 to 2002). In The Psychology of Interpersonal Behaviour (1967) Argyle proposed that social behaviour can be understood as a social skill model, analogous to a motor skill such as driving or cycling: the person has a goal (to make a friend, to get information), perceives the other person's behaviour, translates the goal into a plan, performs motor responses (words, gaze, facial expression, gesture, posture, distance and tone of voice), and then uses feedback from the other person's reaction to correct his performance, in a continuous loop. If social behaviour is a skill, then poor social performance can be analysed into its components and each one taught, just as a cricket coach corrects a batsman's grip or footwork. With Peter Trower and Bridget Bryant, Argyle applied the idea to psychiatric outpatients in Social Skills and Mental Health (1978), teaching non-verbal signals, conversational skills, rewardingness (making interaction pleasant for the other person) and the rules of common social situations.

The best-known applications are in schizophrenia, where social disability is common and persists even when hallucinations and delusions are controlled by medication. The American psychiatrist Robert Paul Liberman and his colleagues at the University of California, Los Angeles, developed SST for this population from the 1970s, and from the 1980s packaged it into the Social and Independent Living Skills modules, whose topics include medication management, symptom management, basic conversation, recreation and leisure, and community re-entry. With Charles Wallace, Liberman analysed social skill into three stages: receiving skills (perceiving accurately what the other person is saying and feeling), processing skills (generating possible responses and choosing a good one) and sending skills (delivering the response with appropriate words and non-verbal behaviour). Patients with schizophrenia can have difficulty at any stage, so training breaks each skill into small steps, repeats them many times, and uses prompts and reinforcement generously. A widely used manual by Alan Bellack, Kim Mueser and their colleagues (1997) sets out the steps of an SST group session as follows.

  1. Establish a rationale for the skill: why it matters to the members, in their own words.
  2. Discuss the steps of the skill, written on a poster or board (for "expressing a complaint": look at the person; say calmly what the problem is; say how it makes you feel; suggest how it might be solved).
  3. Model the skill in a role play, then review the role play with the group.
  4. Engage a member in a role play of the same situation.
  5. Give positive feedback on what the member did well, specifically.
  6. Give corrective feedback: one or two concrete suggestions for improvement.
  7. Engage the member in another role play of the same situation, using the suggestions.
  8. Give additional feedback, and repeat with the other members of the group.
  9. Assign homework: a specific real-life use of the skill before the next session, to be reported back.

Here is one case. Suresh, 32, has schizophrenia and has been stable on medication for a year. He attends a day-care centre attached to a teaching hospital in Bengaluru. Since starting a new antipsychotic he has felt restless and sleepy, but at his monthly review he says nothing to the psychiatrist, who is busy, and twice he has simply stopped his tablets. In the medication management group the skill of the week is "asking your doctor about a side effect". The group names the steps (greet the doctor; describe the side effect and when it happens; ask what can be done; repeat back what you heard). The therapist models the conversation with a co-therapist playing a hurried doctor. Suresh role-plays it, speaking very softly and forgetting to describe when the restlessness happens; the group praises his greeting and his question, and the therapist suggests that he speak up and bring a written note. In the second role play he reads from his note and asks his question clearly. His homework is to use the note at his next review. He does, and his dose is changed instead of his tablets being stopped.

How well does SST work? A meta-analysis by Matthew Kurtz and Kim Mueser in 2008 found a clear gradient. Pooling 22 randomised trials, they found that the effect of SST was large on tests of what patients had been taught, moderate on performance-based measures of social and daily living skills and, to the same degree, on everyday community functioning, moderate on negative symptoms, and small on other symptoms and on relapse. That gradient, from the skills taught to the life lived, captures SST's central problem: generalisation. Skills learned in a group room do not automatically appear in a shop, a bus or a doctor's office, which is why good programmes build in homework, practice in real settings, the involvement of families and staff who prompt and reinforce the skills, and long-term booster sessions. British guidance from the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence has advised against offering SST routinely as a stand-alone treatment for psychosis; it is best seen as one part of psychosocial rehabilitation, alongside medication, family work, supported employment and cognitive remediation, which is where Section 9.8 places it.

SST is used well beyond schizophrenia. In 1969 Robert O'Connor showed that socially withdrawn nursery-school children who watched a 23-minute film of other children joining in play (symbolic modelling) began to interact with their peers far more. Social skills groups are widely run for children with attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder or autism (the PEERS programme for adolescents with autism, developed by Elizabeth Laugeson at UCLA, is a well-known example), for adolescents with conduct problems, for people with intellectual disability, for offenders and for some people with depression, whom Peter Lewinsohn saw as caught in a cycle of poor social skill and lost reinforcement (Section 4.9). In social anxiety the picture is different: most socially anxious people have adequate skills that anxiety stops them using, so modern treatment relies mainly on exposure and cognitive restructuring, with skills work for those who really lack them (Section 5.6). In schools, the broader idea lives on in life skills education, built on the World Health Organization's list of ten core life skills (decision making, problem solving, creative thinking, critical thinking, effective communication, interpersonal relationship skills, self-awareness, empathy, coping with emotions and coping with stress), which many Indian school mental health programmes teach through role play and group activity.

Exam trap

"The front-line treatment for schizophrenia is..." antipsychotic medication (RML 2021). Social skills training, CBT for psychosis, stress management and family psychoeducation are valuable adjuncts, not the first-line treatment. For social phobia, successful CBT packages include exposure, social skills retraining and cognitive restructuring together ("all of the above", IHBAS 2020). Attributions: the social skill model (social behaviour as a motor skill) is Michael Argyle's (1967); SST for schizophrenia and the UCLA Social and Independent Living Skills modules are Robert Paul Liberman's; the receiving, processing and sending stages are Wallace and Liberman's. The components of SST are instruction, modelling, behavioural rehearsal, feedback, reinforcement and homework, never free association or interpretation.

Clinical link

Interviewers for clinical psychology programmes often ask how you would help a patient with schizophrenia "who has no friends". A strong answer starts with a functional assessment of what the person wants (a friend, a job, a conversation with a sister), identifies which skills are missing and which are present but blocked by anxiety, symptoms or medication side effects, and then describes SST: break the skill into steps, model it, role-play it with specific positive and corrective feedback, and assign real-life homework with someone in the person's life to prompt and praise it. Then say how you would measure whether it worked outside the room.

Scan Your Brain

  3. Reinforcing a behaviour that cannot physically occur at the same time as the problem behaviour is called .
  4. Time-out and response cost are both forms of punishment.
  5. Requiring a patient who overturned a table to straighten every table in the room is overcorrection, a method described by Foxx and Azrin in 1972.
  6. Ayllon's patient stopped hoarding towels after she was given hundreds of them, an example of stimulus .
