4.8 Relaxation and BiofeedbackMedium yield · 5 Qs

Relaxation has already appeared twice in this chapter. In Section 4.2 it was the response that Wolpe set against anxiety at every rung of the hierarchy, and in Section 4.3 the dismantling studies showed that exposure works perfectly well without it. You might conclude that relaxation is a pleasant extra that behaviour therapy could do without. It is not. For a large group of problems the trouble is not a fear of one particular thing but a body that is too often, and for too long, too aroused: the tension headache that builds through a working day, the jaw clenched over a pile of answer scripts, the shoulders that creep up towards the ears while a person worries, the hands that turn white and numb in an air-conditioned office. For these the goal is not to face a feared stimulus but to learn a skill, the skill of switching arousal off at will. This section describes the main ways of teaching that skill, from Jacobson's progressive relaxation to Öst's applied relaxation, and then biofeedback, which uses instruments to make the body's hidden signals visible so that they can be brought under control. Section 9.4 describes how these methods are used in health settings; here the emphasis is on where each method came from, how it is taught and how well it works.

Progressive muscle relaxation: Jacobson's method and its abbreviations

Edmund Jacobson (1888 to 1983) was a Chicago physician and physiologist who began studying muscular tension as a graduate student at Harvard in the first decade of the twentieth century. One of his early observations was that people who were tense startled much more violently at a sudden noise than people who were relaxed. Over the next twenty years, first at Harvard and then in Chicago, he built that observation into a theory and a treatment. Anxiety, he argued, is not only a state of mind; it is also a pattern of contraction in the skeletal muscles, and the two are so tightly bound that a person whose muscles are completely relaxed cannot at the same time feel anxious. His premise is often paraphrased as "an anxious mind cannot exist in a relaxed body." He set out the method in Progressive Relaxation (1929; enlarged second edition 1938) and for general readers in You Must Relax (1934).

progressive muscle relaxation (PMR) is a technique in which a person learns to relax deeply by tensing and then releasing one muscle group after another, attending closely to the contrast between the sensations of tension and of letting go. The word "progressive" has three meanings, all of which Jacobson intended: the training progresses from one muscle group to the next, the relaxation itself progresses to deeper and deeper levels, and the skill progresses from the couch to everyday life.

Jacobson's own method was slow and exacting. The patient lay on a couch and, for one muscle group at a time, produced a slight contraction (bending the hand back at the wrist, for example) in order to learn to recognise the faint sensation of tension, which Jacobson called the control sensation. Then the patient simply stopped contracting. Jacobson insisted that relaxation is not something one does but the absence of doing: trying hard to relax is itself a form of tension. Each group might take several hour-long sessions, and a full course ran to dozens of sessions over months. He also taught differential relaxation, keeping tense only the muscles a task needs (the fingers that write, the eyes that read) and relaxing all the rest, so that a person could stay relaxed while driving, typing or talking. In the 1930s Jacobson recorded the tiny electrical potentials of muscles with sensitive instruments and showed that merely imagining lifting the right arm produced measurable activity in the muscles of that arm. That work makes him one of the ancestors of the electromyographic biofeedback described later in this section.

No busy clinic could spend fifty sessions on relaxation, and the method survived because it was cut down. Wolpe compressed it into about six sessions for systematic desensitisation (Section 4.2). In 1973 Douglas A. Bernstein and Thomas D. Borkovec published Progressive Relaxation Training: A Manual for the Helping Professions, and their abbreviated version is the one most clinicians now teach. Unlike Jacobson, they used a strong, deliberate tension, because a beginner notices the contrast more easily when the tension is obvious. The client focuses on one muscle group, tenses it on the therapist's signal for about 5 to 7 seconds, releases it all at once on the cue word "relax," and attends to the spreading feeling of relaxation for 30 to 40 seconds; each group is usually done twice. Training begins with 16 muscle groups (hands and forearms, upper arms, forehead, cheeks and nose, jaw, neck, chest and shoulders, abdomen, thighs, calves and feet, right and left separately). As skill grows the groups are combined, first into 7 and then into 4. Then the tensing is dropped altogether: in relaxation by recall the client simply remembers the feeling of release in each group, then recall is combined with slow counting from one to ten, and finally counting alone is enough to produce relaxation. Clients practise for 15 to 20 minutes once or twice a day, and the practice matters: people who practise at home improve more than those who do not.

Consider Farida, a 38-year-old schoolteacher in Mysuru who has had a tension-type headache most afternoons for two years. The pain starts as a band across her forehead and tightens round to the back of her neck. Her physician has found no medical cause. In the first session of PMR she discovers something that surprises her: when she is asked to release her jaw, it drops a full centimetre, because she had been clenching it without knowing. Over three weeks she works through the 16 groups, then the 7 and the 4; by the eighth week she can relax in about two minutes by counting slowly to ten, and she has learned to catch the tight jaw and raised shoulders while correcting notebooks, and to let them go. Her headache diary shows the afternoon headaches falling from five days a week to one. Notice that the decisive step was not the deep relaxation lying down but the transfer of the skill to the classroom, a point Öst would later make the centre of his method.

Progressive relaxation has good evidence for tension-type headache, for insomnia (Section 4.9) and, as part of applied relaxation, for generalised anxiety; it lowers blood pressure modestly and helps with chemotherapy nausea and chronic pain (Section 9.4). It has one paradoxical side effect that every therapist should know about. In 1983 Frederick J. Heide and Borkovec gave chronically tense adults one session of progressive relaxation and one of mantra meditation; about 31 percent reported that the relaxation session made them more tense, and about 54 percent said the same of meditation. This relaxation-induced anxiety seems most likely in people who fear losing control or who are alarmed by unfamiliar bodily sensations (heaviness, tingling, a slowing heart). The remedy is to warn clients in advance that such sensations are harmless, let them keep their eyes open and stay in control of the pace, and treat the sensations as information rather than danger.

Exam trap

"Progressive deep relaxation" is Edmund Jacobson's technique (RML 2022), not Wolpe's, Skinner's or Pavlov's. Wolpe borrowed and shortened it for systematic desensitisation; Bernstein and Borkovec (1973) wrote the abbreviated manual. For the date, Progressive Relaxation appeared in 1929 and its enlarged second edition in 1938; either may be quoted. Notes that give "1920" (Psycho Didi, for example) are pointing loosely to the 1920s, the decade in which Jacobson worked the method out and first published it; no book of his appeared in 1920. Beware of the other Jacobsons: Edith Jacobson was a psychoanalyst of object relations (Chapter 3), Neil Jacobson developed behavioural activation (Section 4.9) and integrative couple therapy (Chapter 7), and Lenore Jacobson co-authored the Pygmalion study of teacher expectancy with Robert Rosenthal (Social book).

Autogenic training: relaxation by self-suggestion

Progressive relaxation reaches the mind through the muscles. Autogenic training takes the opposite road. Its founder, the Berlin psychiatrist Johannes Heinrich Schultz (1884 to 1970), had worked with the brain researcher Oskar Vogt, who studied hypnosis in the 1890s and noticed that some of his patients could put themselves, briefly and on their own, into a restful hypnotic-like state and came out of it refreshed. Schultz noticed something else: hypnotised people very often reported two sensations, heaviness and a pleasant warmth in the limbs. He asked whether a person could produce those sensations, and the deep rest that went with them, without a hypnotist at all, simply by attending to them. He presented his answer to colleagues in Berlin in the 1920s and published it in 1932 as Das autogene Training. The German physician Wolfgang Luthe (1922 to 1985), who trained with Schultz and later worked in Montreal, brought the method to the English-speaking world and with Schultz wrote the multi-volume Autogenic Therapy (from 1969).

autogenic training (autogenic means "self-generated") is a method of deep relaxation in which a person silently repeats a fixed series of verbal formulae describing sensations of heaviness, warmth and calm in the body, and attends to those sensations until they arise of their own accord. The attitude Schultz demanded is passive concentration: a casual, effortless attention to the formula and to the part of the body it names, without trying to make anything happen and without caring whether it does. The moment the trainee strains to make the arm feel heavy, the effort itself produces tension, which is the same point Jacobson made about trying to relax.

The trainee sits or lies in one of three positions: lying flat, reclining in an armchair, or the so-called coachman's posture, sitting on a stool slumped forward with the head hanging and the forearms resting on the thighs, like a cab driver dozing on his seat. Eyes closed, he repeats the formula for the current exercise several times, interspersed with the calming phrase "I am completely calm." There are six standard exercises, always learned in the same order (Table 4.18). Each is practised for a few minutes, two or three times a day, and a new exercise is added only when the previous one comes easily, typically after a week or two, so that the whole sequence takes some weeks to a few months to master. Every practice ends with a deliberate "taking back" (cancellation): the trainee flexes the arms vigorously, breathes deeply and opens his eyes, so that he does not walk away groggy. During training people sometimes notice brief twitches, tingling, images or surges of feeling, which Schultz and Luthe called autogenic discharges and regarded as harmless.

Table 4.18 The six standard exercises of Schultz's autogenic training, in the order in which they are learned.
ExerciseTypical formula (repeated silently)Bodily target
1. Heaviness"My right arm is heavy," then both arms, then the legsRelaxation of the skeletal muscles
2. Warmth"My right arm is warm," spreading to all the limbsWidening of the blood vessels in the limbs
3. Heart"My heartbeat is calm and regular"Heart rate and rhythm
4. Breathing"My breathing is calm" (or "It breathes me")Slow, effortless respiration
5. Solar plexus"My solar plexus is warm"Warmth in the upper abdomen; the digestive organs
6. Forehead"My forehead is pleasantly cool"A cool, clear head

Look at the right-hand column of Table 4.18 and you will see the logic. Heaviness is what relaxed muscles feel like; warmth is what dilated blood vessels feel like, which happens when sympathetic activity falls; the heart and breathing exercises address the two rhythms most disturbed by anxiety. Autogenic training is therefore aimed squarely at the autonomic nervous system, through suggestion, which is why it is so closely related to self-hypnosis. Trainers usually leave out or soften the heart exercise for people with heart disease or with a fear centred on the heart, and the method is not taught during an acute psychotic episode. After the standard exercises, advanced trainees may go on to meditative exercises (holding a colour or an image in mind) and to short personal resolutions ("Examinations are unimportant; I stay calm").

Autogenic training has always been more popular in Germany and the rest of continental Europe than in Britain, America or India. A meta-analysis of 60 controlled outcome studies by Friedhelm Stetter and Sirko Kupper (2002) found medium-sized benefits over control conditions for tension headache and migraine, mild hypertension, anxiety, mild depression and sleep problems, among others; but when autogenic training was compared head to head with other psychological treatments it was usually no better and sometimes slightly worse. Its formulae also found a second life in biofeedback: the Menninger Foundation's treatment of migraine, described later in this section, combined autogenic phrases about warm hands with an instrument that showed the patient her finger temperature.

Exam trap

Autogenic training is Johannes Heinrich Schultz's (1932) and works by self-suggestion (passive concentration on formulae of heaviness and warmth); progressive muscle relaxation is Jacobson's and works by tensing and releasing muscles. The first autogenic exercise is heaviness, the second warmth. Some revision notes describe PMR as "bottom up" (body to mind) and autogenic training as "top down" (mind to body). Do not confuse J. H. Schultz with Duane P. Schultz and Sydney Ellen Schultz, the authors of a well-known history of psychology textbook, or with William Schutz, who devised the FIRO theory of interpersonal needs and ran encounter groups (group therapies, Chapter 7).

Breathing retraining and the relaxation response

Try this for one minute: breathe quickly and deeply through your mouth, as if you had just run for a bus. Most people soon feel light-headed and dizzy, notice tingling in the fingers or round the mouth, feel short of breath despite breathing hard, and sense their heart racing. Some feel strangely unreal. Breathing more than the body needs, called hyperventilation, washes carbon dioxide out of the blood faster than the body produces it, the blood becomes slightly more alkaline, and the resulting sensations are almost exactly the symptoms of a panic attack. In the 1980s several researchers, among them the American psychologist Ronald Ley, argued that chronic over-breathing plays a causal part in many panic attacks; if so, teaching people to breathe slowly should prevent them.

Key study: Clark, Salkovskis and Chalkley's respiratory control (1985)

Question. Can panic attacks be reduced by treating the breathing that seems to drive them? Method. At Oxford, David M. Clark, Paul M. Salkovskis and Anne Chalkley treated 18 patients with frequent panic attacks in three steps: a brief bout of deliberate over-breathing in the clinic, which brought on a mild attack; an explanation of how over-breathing produces exactly these sensations, so that the patient could reattribute his attacks to breathing rather than to a heart attack or madness; and training in slow, controlled breathing. Result. Within two weeks panic attacks were much less frequent, and fear in a behavioural test had fallen, even though the patients had not been exposed to the situations they feared; further gains appeared at follow-up, though by then other treatments had been added. Conclusion. Respiratory control, together with a new explanation of the symptoms, can reduce panic. Criticism. There was no control group, and the treatment contained two ingredients, breathing and reattribution. Clark himself came to believe that the reattribution, the change in what the patient thought the sensations meant, was the more important of the two, and that idea grew into his cognitive model of panic (Section 5.6).

breathing retraining is the teaching of slow, regular, abdominal (diaphragmatic) breathing as a way to correct over-breathing and to calm arousal quickly. The client sits upright with one hand on the chest and one on the abdomen, breathes in through the nose so that the lower hand rises while the upper hand stays almost still, and slows the rhythm to about 8 to 10 breaths a minute, with an out-breath slightly longer than the in-breath and a short pause before the next breath. Some programmes pace the breathing with a recorded count or a phone app. In Foa's prolonged exposure for post-traumatic stress (Section 4.3) the client silently says a calming word such as "calm" on each out-breath. As with any relaxation skill, it is practised for ten minutes or so twice a day while calm before it is used when anxious.

Breathing retraining feels natural and clients like it, but the evidence has turned out to be weaker than its popularity. In 2000 Norman B. Schmidt and his colleagues randomly assigned 77 patients with panic disorder to cognitive behavioural treatment with breathing retraining, the same treatment without it, or a waiting list. Breathing retraining added nothing, and some of the data suggested that patients who received it did slightly worse. The likely reason takes you back to Section 4.3. A patient who believes "I survived that attack only because I controlled my breathing" is using the breathing as a safety behaviour, and so never learns that the sensations were harmless all along. Modern panic treatment therefore relies more on interoceptive exposure, deliberately bringing on the sensations, than on techniques to make them go away, and uses breathing, if at all, as a temporary coping skill early in treatment rather than as an escape. A more technical descendant, capnometry-assisted respiratory training, developed by Alicia Meuret and her colleagues in the 2000s, uses a sensor that shows the patient the carbon dioxide level in his exhaled breath and teaches him to raise it; it is, in effect, a form of biofeedback, and in trials it has done about as well as cognitive training for panic.

Indian context

Regulated breathing has a far longer history in India than in behaviour therapy. Pranayama, the breath control of yoga, includes slow, alternate-nostril breathing that closely resembles breathing retraining, and many Indian clients already know it; building on a practice the client trusts often improves adherence. Not every yogic breathing method is slow, however. In 2000 a team at NIMHANS, Bengaluru (N. Janakiramaiah, B. N. Gangadhar and colleagues) randomly assigned 45 inpatients with melancholic depression to Sudarshan Kriya Yoga, a practice built on rhythmic, partly rapid breathing, or to electroconvulsive therapy or the antidepressant imipramine. All three groups improved; remission rates at four weeks were 93 percent with ECT, 73 percent with imipramine and 67 percent with the yoga, so the yoga was inferior to ECT but similar to the drug, in a trial that could not be blinded.

While psychologists were refining relaxation techniques, a cardiologist arrived at the same destination from a different direction. In the late 1960s practitioners of Transcendental Meditation, a mantra-based meditation popularised by Maharishi Mahesh Yogi, approached Herbert Benson (1935 to 2022) at Harvard Medical School and offered to show that they could lower their blood pressure. With the physiologist Robert Keith Wallace, Benson recorded meditators' physiology and found that oxygen consumption, heart rate and breathing rate fell during meditation, a state of wakeful low metabolism. Benson's next step was the important one. He argued that there was nothing unique about Transcendental Meditation, or indeed about any one tradition: prayer, meditation, autogenic training and progressive relaxation all trigger the same innate reaction, which he called the relaxation response, a coordinated fall in sympathetic nervous activity that is the physiological opposite of the fight-or-flight response described early in the twentieth century by the physiologist Walter Bradford Cannon. He set this out in The Relaxation Response (1975).

Section 9.4 lists the four elements that Benson said elicit the response: a quiet environment, a mental device (a word, sound or phrase repeated silently, or the breath), a passive attitude towards distracting thoughts, and a comfortable position. His own procedure is simple enough to teach in one session. Sit quietly with eyes closed, relax the muscles from the feet upwards, breathe through the nose, and on each out-breath silently say "one." When thoughts intrude, do not fight them; return to the word. Continue for 10 to 20 minutes, once or twice a day. Later Benson encouraged people to choose a word or short prayer from their own faith (a "faith factor" in his phrase), so that an Indian client might use "Om," "Ram" or a line of a prayer, which makes the method easy to fit to a client's culture. Mindfulness meditation, which is often confused with relaxation, has a different aim: noticing experience without judging it, whether or not one becomes calm (Section 5.9).

Exam trap

The relaxation response is Herbert Benson's term (1975); the fight-or-flight response it opposes is Walter Cannon's; the general adaptation syndrome (alarm, resistance, exhaustion) is Hans Selye's description of the stress response and is not a relaxation technique at all. Transcendental Meditation was Maharishi Mahesh Yogi's, not Benson's: Benson's point was that its benefits did not depend on its mantra or its tradition. And remember the hyperventilation logic: over-breathing produces panic-like sensations (which is why interoceptive exposure uses it deliberately), whereas slow breathing retraining aims to prevent them.

Applied relaxation: relaxation as a portable coping skill

Every method so far has one practical weakness. Relaxation is learned lying down, with eyes closed, in a quiet room, over twenty minutes. Anxiety, however, strikes at bus stops, in meetings and in examination halls, and nobody can lie down in an examination hall and tense sixteen muscle groups. In the early 1970s two American psychologists reframed relaxation as an active coping skill rather than a passive state: Marvin R. Goldfried (1971) recast systematic desensitisation as training in self-control, teaching clients to relax away anxiety as it arose during imagined scenes, and Richard Suinn and Frank Richardson (1971) described anxiety management training on the same principle. The most complete version came from Sweden.

applied relaxation is Lars-Göran Öst's (1987) method of training a person, step by step, to relax within 20 to 30 seconds in any everyday situation and to apply that skill at the first signs of anxiety. Öst, whom you met in Section 4.3 as the author of the one-session treatment for phobias, described it in "Applied relaxation: description of a coping technique and review of controlled studies," published in Behaviour Research and Therapy in 1987. The method usually takes about 10 to 12 weekly sessions, with home practice twice a day, and moves through a fixed sequence of stages, each one shorter and more portable than the one before (Figure 4.11).

  1. Self-observation. Before learning to relax, the client keeps a diary of anxiety episodes and learns to spot his own early warning signs: a tight throat, cold hands, a particular worrying thought. Relaxation is useful only if it is applied early, before anxiety has taken hold.
  2. Tension-release relaxation. The client learns progressive relaxation in the Bernstein and Borkovec style, tensing and releasing the muscle groups; a session takes 15 to 20 minutes.
  3. Release-only relaxation. The tensing is dropped and the client relaxes each group directly, which halves the time to about 5 to 7 minutes.
  4. Cue-controlled relaxation. The client pairs relaxation with his breathing, thinking "inhale" on the in-breath and "relax" on the out-breath while letting tension go, so that the word itself becomes a conditioned cue for relaxing (hence the name cue-controlled relaxation); relaxation now takes 2 to 3 minutes.
  5. Differential relaxation. Using Jacobson's idea, the client learns to relax the muscles he does not need while sitting, writing, telephoning and walking, so that relaxation no longer requires lying still.
  6. Rapid relaxation. The goal of the training, rapid relaxation, is relaxing in 20 to 30 seconds, practised 15 to 20 times a day in natural settings. Everyday events are used as reminders (a glance at the watch, a coloured sticker on the phone, entering a room): the client takes one to three slow breaths, thinks "relax" on each out-breath, scans the body for tension and lets it go.
  7. Application training. Finally the client practises the skill under real stress: the therapist deliberately provokes anxiety (by hyperventilation, by frightening film clips, by role-played confrontations, or by brief exposure to the feared situation), and the client relaxes it away. A maintenance programme follows: rapid relaxation daily and a full body scan once or twice a week.

Let us push a case through. Anjali, a 34-year-old bank officer in Mysuru, has worried excessively about almost everything (her health, her son's marks, her mother-in-law's moods, mistakes at work) for most of her adult life; she sleeps poorly and has constant aches in her neck and shoulders. Her diary in the first week shows that her earliest sign is not a thought at all but a tightening across her shoulders, followed by shallow breathing, usually within a minute of a message arriving on her phone. She learns tension-release relaxation in two weeks, release-only in two more, and cue-controlled relaxation by the sixth week. She puts a small green sticker on the back of her phone, and every time she picks it up she takes a slow breath, thinks "relax" and drops her shoulders: rapid relaxation, perhaps thirty times a day. In the application sessions the therapist asks her to imagine her manager calling her in about an error, and later to rehearse a difficult conversation with her mother-in-law, while she relaxes away the rising tension. By the twelfth week she still worries, but the worries no longer run on unchecked for hours, and the neck pain has eased.

Öst's 1987 review of 18 controlled studies found applied relaxation clearly better than no treatment or attention placebo, and as effective as the other behavioural methods with which it had been compared, across panic, phobias, generalised anxiety and several pain conditions. For generalised anxiety disorder, Öst and Elisabeth Breitholtz (2000) found applied relaxation about as effective as cognitive therapy, and the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) in England recommends it as an alternative to cognitive behaviour therapy for that disorder. For panic disorder the record is mixed: in a well-known trial by Clark and his colleagues (1994), cognitive therapy did better than applied relaxation (Section 5.6). Table 4.19 brings together the relaxation methods of this section.

Table 4.19 The main relaxation methods compared.
MethodOriginatorWhat the person doesTypical uses
Progressive muscle relaxationEdmund Jacobson (1929; 2nd edition 1938)Tenses and releases muscle groups, noticing the contrastTension headache, insomnia, anxiety; step 1 of desensitisation
Abbreviated progressive relaxationBernstein and Borkovec (1973)16, then 7, then 4 groups; then recall and countingThe standard clinical version
Autogenic trainingJ. H. Schultz (1932)Repeats formulae of heaviness, warmth and calm with passive concentrationAnxiety, headache, mild hypertension, sleep
Breathing retrainingDeveloped for panic in the 1980s (Clark, Salkovskis and Chalkley, 1985)Slow abdominal breathing, about 8 to 10 breaths a minutePanic (now used with caution), part of prolonged exposure
