Exam trap

Öst has two techniques in this chapter: applied relaxation (1987), a coping skill for anxiety, and the one-session treatment for specific phobias (1989; Section 4.3), which is intensive exposure. Within applied relaxation, cue-controlled relaxation links a cue word ("relax") to the out-breath; rapid relaxation is the 20 to 30 second version triggered by everyday reminders; differential relaxation (a term that goes back to Jacobson) relaxes the muscles not needed for the current activity. The defining feature of applied relaxation is that the skill is applied at the early signs of anxiety in real situations, not merely practised once a day; a question that offers "a relaxation exercise practised once a day" as a treatment for a habit is describing neither applied relaxation nor habit reversal (Section 4.9).

Biofeedback: making the body's signals visible

Can you lower your skin temperature by two degrees? Slow your heart by ten beats a minute? Relax one muscle in your forehead while keeping the one beside it tense? Most people would say no, and the reason is not that the body cannot do these things but that we receive almost no information about them. You cannot learn to throw darts blindfolded, because you never find out where the dart landed. biofeedback removes the blindfold. It is a procedure in which an instrument measures a bodily process that a person cannot normally sense (muscle tension, skin temperature, sweat gland activity, heart rhythm, brain waves) and feeds it back to him, moment by moment, as a tone, a light or a moving line on a screen, so that he can learn to control it. Section 9.4 introduced the technique; here we look at how it works and where it came from.

Figure 4.12 shows the loop. A sensor picks up the physiological signal, a computer amplifies and processes it, and a display turns it into something the person can see or hear. The person tries a strategy (imagining warmth in the hands, letting the jaw go slack, slowing the breath) and watches what happens. Any change in the desired direction is signalled at once, so the feedback serves two functions: it is information (it tells him which strategies work) and it is reinforcement (a falling tone or a rising line is a small reward for each success). Biofeedback is therefore an application of operant conditioning. The therapist shapes the response by setting an easy target at first and raising it as the client succeeds, exactly as in shaping (Section 4.5), and then fades the feedback, so that the client learns to produce the change without the machine, in the places where he needs it.

The idea is older than the word. In 1948 the Los Angeles gynaecologist Arnold Henry Kegel (1894 to 1981) published his exercises for women whose pelvic floor muscles had been weakened by childbirth, leaving them unable to hold their urine when they coughed or laughed. The difficulty was that most women cannot find these muscles by feel, so Kegel gave them a perineometer, an air-filled chamber placed in the vagina and connected to a pressure gauge. As the woman squeezed the right muscles she watched the needle rise, and she could track her strength as it grew week by week. The perineometer made an invisible muscle visible, which makes Kegel's method an early form of biofeedback. In 1963 the anatomist John V. Basmajian showed in Science that people listening to the amplified electrical activity of their own muscles could learn to fire a single motor unit (one nerve cell and the muscle fibres it controls) while keeping its neighbours silent, and some became skilled enough to tap out drum rolls with one unit. Around the same time Joe Kamiya, working first at the University of Chicago, rang a bell at intervals and asked volunteers to guess whether their brains were producing alpha waves, the 8 to 12 cycles a second rhythm of relaxed wakefulness. With feedback they learned first to recognise the state and then to produce it at will, and the "alpha craze" of the late 1960s followed. But the experiments that convinced the scientific world that internal organs could be trained came from an animal laboratory.

Key study: Miller's visceral learning, and its failure to replicate (1967 to 1986)

Question. For decades learning theorists had divided responses into two kinds: skeletal, "voluntary" responses, which could be shaped by their consequences (operant conditioning), and visceral, "involuntary" responses of the heart, gut and glands controlled by the autonomic nervous system, which could only be classically conditioned. Whenever a person seemed to change his heart rate for a reward, critics argued that he had done it indirectly, by tensing muscles or changing his breathing. Could autonomic responses be operantly conditioned directly? Method. Neal Elgar Miller (1909 to 2002), the American psychologist who had earlier written Personality and Psychotherapy with Dollard (Section 4.1), and his colleague Leo DiCara paralysed rats with curare, a drug that blocks the junction between nerve and skeletal muscle, and kept them alive on artificial respiration. A paralysed rat cannot use its muscles or its breathing to influence its heart. Because it cannot eat either, the reward was a brief electrical stimulation of a pleasure centre in the brain, delivered whenever the heart rate shifted slightly in the required direction, with the criterion raised step by step. Result. Rats rewarded for speeding up learned to speed up their hearts, and rats rewarded for slowing down learned to slow them. Over the next few years Miller's laboratory reported operant control of blood pressure, intestinal contractions, urine formation and even the blood flow in one ear but not the other, and Miller summarised the work in "Learning of visceral and glandular responses" in Science in 1969. Conclusion. The autonomic nervous system appeared to be open to operant conditioning after all, which suggested that people might learn to control their own blood pressure, heart rhythm and gut, and clinical biofeedback grew rapidly on that promise. Criticism. The effects grew smaller in each new series of experiments and then vanished. Miller himself, with Barry R. Dworkin, spent years trying to reproduce them, and in 1986 they reported that in experiments on more than 2,500 rats the original findings could not be replicated; the visceral learning of curarised rats remains unproven. Miller published the failure himself, a model of scientific honesty. By then, however, human biofeedback had its own evidence, and in people whose muscles and breathing work normally it does not matter clinically whether the control is direct or "mediated": a lowered blood pressure is lowered whichever route the body took.

The name "biofeedback" came into use at the end of the 1960s, and the Biofeedback Research Society was founded in 1969 (it later became the Association for Applied Psychophysiology and Biofeedback). Through the 1970s biofeedback was hailed as a way to cure almost anything psychosomatic, which was more than it could deliver. The next subsection sorts out what it actually does well.

Exam trap

"The technique based on the idea that the autonomic nervous system can come under voluntary control through operant conditioning" is biofeedback (JEMAS 2025), and the name linked to that idea is Neal Miller. The distractors come from other chapters: hypnosis works by suggestion in a state of focused attention (it was Charcot's and Freud's starting point, Chapter 3), sublimation is the Freudian defence mechanism that channels unacceptable impulses into socially valued activity (Chapter 3), and systematic desensitisation is classical counterconditioning (Section 4.2). Kegel's perineometer (1948) was an early form of biofeedback (NIMHANS 2026 recall), not aversion therapy, a token economy or desensitisation. In one line: biofeedback means learning to control a physiological process using real-time feedback from an instrument (MET mock), not dream interpretation, full-intensity exposure or medication.

The forms of biofeedback and what they treat

Each form of biofeedback is named after the signal it measures, and each signal suits particular problems (Table 4.20).

electromyographic biofeedback (EMG biofeedback) records the electrical activity of contracting muscles through electrodes on the skin, usually over the frontalis muscle of the forehead or the trapezius of the neck and shoulders, and feeds back a tone that falls as the muscle relaxes. Its flagship use is tension-type headache. In 1973 Thomas Budzynski, Johann Stoyva and their colleagues at the University of Colorado reported a controlled trial in which patients given frontalis EMG feedback had fewer headaches than those given false feedback or no treatment, and later meta-analyses have confirmed a moderate to large benefit. EMG feedback is also used for clenching and grinding of the teeth, jaw pain and chronic back pain, and in the opposite direction, to strengthen weak muscles after a stroke or nerve injury, where the patient learns to increase rather than decrease the signal. The same principle, applied to the pelvic floor, is the modern descendant of Kegel's perineometer, and pelvic floor biofeedback for some forms of incontinence and for constipation caused by poorly coordinated straining is among the best-supported uses of biofeedback.

thermal biofeedback (temperature biofeedback) uses a small temperature sensor, a thermistor, taped to a finger. Finger temperature reflects blood flow in the skin: when sympathetic arousal falls, the small blood vessels widen and the hand warms. At the Menninger Foundation in Topeka, Kansas, the research team of Joseph Sargent, Elmer Green and Dale Walters combined finger-temperature feedback with autogenic phrases ("my hands are warm and heavy") in a package they called autogenic feedback training, and in 1972 and 1973 reported that most of their migraine patients improved; according to the Greens' own account, the idea came from a volunteer whose migraine eased during a session in which her hand temperature rose sharply. Hand warming became the standard biofeedback treatment for migraine, and an often-cited meta-analysis by Kenneth Holroyd and Donald Penzien (1990) found relaxation combined with thermal biofeedback roughly as effective in preventing migraine as the drug propranolol. Thermal feedback was also the obvious choice for Raynaud's phenomenon, in which the fingers turn white and painful in the cold because their blood vessels clamp down. Early small studies were encouraging, but the large Raynaud's Treatment Study (2000), which randomly assigned 313 patients to sustained-release nifedipine, placebo, temperature biofeedback or a control form of biofeedback, found that temperature biofeedback reduced attacks no more than the control feedback did, whereas nifedipine reduced them by about two-thirds compared with placebo. (Some critics argued that the control biofeedback, which trained forehead relaxation, was not truly inactive.)

electrodermal biofeedback feeds back skin conductance, the ease with which a tiny electric current passes across the skin of the fingers or palm, once called the galvanic skin response (GSR). Conductance rises as the sweat glands, controlled by the sympathetic nervous system, become active, so it is a sensitive index of arousal; the client learns to bring it down, and it is used mainly in anxiety and stress management. (The same measure is one channel of the polygraph, the so-called lie detector.)

heart rate variability biofeedback (HRV biofeedback) works with a rhythm everyone has but few notice: the heart speeds up slightly with each in-breath and slows with each out-breath. When a person breathes slowly, at about six breaths a minute (the "resonance frequency" for most adults), these swings become very large and regular. Paul M. Lehrer and Evgeny Vaschillo developed a protocol around 2000 in which the client watches his heart rate rise and fall on a screen and adjusts his breathing to maximise the swing. It is used for anxiety, depression and asthma; a 2017 meta-analysis of some two dozen studies found sizeable reductions in self-reported stress and anxiety, although many of the studies were small. Notice how close this is to breathing retraining: here the instrument tells the client whether his slow breathing is doing what it should.

neurofeedback (EEG biofeedback) feeds back the electrical rhythms of the brain recorded from the scalp. Its clinical history began with an accident. In the late 1960s M. Barry Sterman, at the University of California, Los Angeles, had trained cats to produce the sensorimotor rhythm (SMR), a 12 to 15 cycles a second rhythm over the motor areas of the cortex. In a separate project testing the toxicity of a rocket fuel for the American space programme, the cats that had happened to receive SMR training turned out to be unusually resistant to the seizures the fuel caused. Sterman went on to train people with epilepsy, and in 1976 Joel F. Lubar and Margaret Shouse reported SMR training in a hyperactive child. Later protocols for attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) trained children to reduce slow theta activity (about 4 to 8 cycles a second) and increase faster beta activity, on the theory that inattentive brains produce too much slow activity. The evidence divides sharply according to who rates the outcome. In unblinded studies parents, who know their child is in treatment, report clear improvement. But when the European ADHD Guidelines Group, led by Samuele Cortese, analysed 13 randomised trials with 520 children in 2016, the effects were not significant on ratings made by people who probably did not know which treatment the child had received, nor in trials with sham feedback. Neurofeedback for ADHD is therefore not an established treatment; medication and behavioural parent training remain the first-line options (Section 4.10 describes parent training).

Table 4.20 The forms of biofeedback.
FormWhat is measuredWhat the client learnsMain uses (strength of evidence)
EMG (electromyographic)Electrical activity of musclesTo relax a tense muscle, or to activate a weak oneTension-type headache (good); jaw and back pain; stroke rehabilitation; pelvic floor problems (good)
Thermal (skin temperature)Finger temperature, reflecting skin blood flowTo warm the hands by lowering sympathetic arousalMigraine (good, with relaxation); Raynaud's (disappointing in the largest trial)
Electrodermal (GSR, skin conductance)Sweat gland activityTo lower general arousalAnxiety and stress (moderate)
Heart rate variabilityBeat-to-beat change in heart rateTo breathe at about six breaths a minute, maximising the rhythmAnxiety, stress, asthma (promising)
EEG neurofeedbackBrain rhythms (alpha, SMR, theta, beta)To increase or decrease particular rhythmsEpilepsy (some support); ADHD (weak in blinded trials)

Why does biofeedback work when it works? The obvious answer is that the client learns to change the physiological process, and the change relieves the symptom: lower forehead tension, fewer headaches. A pair of ingenious studies suggested that the obvious answer is at best incomplete.

Key study: Andrasik and Holroyd's misleading feedback (1980)

Question. Does EMG biofeedback relieve tension headache because patients learn to relax their forehead muscles? Method. Frank Andrasik and Kenneth A. Holroyd gave tension headache sufferers frontalis EMG biofeedback, and told all of them that they were learning to reduce muscle tension. In fact the feedback was arranged so that one group was rewarded for decreasing forehead tension, a second for keeping it unchanged and a third for increasing it. A fourth group only recorded their headaches. Result. All three biofeedback groups improved, by about the same amount and more than the monitoring group, although only the first had actually learned to lower its muscle tension. Conclusion. The headache relief did not depend on learning the specific physiological skill. In a follow-up study (1984) Holroyd and his colleagues manipulated what patients believed about their success, and found that patients led to think they were very successful improved more, whatever their muscles had actually done. Improvement seemed to come from cognitive changes: a growing sense of control over the headaches (self-efficacy, Section 4.7) and changes in how patients noticed and coped with the early signs of stress. Criticism. The findings apply to tension headache. Where the target is a specific, unfelt skill (contracting the right pelvic floor muscle, firing a weak muscle after a stroke) the physiological learning clearly does matter.

Put these findings together and a balanced verdict emerges. For headache and for general arousal, biofeedback works about as well as relaxation training, partly through the same routes (lowered arousal and a sense of control), and relaxation is cheaper. Biofeedback earns its extra cost in three situations: when the target is a specific process the person cannot feel at all (single muscles, the pelvic floor, the carbon dioxide level of the breath), when a client does not respond to relaxation alone, and when a client is sceptical of "psychological" treatment but finds a measured signal on a screen convincing. Modern equipment has also become cheap; sensors that link to a mobile phone now measure heart rate variability and skin conductance for a small fraction of what a laboratory instrument once cost.

Clinical link

A biofeedback display is a powerful teaching aid even when it is not the treatment. Many clients in Indian general hospital clinics present their distress as bodily complaints (headache, "gas," weakness, palpitations) and reject any suggestion that stress is involved. Attach a skin conductance or EMG sensor, ask the client to sit quietly, then ask about the worrying situation at home, and the line on the screen climbs within seconds; ask him to breathe slowly and it falls. The link between mind and body, which no amount of explanation had established, is now something he has seen for himself, and the rest of the treatment becomes far easier to accept.

Exam trap

Match each form to its classic use: EMG biofeedback to tension-type headache (and muscle re-education); thermal biofeedback to migraine and Raynaud's; electrodermal (GSR) to general arousal and anxiety; EEG neurofeedback to epilepsy and ADHD. EMG biofeedback is not a treatment for a bipolar depressive episode, acute psychosis or antisocial personality disorder (PYQ mock). Neal Miller's dramatic curarised-rat results failed to replicate (Dworkin and Miller, 1986), yet biofeedback in people survived on its own evidence. And biofeedback is not superior to relaxation for most stress-related conditions; its specific value lies where the target process cannot be felt without an instrument.

4.9 Habit Change and Self-ManagementMedium yield · 3 Qs

Relaxation and biofeedback still depend on a teacher, and often on an instrument. The methods in this section move the therapist further out of the picture. A nail-biter learns to catch his own hand on its way to his mouth; a depressed woman plans her own week; a student reorganises his own desk; an insomniac changes what he does with his own bed. In each case the client is taught to act as his own behaviour therapist, observing his behaviour, changing its antecedents and arranging its consequences, an approach that behaviour therapists call self-management. The section begins with the most structured of these methods, habit reversal, looks at two older techniques (negative practice and thought stopping) that turned out less well, and then follows self-management into three areas where it is now central: self-control, depression and sleep.

Habit reversal training

Everyone has a nervous habit or two: biting the nails during a tense cricket match, twisting a lock of hair while reading, cracking the knuckles, chewing the inside of the cheek. Most are harmless. For some people the habit becomes severe enough to damage the body or to embarrass them into hiding: bleeding fingertips, a bald patch hidden under a dupatta, a jaw sore from grinding. In 1973 Nathan H. Azrin (1930 to 2013), already known for the token economy (Section 4.5) and for overcorrection (Section 4.6), and his colleague R. Gregory Nunn asked why such habits persist when the person wants to stop them. Their answer had four parts. Nervous habits occur largely outside awareness: the hand is in the hair before the person knows it. They are the end of a chain of movements strengthened by thousands of repetitions, so that the habit runs off automatically once the first link begins. They are tolerated by other people, who rarely comment. And the person has no competing response, nothing ready to do instead. A treatment, they argued, should attack each of these in turn.

Key study: Azrin and Nunn's habit reversal (1973)

Question. Can nervous habits and tics be eliminated quickly by teaching people to notice them and to do something incompatible instead? Method. Azrin and Nunn treated 12 clients, adults and children, with habits that included nail-biting, thumb-sucking, lip-biting and tics, in a single counselling session of about two hours, followed by brief telephone contact. Each learned to describe and detect his habit, to anticipate it, to practise a competing response and to enlist the support of his family. They published the results as "Habit-reversal: a method of eliminating nervous habits and tics" in Behaviour Research and Therapy. Result. By the clients' own records the habits fell by about 95 percent after the first day and by about 99 percent after three weeks, and the gains held over the months of follow-up. Conclusion. A brief, structured training in awareness and competing responses could virtually eliminate long-standing habits. Criticism. The sample was small, the data were self-reports and there was no control group, so the near-total success was surely an overestimate. Later controlled trials confirmed that habit reversal works, although the effects are smaller than in that first report.

habit reversal training (HRT) is a multi-component behavioural treatment for repetitive habits and tics in which the client learns to become aware of each occurrence of the habit and of the urge that precedes it, and then to perform a competing response that is physically incompatible with the habit, supported by procedures that build motivation and carry the skill into everyday life. Azrin and Nunn's original package had about a dozen procedures, grouped under four headings (Table 4.21).

awareness training comes first, because a person cannot interrupt a habit he does not notice. In response description the client describes the habit in detail, often while performing it in front of a mirror. In response detection the therapist points out every occurrence during the session until the client is catching each one himself. The early warning procedure teaches him to detect the very first movement or sensation of the chain (a hand drifting upwards, an itch on the scalp), and situation awareness training identifies the places, times, people and activities in which the habit is most likely.

The second component, and the active ingredient, is competing response training. A competing response is a behaviour that is physically incompatible with the habit, which the client performs as soon as he notices the habit or the urge to do it, and holds for about a minute or until the urge has passed (Azrin and Nunn originally asked for three minutes). A good competing response meets several conditions: it makes the habit impossible at that moment; it can be held for a minute or more; it is socially inconspicuous, so that it can be done in a classroom or on a bus without anyone noticing; it can be combined with ordinary activities; and it usually involves a mild isometric tensing of the muscles opposite to those the habit uses, which also sharpens awareness. For hair-pulling or nail-biting the client might clench his fists, grip a pen or press his palms flat on his thighs. For a head-jerking tic he gently tenses his neck muscles with the chin slightly lowered; for an eye-blinking tic he makes slow, deliberate, soft blinks; for throat-clearing or a vocal tic he breathes slowly and rhythmically through the nose with the mouth closed.

The third component, habit control motivation, keeps the client using the competing response after the novelty wears off. In the habit inconvenience review he lists every embarrassment, cost and restriction the habit has caused. Family members are recruited for social support: they praise each use of the competing response and, by agreement, give a gentle prompt when they see the habit. In public display the client deliberately shows others the evidence of improvement, such as growing nails. The fourth component, generalisation training, uses symbolic rehearsal: the client imagines himself in each high-risk situation, noticing the first sign of the habit and performing the competing response, so that the skill transfers beyond the clinic. The original package also included relaxation training and self-recording. In 1998 Raymond Miltenberger, Wayne Fuqua and Douglas Woods reviewed the research and found that a simplified version with only three parts (awareness training, competing response training and social support) works about as well as the full package, and that simplified form is what most clinicians use today.

Table 4.21 The components of habit reversal (Azrin and Nunn, 1973), illustrated with Priya's hair-pulling.
ComponentProceduresIn Priya's treatment
Awareness trainingResponse description, response detection, early warning, situation awarenessDescribes the sequence in a mirror; notices the itch and the hand touching her crown; lists high-risk times (reading in bed, phone calls)
Competing response trainingAn incompatible, inconspicuous response held for about a minute or until the urge passesGrips a pen or clenches her fists for one minute at the first touch of her scalp
Habit control motivationHabit inconvenience review, social support, public displayLists the bald patch, avoiding hair-oiling with her mother, a wedding at which she could not wear her hair open; her roommate praises and prompts
Generalisation trainingSymbolic rehearsal in imagination; practice in real situationsImagines studying late before an examination and using the competing response

Priya, a 19-year-old science student in a Mysuru hostel, has pulled hair from the crown of her head while studying since her pre-university examinations; a bald patch the size of a coin is hidden by her plait. (The disorder, trichotillomania, is described in the Psychiatric Disorders book.) When she describes the sequence in front of a mirror she is startled by how long it is: her hand rises to her scalp, searches for a coarse hair, twirls it, pulls it out, rolls it between her fingers and sometimes brings the root to her lips. Her record shows about fifteen pulling episodes a day, almost all while reading in bed or talking on the phone. She chooses a competing response she can use anywhere, gripping a pen firmly in the hand that pulls, and practises it in the session. She stops reading in bed. Her roommate, briefed with Priya's permission, says "hands" gently when she sees the hand rising and praises her when she uses the pen. After four weeks the record shows two episodes a day, and the hair is beginning to grow back.

Habit reversal is the behavioural treatment of first choice for tics, trichotillomania, skin-picking, nail-biting and thumb-sucking, and it has been used for teeth-grinding and other repetitive behaviours. A close relative treats stuttering. In 1974 Azrin and Nunn described regulated breathing, in which the person who stutters learns to stop speaking at the first sign of a stutter, breathe out and then in, pause to plan the phrase, and begin speaking on the out-breath; the breathing pattern acts as the competing response.

Exam trap

Habit reversal training is primarily a treatment for tics and other repetitive habits (Real PYQ 2025). Stuttering is a tempting option because Azrin and Nunn did adapt the method for it (regulated breathing, 1974), but it is not HRT's main use. Obsessive-compulsive behaviours are treated by exposure and response prevention (Section 4.3), because compulsions are performed to neutralise anxiety, not out of habit; aggressive behaviour is managed with the operant methods of Sections 4.5 and 4.6. A good competing response is physically incompatible with the habit, inconspicuous and held for about a minute or until the urge passes (NIMHANS 2026 recall). The distractors describe other techniques: "an unpleasant consequence each time the habit occurs" is punishment or aversion; "the habit itself, performed deliberately and repeatedly until fatigue sets in" is negative practice (below); "a relaxation exercise practised once a day" is ordinary relaxation training.

Tics, premonitory urges and CBIT

Tics are sudden, rapid, repetitive movements or sounds (blinking, grimacing, shoulder-shrugging, sniffing, throat-clearing), and Tourette's disorder is the combination of several motor tics and at least one vocal tic lasting more than a year (the disorders themselves are described in the Psychiatric Disorders book). For most of the twentieth century tics were treated as purely neurological and therefore beyond the reach of psychology. Two observations changed that view. First, most people with tics, when asked carefully, describe a premonitory urge: an uncomfortable sensation (an itch, pressure or tension in the part of the body that is about to tic) which builds up and is relieved for a while by the tic. Second, tics are strongly affected by the situation: they increase with stress, tiredness, excitement and boredom, and when parents or teachers comment on them or tell the child to stop.

The first observation gives a behavioural model of tics that you have met before. If a tic relieves an aversive urge, then the tic is negatively reinforced by that relief, just as a compulsion is negatively reinforced by the relief of anxiety (Figure 4.4). The competing response then does for the tic what response prevention does for the ritual: it blocks the response while the urge rises, peaks and fades on its own, so the person learns that the urge is tolerable and passes without a tic. It is not surprising, then, that a Dutch trial in 2004 (Cara Verdellen and colleagues) found exposure to the urge with prevention of the tic roughly as effective as habit reversal. Clinicians long worried that suppressing tics would cause a rebound burst afterwards; experiments by Douglas Woods, Michael Himle and their colleagues in the 2000s found no such rebound.

The second observation, that tics vary with their antecedents and consequences, led to a function-based component. The Comprehensive Behavioral Intervention for Tics (CBIT) is a manualised treatment that combines habit reversal (awareness training and competing response training) with function-based interventions (identifying the situations and reactions that make tics worse and changing them), relaxation training, psychoeducation about tics for the child and family, and a reward programme for practice. It usually takes eight sessions over ten weeks.

Key study: CBIT for children with Tourette's disorder (Piacentini and colleagues, 2010)

Question. Does a behavioural treatment reduce tics in children better than a credible supportive treatment? Method. John Piacentini and a team of American researchers that included Douglas Woods, Lawrence Scahill, Sabine Wilhelm, Alan Peterson and John Walkup randomly assigned 126 children aged 9 to 17 with Tourette's or chronic tic disorder to eight sessions over ten weeks of either CBIT or supportive therapy with education about tics. Tic severity was rated by clinicians who did not know which treatment the child had received. Result. About 52.5 percent of the children given CBIT were rated much or very much improved, compared with 18.5 percent of those given supportive therapy, and most of the CBIT responders reassessed six months later had kept their gains. Very few children's tics worsened. Conclusion. A behavioural treatment produced improvements comparable to those reported for medication, without its side effects. A companion trial in adults led by Wilhelm (2012) found 38 percent much improved with CBIT against about 6 percent with supportive therapy. Criticism. About a third of the children were also taking stable doses of medication for their tics, and about half did not respond; CBIT reduces tics rather than curing them.

On the strength of these trials, clinical guidelines in Europe and North America now recommend habit reversal or CBIT as the first treatment to try when tics are troublesome enough to need treatment, before medication. For an Indian family whose child's blinking and grunting have drawn ridicule at school, and who have been told that "nothing can be done except tablets," this is important news.

Exam trap

CBIT stands for Comprehensive Behavioral Intervention for Tics; its core is habit reversal, to which it adds function-based interventions, relaxation and psychoeducation. Tell a tic from a compulsion: a tic relieves a sensory premonitory urge and is treated with habit reversal or CBIT, whereas a compulsion neutralises an obsessional fear and is treated with exposure and response prevention. And do not assume that tic suppression causes a rebound: the rebound effect belongs to the suppression of thoughts (Wegner, later in this section), not of tics.

Massed or negative practice

Habit reversal is not the first behavioural attack on habits. Forty years earlier, the American psychologist Knight Dunlap (1875 to 1949) of Johns Hopkins University proposed something that sounds like nonsense: to break a habit, practise it. Edward Thorndike's law of exercise held that repeating a response strengthens it, and Dunlap called this the alpha hypothesis. In a 1928 paper in Science, "A revision of the fundamental law of habit formation," and in his 1932 book Habits: Their Making and Unmaking, he proposed a beta hypothesis: under the right conditions, deliberate repetition of a response, made with full awareness and with the intention of getting rid of it, weakens it. His favourite example came from his own typewriter. He kept typing "hte" for "the," so he sat down and deliberately typed "hte" many times, attending to it as a mistake, and the error disappeared. He applied the same idea to stammering, thumb-sucking, nail-biting and tics, and called it negative practice.

negative practice (also called massed practice when it is carried out in long, concentrated sessions) is the deliberate, repeated performance of an unwanted habit or tic, usually for a set period such as several minutes at a time, in order to weaken it. The best-known theory of why it should work came from Aubrey J. Yates, who in 1958 treated a young woman with four distinct tics by having her perform each tic voluntarily, as fast and as often as she could, for long periods. Yates drew on Clark Leonard Hull's learning theory. Every repetition of a response builds up reactive inhibition, a fatigue-like state that makes the response harder to perform. When the person finally stops and rests, the reduction in fatigue rewards the act of not ticcing, so that a habit of not performing the tic (conditioned inhibition, in Hull's terms) is gradually learned. Yates's patient improved, though not completely.

The method has been used for tics, nail-biting, hair-pulling and stuttering, and occasionally for typing and spelling errors, but its results were modest and often short-lived. When Azrin, Nunn and Frantz-Renshaw compared it directly with habit reversal in 1980, in 22 people with tics, habit reversal reduced the tics by more than 90 percent and massed practice by only about a third; a parallel comparison for nail-biting also favoured habit reversal. Negative practice is now rarely used. It is worth knowing chiefly because examiners place it beside three techniques that look similar. Satiation (Section 4.6) presents a reinforcer in such abundance that it loses its value; negative practice repeats a response, not a reinforcer. Rapid smoking, in which the smoker puffs every few seconds until he feels sick, is closer to aversion therapy (Section 4.4). And paradoxical intention (Section 4.10) asks a person to intend or wish for the very symptom he fears, with humour, in order to dissolve anticipatory anxiety; it works on the fear of the symptom rather than on fatigue of the response.

Exam trap

Negative (massed) practice is Knight Dunlap's (1928; Habits: Their Making and Unmaking, 1932), with a Hullian rationale supplied by Aubrey Yates (1958). "An intervention in which the person deliberately does, or wishes for, the very opposite of the therapeutic goal in order to reach that goal" is paradoxical intention (Viktor Frankl; NIMHANS 2026 recall), not negative practice: negative practice repeats a habit to exhaust it, and has no "wishing" in it. Dereflection is Frankl's other logotherapy technique, which turns the person's attention away from himself (Chapter 6), and reciprocal inhibition is Wolpe's principle behind desensitisation (Section 4.2). If a habit reversal question offers "performing the habit deliberately and repeatedly until fatigue sets in" as the competing response, that option is negative practice.

Thought stopping, and why it is now discouraged

What can a behaviour therapist do about a thought? The earliest answer was to treat it like any other unwanted response and suppress it. thought stopping is a technique in which an unwanted, recurring thought is interrupted by a sudden command, "Stop!", first shouted by the therapist and then said by the client himself, aloud and later silently, usually followed by a deliberate switch to a pleasant or neutral thought. It was described in 1928 by J. A. Bain in a self-help book, Thought Control in Everyday Life; adapted for obsessional thinking in the 1950s by J. G. Taylor; and made widely known by Wolpe, who included it with Lazarus in Behavior Therapy Techniques (1966).

The standard procedure runs like this. The client closes his eyes and deliberately brings the unwanted thought to mind, raising a finger when it is clearly present. The therapist suddenly shouts "Stop!", sometimes banging the desk at the same time. Startled, the client usually finds that the thought has gone, and the demonstration is repeated several times. Then the client shouts "Stop!" himself, then says it in a normal voice, then whispers it, and finally says it silently, so that he can use it anywhere. Some therapists added a sharp snap of a rubber band worn on the wrist, and many added a substitute thought, a pleasant scene or a reassuring statement. The rationale varied with the theorist: the shout was a mildly aversive stimulus that punished the thought, or a distraction, or an interruption that broke an otherwise automatic chain of thoughts. It was used for obsessional ruminations, jealousy, worry, self-critical thoughts and cravings.

Thought stopping is simple, quick and intuitively appealing, which explains its long popularity in counselling manuals. The evidence never supported it. In a small controlled trial at the Maudsley in 1973, Richard Stern, Maurice Lipsedge and Isaac Marks found that thought stopping produced little improvement in patients with obsessional ruminations. The deeper problem was explained by the "white bear" experiment of Daniel M. Wegner and his colleagues in 1987, told as a key study in Section 5.9: students told not to think of a white bear kept thinking of it, and when the ban was lifted they thought of it more often than students who had never tried to suppress it. Deliberately pushing a thought out of mind tends to produce a rebound effect, a later increase in the very thought that was suppressed, and Wegner's later ironic process theory explains why suppression fails most under stress or mental load, which is exactly when people with obsessions try hardest. For obsessions there is a further cost. In the cognitive model of obsessive-compulsive disorder (Section 5.6), intrusive thoughts become obsessions because they are treated as dangerous and in need of control, and every attempt to suppress or neutralise them confirms that belief. Thought stopping teaches the client that the thought is dangerous and must be stopped, and the silent "Stop!" easily becomes one more covert ritual of the kind that defeats exposure and response prevention (Section 4.3).

Today, therefore, most clinicians teach the opposite. Obsessional thoughts are treated by exposure to them, through written scripts and recordings, with prevention of neutralising (Section 4.3); worries are handled by postponing them to a set worry period, a stimulus control technique described by Borkovec and colleagues in 1983 and later built into metacognitive therapy (Section 5.10); and acceptance and commitment therapy teaches clients to notice thoughts without fighting them (Section 5.9). A few clinicians still defend a brief "stop" as a cue to interrupt ruminative brooding and turn to an activity, which is close to the way behavioural activation treats rumination (below), but not as a way of getting rid of obsessions.

Clinical link

A devout 24-year-old man in Mysuru is tormented by obscene images of a deity that intrude during prayer. Each time, he silently says "Rama, Rama" and taps his cheek, and a counsellor has taught him to shout "Stop!" in his mind as well. The images are now more frequent than ever. Seen through this section, every one of these responses is a neutraliser that tells him the thought is dangerous. Treatment reverses the strategy: he writes the feared image into a short script, listens to it repeatedly without praying it away, and learns, often with relief, that such intrusions are common in people of deep faith precisely because they find them so repugnant.

Exam trap

Thought stopping: Bain (1928) first described it, Taylor adapted it for obsessions and Wolpe (with Lazarus) popularised it. Do not confuse J. A. Bain with Alexander Bain, the nineteenth-century Scottish philosopher of associationism. Thought stopping is a form of thought suppression, and suppression produces a rebound (Wegner, 1987, the white bear study); this is why it is now discouraged, especially for obsessions. It is not the same as cognitive restructuring (which examines the evidence for a thought, Chapter 5), Meichenbaum's self-instructional training (which guides action with self-statements, Chapter 5) or defusion (which changes one's relationship to a thought without trying to remove it, Section 5.9).

Self-monitoring, self-reinforcement and self-control

Section 2.8 taught self-monitoring as a method of assessment: the client records his own behaviour, thoughts or feelings as they occur, and the record is prone both to inaccuracy and to reactivity, the change that recording itself produces. Section 4.1 noted that reactivity is a nuisance for measurement but a gift for treatment. Here we use the gift. self-monitoring, used as a treatment, is the deliberate recording by the client of a target behaviour in order to change it, arranged so as to make the reactive effect as large as possible.

A neat early demonstration came from an American junior high school. In 1971 Marcia Broden, R. Vance Hall and Brenda Mitts gave an eighth-grade girl who rarely studied in her history class a stack of slips on which to mark, at intervals during the lesson, whether she had been studying. Her study behaviour rose as soon as the slips were introduced, fell when they were withdrawn and rose again when they were reinstated, the reversal design of applied behaviour analysis showing that the recording itself was responsible; once her teacher began praising her studying, the slips could be dropped without loss. In a second experiment an eighth-grade boy who constantly talked out of turn in his mathematics class recorded his talk-outs, and they fell. Today self-monitoring is the first component of most self-management programmes. A food record is one of the most consistent predictors of success in behavioural weight-loss programmes, smokers count cigarettes, drinkers keep a drinks diary, and a person who pulls her hair keeps a tally on a counter. The findings summarised in Section 2.8 tell the therapist how to make it work: record each instance immediately, record the urge before acting on it rather than the act afterwards, use an obtrusive device (a wrist counter is a constant reminder), set a goal, graph the results and look at the record in every session. And remember its limitation: reactive effects alone are often short-lived, which is why self-monitoring usually opens a treatment rather than being the whole of it.

Self-monitoring is the first step of self-control, and behaviour therapists had to explain how a person can control his own behaviour at all. Skinner offered a behavioural answer in Science and Human Behavior (1953). In self-control, he said, one response (the controlling response) alters the variables of which another response (the controlled response) is a function. A person who sets the alarm clock across the room so that he must get up to switch it off, or who leaves his credit card at home before visiting a shopping mall, is not exercising a mysterious willpower; he is arranging his own environment so that the behaviour he wants becomes more likely. The Greek hero Odysseus, who had himself tied to the mast so that he could hear the Sirens without steering towards them, is the classic example. In 1965 Israel Goldiamond applied the idea to a student who could not study at his desk. Goldiamond told him to use the desk only for studying; whenever he caught himself daydreaming, he was to get up and daydream somewhere else. Gradually the desk became a cue for studying and nothing else, and his studying increased. Remember that desk: Bootzin applied the same logic to the bed (below).

Frederick Kanfer, whom you met in Section 4.1 as co-author of the SORKC model, gave self-control its best-known psychological model in the early 1970s (Kanfer, 1970; Kanfer and Paul Karoly, 1972). In Kanfer's model of self-regulation, when a person's routine behaviour is disrupted or he decides to change it, he moves through three stages in a feedback loop. In self-monitoring he observes what he is doing. In self-evaluation he compares his performance with a standard or goal. In self-reinforcement he rewards himself (with praise, a treat or a privilege) when he meets the standard and criticises or punishes himself when he does not. Therapy can target any stage: teaching accurate monitoring, setting realistic standards, and arranging reasonable rewards. In 1977 Lynn P. Rehm turned Kanfer's model into a theory and a therapy for depression. Depressed people, he argued, monitor selectively (noticing failures and ignoring successes, and attending to immediate rather than long-term outcomes), evaluate themselves against perfectionist standards, and give themselves little reward and much punishment; his self-control therapy trains each of the three stages in turn.

In Behavioral Self-Control (1974) Carl E. Thoresen and Michael J. Mahoney argued that self-control is not a trait one has or lacks but a set of skills that can be taught, and they sorted the skills into two families. Environmental planning changes the antecedents before the behaviour occurs: not keeping sweets in the house, studying in the library rather than the hostel room, deleting the gaming application during examinations. Behavioural programming arranges consequences after it: self-reward, self-punishment and self-contracts. Self-reinforcement raised an awkward question. If the reward is freely available to the person who sets it, why would he ever withhold it from himself? Research by Bandura and others (Section 4.7) showed that people do hold themselves to standards, but self-rewards work best when they are backed by other people, for example through a deposit contract in which a friend holds a sum of money and returns it in instalments for each week the target is met.

Consider Arvind, a 22-year-old preparing in Mysuru for a postgraduate entrance examination, who sits at his books for nine hours a day and studies for perhaps two. His plan follows Kanfer's three stages and Thoresen and Mahoney's two families. He self-monitors with a log of 30-minute blocks, marking each as studied or lost. He plans his environment: his phone stays in another room during study blocks, and his desk is used for nothing but study (Goldiamond's rule). He evaluates himself against a modest standard, four focused hours a day in the first week, rising by half an hour a week. And he reinforces himself: he watches the highlights of the day's cricket match only after the day's target is met, which uses the Premack principle (Section 4.5), and his roommate holds back two thousand rupees, returning five hundred for each week in which the log shows the target met. Within a month the log shows five hours of real study a day, out of fewer hours at the desk.

Exam trap

Kanfer's model of self-regulation has three stages in a fixed order: self-monitoring, self-evaluation, self-reinforcement. Behavioral Self-Control (1974) is Thoresen and Mahoney's. Self-control therapy for depression is Rehm's (1977), built on Kanfer. When recording a behaviour changes it, the term is reactivity (Amity 2023), and it is reactivity that makes self-monitoring therapeutic; shaping, chaining and applied behaviour analysis are not the answer. Skinner's account of self-control distinguishes the controlling response from the controlled response.

Behavioural activation for depression

Behavioural activation applies self-management to depression. Its theory is told in full in Section 5.10, so a summary is enough here. In 1974 Peter M. Lewinsohn proposed that depression results from a low rate of response-contingent positive reinforcement, rewards that follow from one's own actions, which can fall because the environment offers few rewards, because the person lacks the skills to obtain them, or because few events are rewarding for him. Low mood then lowers activity, which lowers reinforcement further, and withdrawal is negatively reinforced because it brings short-term relief from effort, sadness and social demands. In their component analysis of 1996 Neil S. Jacobson (1949 to 1999) and his colleagues found that the behavioural component of Beck's cognitive therapy did as well on its own as the full package (Section 5.11). Christopher R. Martell, Michael Addis and Jacobson then set out the modern treatment in Depression in Context (2001), and in the Seattle trial led by Sona Dimidjian (2006), behavioural activation on its own did as well as antidepressant medication, and better than cognitive therapy, for the more severely depressed patients. Behavioural activation's place among the third-wave therapies, its acronyms for avoidance and India's Healthy Activity Program are in Section 5.10.

behavioural activation (BA) is a structured behavioural treatment for depression in which the client systematically increases his engagement in rewarding and meaningful activities and reduces avoidance and withdrawal, acting according to a plan rather than according to his mood. Its central instruction reverses the depressed person's natural rule. He waits to feel better before he does things; BA asks him to do things in order to feel better, working "from the outside in." A behaviour therapist delivers it in a sequence of steps.

  1. Rationale. The therapist explains the cycle of low mood, withdrawal and lost reward, and the plan to reverse it, so that the client sees activity as treatment rather than as a demand.
  2. Activity monitoring. For a week the client records what he does each hour, with a mood rating from 0 to 10 (and often ratings of mastery and pleasure), using the methods of Section 2.8. The record shows which activities lift mood and which, like lying in bed or scrolling the phone, deepen it.
  3. Values and goals. Client and therapist identify what matters to him in each area of life (family, friends, work or study, health, faith, leisure) and choose activities that serve those values, not merely activities that are pleasant.
  4. Graded task assignment. Activities are broken into small steps and arranged from easiest to hardest, so that the client begins with something he can almost certainly do; this is shaping (Section 4.5) applied to a whole life.
  5. Activity scheduling. Each activity is written into a timetable with a day, time, place and duration ("Tuesday, 5 p.m., walk to the vegetable market, 20 minutes"), because a specific plan is far more likely to be carried out than a good intention.
  6. Tackling avoidance and rumination. When a planned activity is skipped, client and therapist analyse what happened (the trigger, the feeling, the avoidance and its short-term pay-off) and plan an alternative. Rumination is treated as a behaviour, an avoidant one, and the urge to brood becomes a cue to turn to the next scheduled activity.
  7. Review and reinforcement. Each session reviews the schedule, praises what was done, solves problems with what was not, and gradually hands planning over to the client.

Mrs Lakshmi, a 52-year-old homemaker in Mysuru, became depressed after her only daughter married and moved to Bengaluru. Her husband works long hours, and she has stopped going to her Thursday bhajan group, stopped cooking anything but the simplest meals and stopped visiting her sister; most afternoons she lies down with the television on. Her activity record shows a mood of 2 or 3 for most of the day, rising to 6 on the two occasions when her neighbour dropped in and on the one evening she walked to the market. The plan begins there: a daily 20-minute walk to the market at five o'clock in the first week; two telephone calls to her sister in the second; the bhajan group again in the third, with the neighbour accompanying her the first time; and in the fifth week, teaching Carnatic music to the neighbour's daughter once a week, an activity that gives her a sense of mastery as well as pleasure. Her mood ratings rise week by week, and the activities, once restarted, are kept going by their own natural rewards, the conversation, the music, the company, which is the real aim of BA: to reconnect the client with reinforcers in his own life rather than to supply artificial ones.

Exam trap

Behavioural activation is best described as encouraging clients to engage in meaningful activities to counteract depression (JEMAS 2025). Analysing early childhood experiences is the psychodynamic approach (Chapter 3), relaxation training for anxiety belongs to Section 4.8, and hypnosis for symptom relief has nothing to do with it. The theory behind BA is Lewinsohn's low rate of response-contingent positive reinforcement; the evidence that it can stand alone comes from Jacobson's 1996 component analysis and Dimidjian's 2006 Seattle trial; the modern manual is Martell, Addis and Jacobson (2001). BA schedules activities to restore reward and reduce avoidance; it does not challenge the content of thoughts.

The behavioural treatment of insomnia

Insomnia might seem an odd problem for behaviour therapy. Sleep is not something a person does; it is something that happens to him, and the harder he tries, the less it happens. Yet the most effective treatments for chronic insomnia are behavioural, and they work by changing what the person does around sleep rather than by making him sleep. Section 9.4 lists the components of cognitive behavioural therapy for insomnia (CBT-I), gives the rules of stimulus control and works through a sleep restriction schedule for Ms Kavya. This subsection explains the learning theory behind those components and the model that ties them together.

Why does insomnia become chronic? In 1987 Arthur J. Spielman and his colleagues proposed what is now called the 3P model of insomnia (Figure 4.13). Predisposing factors make a person vulnerable: a tendency to physiological and mental hyperarousal, a worrying temperament, a family history of poor sleep. Alone they rarely cause insomnia. Precipitating factors push the person over the threshold: examinations, a bereavement, a new baby, an illness, a move to night shifts. Most people sleep normally again once the precipitant passes. In some, however, perpetuating factors take over: the things the person does and believes in order to cope with the bad nights. He goes to bed earlier to "catch up," lies in bed for hours trying to sleep, naps in the afternoon, drinks more coffee to get through the day, watches the clock, and starts to dread bedtime. These habits and beliefs keep the insomnia going long after the original stress has gone. The model's practical message is that treatment of chronic insomnia should target the perpetuating factors, which are learned behaviours and beliefs, and that is exactly what behaviour therapy is equipped to change.

The first behavioural treatment aimed at those habits came from Richard R. Bootzin in 1972. stimulus control therapy for insomnia is a set of rules that limit what is done in bed and in the bedroom so that the bed becomes once again a reliable cue for falling asleep quickly. Bootzin's analysis was operant. For a good sleeper, the bed, the bedroom and the usual bedtime are discriminative stimuli for sleep: lie down and sleep follows. For a poor sleeper the same stimuli have become cues for everything except sleep: watching television, answering messages, planning tomorrow, worrying about money, and above all lying awake in frustration. Through classical conditioning, too, the bed becomes a conditioned stimulus for arousal, which is why many insomniacs feel drowsy in front of the television and wide awake the moment their head touches the pillow, and why they often sleep better in a hotel or at a relative's house. The rules (go to bed only when sleepy; use the bed only for sleep and sex; if not asleep within about 20 minutes, get up and go to another room until sleepy again; get up at the same time every morning; no daytime naps) are Goldiamond's study desk applied to the bed. Each rule either strengthens the bed's link with sleep or stops new pairings of bed and wakefulness, so that the conditioned arousal can extinguish. The fixed rising time anchors the body clock, and the ban on naps preserves the pressure to sleep at night.

The second technique attacks a different perpetuating habit: spending too long in bed. Spielman, Paul Saskin and Michael Thorpy introduced sleep restriction therapy in 1987. They treated 35 people who had had insomnia for an average of 15 years by first restricting the time they were allowed to spend in bed to roughly the time they actually slept, then extending it in small steps as their sleep efficiency (total sleep time as a percentage of time in bed) improved. After eight weeks the patients fell asleep faster, woke less, slept more efficiently and rated their insomnia as much improved, and the improvements were still present in the 23 patients reassessed an average of 36 weeks later. Two processes explain the effect. The mild sleep loss of the first weeks builds up the homeostatic drive to sleep (the pressure for sleep that accumulates with time awake; the physiology of sleep is in the Physiological Psychology book), so that sleep comes faster and is deeper and less broken. And because the person no longer lies awake for hours, the bed stops being paired with wakefulness, which is stimulus control by another route. Sleep restriction makes people sleepy during the first weeks, so it is used with care, or not at all, in people with bipolar disorder (in whom sleep loss can trigger mania), epilepsy, or jobs that require them to drive or operate machinery.

Two further components complete the behavioural picture. sleep hygiene education, whose modern list of rules is usually credited to the sleep researcher Peter J. Hauri (1977), covers caffeine, nicotine, alcohol, heavy meals, exercise, light, noise and a regular schedule; it is sensible advice, but on its own it does little for chronic insomnia (the exam trap in Section 9.4). Relaxation training (Section 4.8) lowers the physiological arousal that keeps the poor sleeper awake. A third technique, paradoxical intention, in which the person lies in bed and tries to stay awake so that the anxious effort to sleep disappears, is Frankl's method applied to sleep and is described in Section 4.10. Combined with cognitive therapy for beliefs such as "If I don't get eight hours I won't be able to function," these components make up CBT-I, which major guidelines, including the American College of Physicians in 2016, recommend as the first treatment for chronic insomnia in adults, ahead of sleeping tablets. Table 4.22 sorts the techniques.

Table 4.22 Which insomnia technique is it?
TechniqueKey instructionPrincipleOrigin
Stimulus controlBed only for sleep and sex; get up if awake about 20 minutes; fixed rising time; no napsBed becomes a cue for sleep again; conditioned arousal extinguishesBootzin (1972)

Mr Venkatesh, a 61-year-old retired engineer in Mysuru, goes to bed at nine because there is "nothing else to do," watches the news on a television at the foot of the bed, lies awake until midnight thinking about his pension and his son's job abroad, wakes at three, and stays in bed until seven trying to get back to sleep. His sleep diary shows about five and a half hours of sleep in ten hours in bed, a sleep efficiency of 55 percent. The television moves to the sitting room. He goes to bed only when sleepy, at around half past eleven, and gets up at a fixed half past five; if he is awake for more than about twenty minutes, he goes to the sitting room and reads under a dim lamp until he feels sleepy. He gives up his afternoon nap. The first fortnight is hard, and he is sleepy in the afternoons, but by the fourth week he is falling asleep within a quarter of an hour and his sleep efficiency has passed 85 percent, and the window is widened by a quarter of an hour at a time.

Exam trap

"A man with sleep-onset insomnia is told to lie in bed with the lights off and try as hard as he can to stay awake" is paradoxical intention (NIMHANS 2026 recall; Section 4.10). The distractors are the other insomnia techniques of this subsection: stimulus control (Bootzin, 1972) tells him to get out of bed if he cannot sleep and to keep the bed for sleep and sex; sleep restriction (Spielman, 1987) limits his time in bed to the time he actually sleeps; dereflection (Frankl) turns his attention away from himself towards something else, rather than asking him to will the symptom. The 3P model's factors are predisposing, precipitating and perpetuating, and treatment targets the perpetuating ones. Sleep hygiene alone is not an adequate treatment for chronic insomnia.

Table 4.23 brings together the techniques of this section. What unites them is the move that gives the section its title: in each, the client learns to observe his own behaviour, rearrange its antecedents and consequences, and so become his own behaviour therapist.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. Progressive muscle relaxation, which teaches the contrast between tensing and releasing muscle groups, was developed by Edmund .
  2. The first of the six standard exercises of Schultz's autogenic training is the sensation of .
  3. Herbert Benson called the physiological opposite of the fight-or-flight response the response.
  4. In Öst's applied relaxation, the 20 to 30 second version triggered by everyday reminders is called relaxation.
  5. Neal Miller's experiments on curarised rats claimed that autonomic responses could be changed by conditioning, but they failed to replicate.
  6. Electromyographic biofeedback is best known as a treatment for headache.
  7. In habit reversal training, the inconspicuous behaviour that is physically incompatible with the habit is called the response.
  8. Knight Dunlap's technique of deliberately repeating a habit in order to weaken it is called practice.
  9. Wegner's white bear study showed that trying to suppress a thought produces a later effect.
  10. Bootzin's (1972) rule that the bed is to be used only for sleep and sex belongs to control therapy.

Answers: (1) Jacobson, (2) heaviness, (3) relaxation, (4) rapid, (5) operant, (6) tension-type (tension), (7) competing, (8) negative (or massed), (9) rebound, (10) stimulus.

4.10 Paradoxical Intention, Parents and ClassroomsMedium yield · 3 Qs

Most of the techniques in this chapter were built for a therapist and a client in a room. The therapist trains relaxation, arranges exposure, delivers reinforcement or teaches a competing response, and the client practises. This last technique section stretches that picture in two directions. It begins with the strangest instruction a behaviour therapist ever gives, an instruction to try to make the problem worse, and asks why such an instruction should work and what the evidence says. It then leaves the consulting room altogether for the homes and classrooms where children's problem behaviour actually happens. There the therapist rarely treats the child directly. Instead she teaches the adults who control the child's everyday antecedents and consequences, the parents and the teachers, to become the agents of change. In 1969 Roland Tharp and Ralph Wetzel called this arrangement the triadic model: a consultant (the psychologist) works through a mediator (the parent or teacher) to change the behaviour of a target (the child). Gerald Patterson's parent training and the classroom methods that came out of the applied behaviour analysis laboratories of the 1960s are the triadic model at its most successful.

Paradoxical intention as a behavioural technique

You met paradoxical intention in Section 6.5 as one of the two techniques of logotherapy. Its founder, the Viennese psychiatrist Viktor Emil Frankl (1905 to 1997), noticed that many anxious patients are caught in a vicious circle of anticipatory anxiety: a person blushes, sweats or lies awake once, fears that it will happen again, and the fear itself brings on the very thing he fears (Figure 6.4). Here is the definition again, because examiners lift it almost word for word. paradoxical intention is a technique in which the client is instructed to intend, wish for or deliberately try to produce the very symptom or outcome he fears, so that the anticipatory anxiety that keeps the symptom going is removed. Frankl described it in print in 1939 and gave his best-known English account in 1960. For him it worked through self-detachment and humour, capacities of the human spirit, and it belonged to a philosophy of meaning. Behaviour therapists had no use for the philosophy. What caught their attention was that the technique was brief, that it could be written down as a precise instruction, that it seemed to work on exactly the problems in which effort and self-watching make matters worse, and that it could therefore be put to the test.

Why should trying to produce a symptom make it go away? Look first at the symptoms Frankl chose. Falling asleep, passing urine, sexual arousal, a dry forehead and an unflushed face are all governed largely by the autonomic nervous system, and fluent speech, though under voluntary control, behaves in much the same way when a stammerer is anxious. They share a cruel property: none of them can be produced by an act of will, and the harder you try, the more the effort itself (arousal, muscle tension, anxious monitoring) gets in the way. A man who lies in bed straining to fall asleep is doing the one thing most likely to keep him awake. Behaviour therapists describe this as performance anxiety: anxiety about one's ability to carry out an act, which disrupts the very act it is about. Paradoxical intention reverses the demand. If the man's task is now to stay awake, he cannot fail at falling asleep; the effort and the fear of failure lose their object, arousal falls, and sleep, which never needed his help, arrives by itself.

Three familiar learning processes probably help as well. The client stops avoiding the feared symptom and in effect exposes himself to it, which allows habituation and extinction (Section 4.3). He discovers that the feared consequence does not follow (he stays in the meeting while trying to sweat buckets, and nothing terrible happens), which disconfirms his prediction in the way the inhibitory learning model recommends. And he gives up the self-monitoring that kept his attention locked on the symptom. On this account paradoxical intention is not a mysterious reversal of human nature; it is a clever way of removing the effort and avoidance that maintain a problem, packaged in an instruction that sounds absurd and is therefore memorable.

The behaviour therapist who did most to test the technique was L. Michael Ascher, a psychologist in the department of psychiatry at Temple University in Philadelphia, where Wolpe also worked. From the late 1970s Ascher and his colleagues applied paradoxical intention to three problems in which performance anxiety is central. The first was sleep-onset insomnia. Ascher and Jay Efran (1978) took clients whose sleep had not improved after weeks of a standard behavioural programme and simply changed the instruction: instead of trying to relax themselves to sleep, they were to lie in bed and try to stay awake. Their time to fall asleep dropped sharply within a few weeks. The second was psychogenic urinary retention (sometimes called paruresis or "shy bladder"), the inability to urinate in a public toilet when other people might be near. In 1979 Ascher reported clients who had not responded to other behavioural methods and who were now told to go into the toilet, take up the usual position and go through all the motions of urinating, but on no account to let any urine pass. Freed from the demand to perform, they improved. The third was the travel restriction of agoraphobia, in which clients were asked to go out and deliberately try to bring on the panic they dreaded, and found that panic does not come on demand.

Key study: Turner and Ascher's insomnia trial (1979)

Question. Is paradoxical intention a genuine treatment for insomnia or only an intriguing trick, and how does it compare with established behavioural methods? Method. Ralph M. Turner and Ascher randomly assigned adults with chronic sleep-onset insomnia to one of five conditions: progressive relaxation (Section 4.8), stimulus control (Section 4.9), paradoxical intention, a placebo procedure presented as a credible treatment, or a waiting list. Sleep was measured with nightly sleep diaries, including the time taken to fall asleep. Result. All three active treatments improved sleep significantly more than the placebo and the waiting list, and they did not differ significantly from one another. Conclusion. In a controlled comparison, paradoxical intention was as effective as the two best-established behavioural treatments of its day. Criticism. The outcomes were self-reported and the follow-up short, and later studies found the response to paradoxical intention more variable from client to client than the response to stimulus control.

Later research confirmed the picture and refined it. In Glasgow, Colin Espie and William Lindsay (1985) described six insomniac clients treated with paradoxical intention whose responses varied widely from one client to the next, and in a controlled trial in 1989 Espie and his colleagues compared stimulus control, paradoxical intention and progressive relaxation with a placebo and with no treatment for chronic sleep-onset insomnia. Only the active treatments produced significant improvement, but they helped in different ways (stimulus control, for example, improved the sleep pattern itself, whereas relaxation mainly improved how well patients felt they had slept), and the gains held at a follow-up 17 months later. In 2003 Niall Broomfield and Espie showed what the technique changes: clients given paradoxical intention reported much less sleep effort and much less anxiety about their sleep performance than untreated clients, exactly as the behavioural account predicts. Sleep-medicine reviews of the 2000s therefore listed paradoxical intention among the effective single-component treatments for insomnia, but with weaker support than stimulus control, sleep restriction or the full cognitive behavioural package for insomnia (Section 4.9).

Let us push one case through. Siddharth, aged 24, has spent two years in a rented room in Delhi's Mukherjee Nagar preparing for the civil services examination. For eight months he has taken an hour or more to fall asleep. He goes to bed at eleven determined to get seven hours, checks his phone at 11.20, 11.45 and 12.10, and calculates how few hours remain before the next morning's mock test. His two-week sleep diary shows an average of 80 minutes to fall asleep. The therapist first explains the circle: his wish to sleep is so strong that it keeps him alert, and sleep, like digestion, works best when nobody is supervising it. Then she gives the instruction, word for word: "Tonight, go to bed at your usual time, switch off the light and leave the phone in the other room. Lie in your usual position with your eyes open. Your task is to stay awake for as long as you can. Do not read, do not get up and do not do anything active to keep yourself awake; just lie there quietly and try not to fall asleep. Keep filling in the diary." Siddharth protests that he wants to sleep, not to stay awake, and the therapist agrees: that is exactly the problem. On the second night he reports, laughing, that he "failed" within about half an hour; by the end of the second week his diary average is 25 minutes. Had paradoxical intention not helped within two or three weeks, the plan was to move to stimulus control and sleep restriction, the first-line behavioural methods.

Notice how many different instructions a therapist could have given Siddharth, each resting on a different principle. Examiners love to describe one of them and offer the others as options, so Table 4.24 sets them side by side.

Table 4.24 Four instructions for a man who cannot fall asleep.
TechniqueAssociated withThe instructionHow it is thought to work
Paradoxical intentionFrankl; tested by Turner and Ascher (1979)"Lie in bed in the dark and try to stay awake as long as you can"Removes sleep effort and performance anxiety
Stimulus controlRichard Bootzin (1972)"Go to bed only when sleepy; if not asleep within about 15 to 20 minutes, get up and return only when sleepy; use the bed only for sleep"Re-establishes the bed as a cue for sleep (operant stimulus control)
Sleep restrictionArthur Spielman (1987)"Spend in bed only as many hours as you actually sleep, then lengthen the time gradually"Builds sleep pressure and consolidates sleep
DereflectionFrankl"Stop watching your sleep; turn your attention to something else worth attending to"Ends hyper-reflection, the anxious self-observation
Clinical link

Offer paradoxical intention with a clear rationale, a straight face and the client's agreement. Explaining why the instruction may help keeps the technique within the rules of informed consent and turns it into an experiment that client and therapist run together. It is unsuitable when the "symptom" is itself dangerous (self-harm, binge drinking, panic while driving) and, as logotherapists warn (Section 6.5), for severely depressed or suicidal clients. It works best for problems driven by anticipatory and performance anxiety: sleep-onset insomnia, shy bladder, blushing and sweating, stammering in feared situations and anxiety about sexual performance.

Exam trap

"The person deliberately does, or wishes for, the very opposite of the therapeutic goal in order to reach that goal" is paradoxical intention (NIMHANS 2026 recall). The distractors are close relatives. Dereflection (also Frankl) turns attention away from the self; it does not ask for the symptom. Negative practice (Dunlap) repeats a voluntary habit deliberately until it is weakened (Section 4.9, and below). Reciprocal inhibition (Wolpe) pairs the feared stimulus with an incompatible response such as relaxation. And the insomniac told to lie in the dark and try hard to stay awake is receiving paradoxical intention (NIMHANS 2026 recall), not stimulus control (getting out of bed when not sleepy), not sleep restriction (cutting time in bed) and not dereflection.

Paradox elsewhere: negative practice and strategic directives

Paradoxical intention is not the only treatment that tells a client to do the very thing he wants to stop. At least three other traditions use a paradox of some kind, and the differences between them, set out in Table 4.25, are a favourite source of exam options. The oldest is Adlerian. Alfred Adler "prescribed the symptom", and his follower Rudolf Dreikurs called the same move "antisuggestion" (Section 3.8): a child who sucks his thumb is told to suck it more often, so that the behaviour loses its purpose and its appeal. Frankl, who began his career in Adler's circle in Vienna, gave the idea its best-known form.

The second tradition is behavioural, and it is easily confused with paradoxical intention because it also asks the client to perform the problem on purpose. negative practice (also called massed practice) is the deliberate, repeated performance of an unwanted voluntary habit, many times in quick succession, until fatigue sets in and the habit weakens. Knight Dunlap described it in 1928 and, most fully, in his 1932 book, using examples such as the typist who keeps typing "hte" for "the" and is told to type "hte" deliberately, again and again. In 1958 the Maudsley psychologist Aubrey J. Yates used massed practice of a woman's tics, asking her to produce them voluntarily for long periods, and her involuntary tics declined. The usual explanation is Clark Hull's: massed repetition builds up fatigue (reactive inhibition), resting is reinforcing, and the organism learns a habit of not making the response (conditioned inhibition). Section 4.9 shows why habit reversal, which trains a competing response instead, has largely replaced it. The contrast with paradoxical intention is sharp. Negative practice works on voluntary motor habits and requires the client to perform them, many times; paradoxical intention works on involuntary, anxiety-driven functions such as sleep and urination, and its success usually shows itself in the client's failure to produce the symptom at all.

The third tradition comes from family therapy. Strategic therapists such as Jay Haley and Cloe Madanes, the brief therapists of the Mental Research Institute in Palo Alto and the Milan group led by Mara Selvini Palazzoli used paradoxical directives: instructions that prescribe the symptom (a quarrelling couple is told to argue every evening at eight o'clock for exactly twenty minutes), that restrain change ("go slowly; perhaps your family is not ready to change yet") or that describe the symptom as serving the family well (the Milan school's positive connotation). These directives are aimed at the pattern of interaction in a family system rather than at one person's anxiety, and they are often given without the rationale being explained. Some are compliance-based: if the couple obeys and quarrels on schedule, the quarrel has become voluntary and loses its force.
