Others are defiance-based: if the client resists the therapist's advice to stay the same, he must change to do so, and the therapist wins either way, a manoeuvre strategic therapists called a therapeutic double bind. Chapter 7 teaches strategic and Milan therapy properly; here the point is the contrast. A behaviour therapist gives paradoxical intention openly, as a reasoned instruction based on a learning account of performance anxiety, and measures what happens; a strategic therapist may give a paradox precisely because the family would resist a straightforward request.

Does paradox work, whatever its form? In 1987 Varda Shoham-Salomon and Robert Rosenthal combined the controlled studies in a meta-analysis and found that paradoxical interventions, taken together, were clearly better than no treatment and roughly as effective as other psychological treatments. The method is a tool, not a school, and its value depends on fitting it to problems in which effort, avoidance or resistance is the thing keeping the problem alive.

Table 4.25 Four ways of asking a client to do the thing he wants to stop.
ApproachAssociated withWhat the client is toldRationale shared?Proposed mechanism
Paradoxical intention (logotherapy)Frankl (1939)Wish for the feared symptom, with humourUsuallySelf-detachment and humour break anticipatory anxiety
Paradoxical intention (behaviour therapy)Ascher; Turner and Ascher (1979)Try to produce the feared outcome: stay awake, do not urinateYesRemoves effort and performance anxiety; ends avoidance
Negative (massed) practiceDunlap (1928, 1932); Yates (1958)Perform the habit deliberately, over and overYesFatigue and conditioned inhibition of a voluntary habit
Symptom prescriptionAdler; strategic and Milan family therapistsKeep or increase the symptom, often on a scheduleOften notChanges the family pattern or harnesses defiance
Exam trap

"Paradoxical intention" in an option list belongs to Frankl (logotherapy), even though Adler and Dreikurs used the move earlier and behaviour therapists tested it. "Paradoxical directives", "paradoxical injunctions", "prescribing the symptom" and "strategic paradox" in a family therapy question point to Haley, Madanes and the Milan group (Chapter 7), not to Bowen, Minuchin or Frankl. Massed or negative practice is Knight Dunlap's (1928; book 1932), applied to tics by Yates (1958); it targets voluntary habits, whereas paradoxical intention targets anxiety-driven involuntary functions.

Patterson and the coercive family

Why do some children become steadily more defiant and aggressive, while their brothers and sisters, raised in the same house, do not? In the middle of the 1960s Gerald R. Patterson (1926 to 2016), a psychologist in Eugene, Oregon, decided to stop asking parents about their children and go and watch. Trained observers visited the homes of boys referred by schools and courts for aggression and stealing, usually in the hour before dinner when everyone was tired and hungry, and recorded every exchange between family members in a detailed behavioural code, every few seconds. They did the same in the homes of ordinary families for comparison. Patterson and Elizabeth Gullion turned the early lessons into a programmed book for parents, Living with Children (1968); in 1977 Patterson, John Reid and their colleagues founded the Oregon Social Learning Center (OSLC), which became the leading centre for research on family processes and antisocial behaviour; and in 1982 Patterson gathered the theory into Coercive Family Process.

What the observers saw was not a household of cruel parents and wicked children. It was a household in which both sides had been trained, by each other, to behave badly. Patterson called the process coercion: the use of aversive behaviour (whining, shouting, hitting, threatening) by one person to make another person stop doing something. Its commonest form is the coercive cycle, shown in Figure 4.14. David Barlow and Mark Durand give the classic example in their textbook of abnormal psychology. A boy's parents ask him to make his bed, and he refuses. One parent yells; the boy yells back and becomes abusive. At some point the exchange becomes so unpleasant that the parent stops and walks away. The fight is over, calm is restored, and the bed is not made.

Look carefully at who is being reinforced. The child's escalation (step 2) is followed by the removal of an aversive demand (step 3), so the escalation is negatively reinforced, and next time he will escalate sooner and harder. The parent's giving in (step 3) is followed by the end of the child's aversive screaming (step 4), so the giving in is negatively reinforced too, and next time the parent will give in sooner. Both partners, in other words, are caught in what is often called a reinforcement trap: a pattern in which the short-term relief each one gets strengthens the behaviour that makes the long-term problem worse. As Barlow and Durand put it, the child learns to keep fighting and not give up, and the parent learns that the only way to "win" is to withdraw all demands.

Three refinements make the theory more powerful. First, parents do not give in every time. Sometimes they hold firm, sometimes they collapse, and so the child's coercion is reinforced on an intermittent schedule, which, as you know from the General Psychology book, produces behaviour that is extremely resistant to extinction. Second, the cycle escalates. When a parent does try to hold firm, the child raises the intensity, and the parent may in turn raise his own, until an explosive episode of shouting or hitting finally stops the child. That explosion is itself negatively reinforced (it worked), and it gives the child a vivid model of aggression. Patterson's observers saw this alternation of ineffective scolding (he called it nattering) and occasional explosive punishment again and again in the families of aggressive boys. Third, the same families tended to ignore the child's good behaviour and to attend, angrily, to his bad behaviour, so that deviance earned positive reinforcement in the form of attention while compliance earned nothing.

The coercive child does not stay at home. In 1989 Patterson, Barbara DeBaryshe and Elizabeth Ramsey described a developmental sequence that has become a standard account of how early conduct problems grow into delinquency: harsh, inconsistent discipline and poor monitoring at home produce a child skilled in coercion; at school his coercion brings academic failure and rejection by ordinary peers; rejected, he drifts in late childhood and early adolescence into a group of deviant peers, who reinforce rule-breaking; and by adolescence the risk of delinquency is high. (Conduct disorder itself is described in the Psychiatric Disorders book.) The clinical message is optimistic. If the problem was taught in the family by ordinary learning processes, it can be untaught in the family by the same processes, and the people best placed to do the teaching are the parents.

Exam trap

The coercive cycle runs on negative reinforcement, on both sides: the child's escalation is reinforced by the removal of the demand, and the parent's giving in by the removal of the tantrum. Do not call the parent's giving in "positive reinforcement of the parent", and do not call the child's tantrum "punishment". The option "impact of parenting on behavioural problems in young children" describes Patterson's coercion research; it is not a definition of the Premack principle, which is a technique of behaviour modification often used with children (high-probability behaviour reinforces low-probability behaviour, Section 4.5; IHBAS 2020).

Parent management training

parent management training (PMT, also called behavioural parent training) is a treatment in which parents are taught, by instruction, modelling, role-play and supervised practice at home, to change the antecedents and consequences of their child's behaviour, so that prosocial behaviour is reinforced and problem behaviour no longer pays. It grew out of Patterson's work at Oregon and was described in detail by Alan E. Kazdin of Yale University, whose own programme for children with conduct problems combined it with problem-solving skills training for the child. Kazdin summarised its defining features in a way worth remembering. Treatment is conducted mainly with the parents, and the therapist rarely works directly with the child. Parents are taught to identify, define and observe the problem behaviour in new ways. They are taught specific procedures based on social learning principles, above all positive reinforcement, mild punishment such as time-out and loss of privileges, and negotiation and contracting. Sessions give parents the chance to see the techniques modelled, to rehearse them in role-play and to review how they went at home. And progress is checked against data, not impressions.

A typical programme runs for a dozen or more weekly sessions and builds the skills in a deliberate order, shown in Table 4.26. It begins with tracking, because parents who are worn out by a difficult child usually report that he is "always" naughty, and a week of counting shows when, where and how often the behaviour actually happens (it is also the baseline, Section 4.1). It moves at once to the positive side: attending to the child and praising good behaviour, which many parents of difficult children have almost stopped doing. Only when praise and a simple reward chart are in place does it add discipline, and the discipline it teaches is mild, brief and predictable: planned ignoring of minor attention-seeking, a few minutes of time-out for aggression or defiance, and loss of a privilege for breaking an agreed rule. With older children it adds supervision (knowing where the child is, with whom, and when he will be home) and family problem-solving and contracts.

Table 4.26 The skills taught in parent management training.
SkillWhat the parent doesLearning principle
Pinpointing and trackingDefines one behaviour precisely and counts it every dayMeasurement and baseline (Section 4.1)
Attending and praiseGives warm, specific attention to good behaviour: "catch the child being good"Positive reinforcement
Point or sticker chartChild earns points for target behaviours, exchanged for privilegesToken reinforcement; Premack principle (Section 4.5)
Clear commandsGives one specific instruction at a time, as a statement, not a question, and waitsAntecedent control
Planned ignoringWithdraws attention from minor attention-seeking misbehaviour such as whiningExtinction (Section 4.6)
Time-outRemoves the child for a few minutes to a dull spot after aggression or defianceNegative punishment (Section 4.6)
Loss of privilegesRemoves a privilege or points after a rule is brokenResponse cost (Section 4.6)
Monitoring and problem solvingSupervises an older child's whereabouts; negotiates rules and contractsAntecedent control; contingency contracting

Notice what is missing from the table. PMT does not teach parents to use negative reinforcement; on the contrary, its whole design is an escape from the negative reinforcement trap of Figure 4.14. The parent stops withdrawing demands when the child screams, and so stops negatively reinforcing the screaming; the parent learns to tolerate the extinction burst that follows (Section 4.6), and so stops being negatively reinforced for giving in. Notice too that "ignoring" in PMT means ignoring minor behaviour that is maintained by attention. It is useless for behaviour that is maintained by escape, and dangerous for behaviour that could hurt someone.

Let us return to Arjun, the seven-year-old whose homework tantrums you analysed in Table 4.2. The functional analysis showed that his tantrums were maintained by escape from maths: each tantrum ended the demand. His mother, Kavitha, adds that he also whines and tugs at her whenever she is on the phone, and that his grandmother, who lives with them in Mysuru, often quietens him with a sweet. The two behaviours have different functions, so they get different treatment. In the first two sessions Kavitha tracks both and learns to give ten minutes a day of "special time", in which Arjun chooses a game and she simply watches, describes and praises. Then a chart goes up on the fridge: each ten-minute block of homework finished earns a star, and five stars on a weekday earn the choice of Sunday activity, usually cricket in the park with his father; cartoons come only after homework, which is the Premack principle at work. For the homework tantrums Kavitha learns escape extinction: she gives one clear instruction ("Write these five sums now"), keeps the work in place however loudly he screams, and calmly guides him back to the table; Arjun is given a card he can hold up once a session to ask for a five-minute break, an acceptable way of getting what the tantrum used to get. For the whining on the phone she learns planned ignoring, together with praise as soon as he plays quietly. Throwing a book earns three minutes of time-out on a chair in the hall.

The therapist warns the family about the extinction burst, and it comes on the fourth evening: the longest tantrum Arjun has ever had, which Kavitha survives only because she knew it was coming. The grandmother's sweets are the other hurdle. Rather than arguing with her, the therapist gives her a job: she is the one who puts the stars on the chart and praises him for them, so her affection now follows good behaviour instead of tantrums. By the sixth week the tantrums have fallen from about eight a week to one or two, and homework that used to take two stormy hours takes forty minutes. Nothing here required insight into Arjun's unconscious. It required the adults who control his consequences to change them, consistently.

The evidence for PMT is among the strongest for any psychological treatment of children. Controlled trials show reductions in oppositional and aggressive behaviour at home and often at school, and Patterson's group reported that untreated brothers and sisters of the referred child improved as well, because the parents' new skills spread to the whole household. Several limits are equally clear. PMT works best with younger children, up to about the end of primary school; adolescents need adapted programmes that bring in peers and school (Chapter 7 describes functional family therapy and multisystemic therapy). Families under heavy strain, with poverty, parental depression, marital conflict or a single parent without support, drop out more often and gain less, which led Kazdin to add attention to the parents' own stress and to the alliance between therapist and parent. In the United Kingdom, guidance from the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) recommends group parent training programmes for children aged about 3 to 11 with conduct problems.

Patterson's programme had many descendants. A parallel line began with Constance Hanf at the University of Oregon Medical School in the late 1960s, whose two-stage model first taught mothers to follow the child's lead in play (the "child's game") and then to give clear commands and use time-out (the "parent's game"); it shaped Rex Forehand and Robert McMahon's Helping the Noncompliant Child (1981) and Russell Barkley's Defiant Children (1987). Three descendants appear in examinations by name (Table 4.27), and you need only their outlines here (Chapter 10 returns to them in child clinical work).

Table 4.27 Three widely used descendants of parent management training.
ProgrammeDeveloperHallmark
Parent-Child Interaction Therapy (PCIT)Sheila Eyberg (from the 1970s, building on Hanf)Two phases: child-directed interaction, using the PRIDE skills (praise, reflect, imitate, describe, enjoy), then parent-directed interaction with clear commands and time-out; the therapist coaches the parent live from behind a one-way mirror through an earpiece; for children of about 2 to 7
The Incredible YearsCarolyn Webster-Stratton (University of Washington, Seattle, from the early 1980s)Group programme built on videotape modelling: parents watch and discuss short filmed scenes of parents handling everyday situations; linked teacher and child (Dinosaur School) programmes
Triple P (Positive Parenting Program)Matthew R. Sanders (University of Queensland, Australia)A public health system of five levels of increasing intensity, from media campaigns and brief advice to intensive family work
Indian context

Parent training in India must usually be family training. In a joint or extended family a grandparent, an aunt or an older cousin may control as many of the child's consequences as the parents do, and one adult who gives in can keep the coercive cycle alive for everyone. Mothers often attend alone because fathers cannot leave work, so practical plans (a phone call with the father, a Sunday session) matter. Threats ("the police will take you away"), shaming in front of relatives and physical punishment are still common, and many parents need to see a gentler method work before they will give up the slipper. Rewards should fit the household: extra time on the phone, choosing the Sunday breakfast, an outing with a favourite uncle. The principles do not change; the people and the reinforcers do.

Exam trap

"Which is not a part of parent management training?" Negative reinforcement (IHBAS 2020). Attending to positive behaviour, rewarding positive behaviour and ignoring undesirable behaviour are all core PMT skills; negative reinforcement is the trap PMT teaches parents to get out of. A related item (Amity 2023): a parent who deliberately ignores a child's temper tantrums to discourage them is using extinction, not operant escape or operant avoidance; the old key answer "operant escape" was wrong and has been corrected to extinction, and the full trap is in Section 4.6 under extinction. If anyone in that scene is learning by escape, it is the parent who gives in to stop the noise, not the parent who ignores it.

Behaviour in the classroom

A classroom is a small society with one adult, many children and a great many consequences, most of them delivered without anyone noticing. When the Journal of Applied Behavior Analysis began in 1968, a large share of its first studies came from classrooms, and the most influential came from the group of Wesley C. Becker at the University of Illinois. Their first discovery was uncomfortable for teachers. In one study the observers asked a teacher to stop praising a class that had been behaving reasonably well; disruptive behaviour rose. When she was then asked to increase her disapproving comments ("Sit down!", "Stop talking!"), disruptive behaviour rose further. In a related study in classrooms of young children, the more often the teachers told children to sit down, the more often the children stood up; when the teachers instead praised children who were sitting and working, standing fell.

The explanation is the same as in Patterson's homes. A reprimand is attention, and for many children attention is a powerful positive reinforcer, so a teacher who notices children mainly when they misbehave is reinforcing misbehaviour. Yet the teacher is herself reinforced for reprimanding, because the child usually stops for a moment, and that brief relief negatively reinforces the reprimand. Becker and his colleagues called this the criticism trap: the teacher's criticism works in the short run, which keeps her criticising, and backfires in the long run, which keeps the children misbehaving. The way out was tested directly.

Key study: Madsen, Becker and Thomas, "Rules, praise and ignoring" (1968)

Question. Which of a teacher's everyday tools actually control classroom behaviour: stating rules, ignoring misbehaviour or praising good behaviour? Method. Charles Madsen, Becker and Don Thomas worked in two primary-school classrooms, observing three children (two in one class, one in the other) whose behaviour was a problem. Observers recorded inappropriate behaviour at short intervals while the teachers introduced the three tools one after another: rules alone (stated and reviewed several times a day), then rules plus ignoring of inappropriate behaviour, then rules plus ignoring plus praise for appropriate behaviour. In one class the teacher then briefly returned to her old ways, and finally reinstated the full package. Result. Rules alone made little difference. Adding the ignoring of misbehaviour produced no clear improvement either. Only when praise for appropriate behaviour was added did inappropriate behaviour fall substantially; in the class with the reversal it rose again when the teacher returned to her old ways and fell when the package returned. Conclusion. Praise for appropriate behaviour is the active ingredient; ignoring works only when it is paired with reinforcement of the alternative. Criticism. The study involved three children in two classrooms, and ignoring cannot work when classmates, not the teacher, supply the attention that maintains the misbehaviour.

From this and later work came the basic package of praise and ignore: a few clear rules, stated positively and displayed ("Raise your hand to speak", not "Don't shout out"); frequent, specific and immediate praise for children who follow them ("Meena, you put your hand up and waited; thank you"); planned ignoring of minor attention-seeking misbehaviour; and firm, brief action for anything dangerous. Teachers are often advised to aim for several positive comments for every negative one. Even reprimands can be made less reinforcing: in 1970 K. Daniel O'Leary and his colleagues found that soft reprimands, audible only to the child concerned, reduced disruptive behaviour more than loud reprimands delivered in front of the whole class.

Praise and ignoring have a weakness, however, which the Key study's criticism points to. In many classrooms the most powerful reinforcer for clowning is not the teacher's attention but the laughter of classmates, and no amount of teacher ignoring can extinguish that. The solution was to make the classmates part of the contingency. A group contingency is an arrangement in which reinforcement depends on the behaviour of a group, or of particular members of it. Behaviour analysts distinguish three kinds: in an independent group contingency every child earns the same reward by meeting the criterion individually; in a dependent group contingency the whole class earns a reward according to the behaviour of one child or a few; and in an interdependent group contingency the reward depends on the performance of the group (or a team) as a whole. The most famous interdependent contingency is a game.

Key study: the Good Behaviour Game (Barrish, Saunders and Wolf, 1969)

Question. Can a classroom's own competitive spirit be harnessed to reduce disruption, without tokens or prizes bought from outside? Method. Harriet H. Barrish, Muriel Saunders and Montrose Wolf worked in a fourth-grade class in Kansas that included several children described as problem pupils. After observers had recorded baseline rates of two behaviours, talking out without permission and leaving one's seat, the teacher divided the class into two teams and announced a game. Every time any member of a team talked out or left his seat, a mark went up against that team on the blackboard. The team with fewer marks at the end of the period won, and both teams could win if each stayed under an agreed number of marks. Winners earned everyday privileges such as wearing victory tags, being first in line for lunch and extra free time. The game was introduced first in the maths period and later in the reading period, and was withdrawn and reinstated, so that each period served as a control for the other. Result. Talking out and out-of-seat behaviour fell sharply whenever, and only when, the game was in force. Conclusion. An interdependent group contingency using privileges the school already had produced large, reliable reductions in disruption. Criticism. One class, one teacher and a short study; and a team contingency can invite peer pressure on the child who keeps losing the game for his team, which the teacher must watch for (one remedy is to let a persistent offender form a "team" of his own).

The Good Behaviour Game became one of the most thoroughly tested interventions in education. From 1985 the psychiatrist and prevention researcher Sheppard G. Kellam and his colleagues ran a randomised field trial in first-grade classrooms in Baltimore, in which some classes played the game and others did not. The children were followed into adulthood. Among boys who had been highly aggressive in first grade, those who had played the game had lower rates of drug and alcohol use disorders, regular smoking and antisocial personality disorder as young adults than comparable boys from ordinary classrooms. Results of this kind led the prevention scientist Dennis Embry to call the game a "behavioural vaccine": a cheap, simple procedure that, given early, protects against later problems.

Three further tools complete the classroom kit, and Table 4.28 brings the whole kit together. A classroom token economy applies the principles of Ayllon and Azrin's ward programmes (Section 4.5) to a class: children earn points, stamps or stickers for specified behaviours, and exchange them for back-up reinforcers such as privileges, small items or free time. In 1967 O'Leary and Becker introduced such a system into a class of disruptive nine-year-olds; disruptive behaviour fell sharply, and the teacher was then able to stretch the interval between exchanges and rely increasingly on praise. Tokens are meant to be faded, because the aim is behaviour maintained by the ordinary rewards of school. Critics have argued that tangible rewards can undermine intrinsic motivation: in a well-known 1973 study by Mark Lepper, David Greene and Richard Nisbett, preschool children who had been promised a certificate for drawing with felt-tip pens later chose to draw less in free time than children who had not. The later meta-analytic debate suggests that the danger is real mainly when expected, tangible rewards are given for activities the child already enjoys. For behaviour the child does not yet perform, or for dull but necessary tasks, rewards combined with praise and then faded do little harm.

The second tool is response cost in the classroom: the child begins the period with a set number of points (five stars on a card taped to his desk, say) and loses one for each agreed rule-break, keeping what remains for a reward. It is the classroom form of the response cost described in Section 4.6 (a negative punishment), and it is often combined with points earned for good behaviour. It has been used particularly with inattentive and hyperactive children, who respond well to frequent, immediate consequences. Two rules keep it fair: the child should never fall below zero, and he should be able to earn points back by behaving well.

The third tool links school to home. A daily report card (DRC, also called a home-school note) lists three to five target behaviours for one child, each defined precisely ("Completes the written work in the maths period", "Follows the teacher's instructions with no more than one reminder"). The teacher marks each target yes or no, period by period, the child carries the card home, and the parents provide a reward that evening when an agreed number of targets has been met. The DRC exploits the fact that parents usually have much more powerful reinforcers than teachers do, and it keeps the two sets of adults talking. William Pelham and his colleagues made it a central part of the behavioural treatment of attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD), and it formed part of the behavioural arm of the large American multimodal treatment study of ADHD (Chapter 10).

Table 4.28 Behavioural tools for the classroom.
ToolWhat the teacher doesPrincipleAssociated with
Rules, praise and ignoringPosts a few positive rules, praises children who follow them, ignores minor misbehaviourPositive reinforcement plus extinctionMadsen, Becker and Thomas (1968)
Soft reprimandsCorrects privately and quietly rather than publiclyReduces the attention value of correctionO'Leary and colleagues (1970)
Good Behaviour GameEach rule-break puts a mark against the team; teams under the limit win privilegesInterdependent group contingencyBarrish, Saunders and Wolf (1969); Kellam's Baltimore trials
Classroom token economyChildren earn points exchangeable for privileges; exchanges are gradually spaced outToken (secondary) reinforcementO'Leary and Becker (1967)
Classroom response costChild starts with points and loses one per rule-breakNegative punishmentWidely used for inattentive, hyperactive children
Daily report cardTeacher rates a few targets each period; parents reward at homeHome-based reinforcementPelham and colleagues (ADHD)

Let us put the kit to work in an Indian classroom. Mrs Shobha teaches Class 4 in a government primary school in Mysuru. She has 46 children, one blackboard and a voice that is giving out by lunchtime. A trainee clinical psychologist from a nearby college spends two Kannada periods counting: in each 40-minute period there are about 50 call-outs and 30 instances of children leaving their seats, and Mrs Shobha reprimands about 25 times and praises about 3. That last pair of numbers is the criticism trap in plain figures. Together they draw up four rules, written in Kannada and English on a chart beside the blackboard: raise your hand to speak; stay in your seat during work time; keep your hands and feet to yourself; listen when someone is speaking. Mrs Shobha sets herself a target of at least four specific praises for every reprimand, and keeps a tally with a pebble moved from one pocket to the other.

Then the class plays the Good Behaviour Game. The two rows by the windows become team Kaveri and the two rows by the door team Kabini. Each call-out or seat-leaving by a team member earns a tally mark under the team's name, and any team with five marks or fewer at the end of the period wins: its members line up first for the mid-day meal, choose the song for the afternoon prayer and get five extra minutes in the games period. Within a week the children are policing their own rows, cheerfully. One boy, Mohan, keeps collecting marks against Kaveri, and the trainee suspects attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder and arranges an assessment (Assessment book). Meanwhile Mohan gets his own small team (so that his team-mates do not suffer for him) and a daily report card pasted into his school diary, which Indian parents are already used to signing: three targets, a tick or a cross for each period, and twenty minutes of cricket with his older brother after school on days with at least two ticks out of three. After three weeks the counts are about 12 call-outs and 8 seat-leavings a period, and Mrs Shobha is praising more than twenty times a period and reprimanding about five. To make the gains last, the game is then played on unannounced days and in unannounced periods, so that the children cannot predict when it will count, and praise carries more of the load.

Indian context

Section 17 of the Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act 2009 prohibits physical punishment and mental harassment of children in school. Many teachers were trained to rely on the cane and on public shaming, and a ban alone does not tell them what to do instead. Behavioural classroom management supplies the answer: clear rules, generous praise, group games and home-school notes are cheap, need no equipment, work in large classes and can be taught to teachers in a few workshops. For a clinical psychologist, consulting with a school is often the most efficient way to help many children at once.

Exam trap

The Good Behaviour Game is Barrish, Saunders and Wolf (1969), an interdependent group contingency (team-based); it is not Ayllon and Azrin's token economy (1968), which was built for hospital wards. In the classic classroom studies, ignoring alone failed; praise for appropriate behaviour was the active ingredient. A teacher whose frequent "Sit down!" makes children stand more often is caught in the criticism trap: her attention positively reinforces the misbehaviour, and the child's brief compliance negatively reinforces her criticism. A daily report card is a home-school contingency; classroom response cost, like time-out, is negative punishment, not negative reinforcement.

4.11 Evaluating Behaviour TherapyLower yield · not asked yet

In 1952 Eysenck demanded that psychotherapy prove itself, and he offered behaviour therapy as the treatment that would. That was a bold move, because it invited everyone to judge behaviour therapy by the same standard. More than seventy years later we can do exactly that. This final section asks four questions. What does behaviour therapy do best? Did the symptom substitution its critics predicted ever appear? Where are its limits? And are its methods, especially the aversive ones and the deliberate control of other people's behaviour, ethical? The tools for weighing therapy research (randomised controlled trials, meta-analysis, the criteria for calling a treatment empirically supported, the debate over common factors) are taught in Chapter 8; here we use their results.

What behaviour therapy does best

No school of therapy has been tested more often. As Richard Sharf observes, behaviour therapy's effectiveness has been studied with more populations and more disorders than that of any other approach, partly because its founders built measurement into the treatment itself. When a task force of the American Psychological Association's Division of Clinical Psychology published its first list of empirically validated treatments in 1995, the large majority of the well-established entries were behavioural or cognitive behavioural. Broad comparisons tell a more modest story: when Smith and Glass pooled 375 controlled studies in 1977, the different families of therapy differed little once the studies were made comparable (Chapter 8). The two findings are not in conflict. Across the general run of outpatient complaints, most well-conducted therapies help to a similar degree; for particular, well-defined problems, behavioural methods are often clearly the best available. An early head-to-head trial showed both sides of this picture.

Key study: Sloane and colleagues, psychotherapy versus behaviour therapy (1975)

Question. When experienced practitioners of each approach treat ordinary outpatients, does behaviour therapy do better than psychodynamic psychotherapy? Method. R. Bruce Sloane and his colleagues at Temple University randomly assigned 94 outpatients, most with moderately severe anxiety problems or personality difficulties, to about four months of behaviour therapy, short-term analytically oriented psychotherapy or a waiting list with minimal contact. The therapists were experienced and respected in their own approach. Independent assessors who did not know the treatment rated the patients' main target symptoms and general adjustment. Result. At four months both treated groups had improved significantly more than the waiting list on their target symptoms, and they did not differ much from each other; behaviour therapy showed some advantage on overall adjustment. By the one-year follow-up the differences had largely disappeared. Unexpectedly, the behaviour therapists were rated at least as warm, empathic and genuine as the psychotherapists, and patients in both groups named much the same things as most helpful, including the therapist's personality and being able to talk to an understanding person. Conclusion. Behaviour therapy was at least as effective as psychotherapy for general outpatient problems, and the relationship mattered in both. Criticism. The treatments were brief, the sample mixed, and a waiting list cannot be kept untreated for a year.

Where, then, is behaviour therapy at its strongest? Table 4.29 lists the main areas, with the section of this chapter in which each treatment is taught. Running through them is one pattern. Behaviour therapy does best when the problem is a specific, observable behaviour or response, when something identifiable in the present (an avoidance, a ritual, a reinforcer, a missing skill) keeps it going, and when the people around the client can change that something. Anxiety disorders fit this description almost perfectly, which is why exposure in one form or another is part of every evidence-based psychological treatment for phobias, panic disorder, social anxiety, post-traumatic stress and obsessive-compulsive disorder. For OCD, Sharf's summary of the research puts improvement with exposure and response prevention at 60 to 75 percent of patients, although only about a quarter are entirely free of symptoms at the end of treatment: a large effect, but not a cure for everyone.

Childhood problems fit the pattern too, because children live inside contingencies that adults control. The bell and pad remains the most effective single treatment for bed-wetting, applied behaviour analysis is the best-supported psychological approach to autism (the Lovaas programme's 1987 report that 47 percent of intensively treated children reached normal intellectual and educational functioning, against 2 percent of controls, is examined critically in Section 4.6), habit reversal is the leading behavioural treatment for tics, and parent management training is among the best-supported treatments for disruptive behaviour. In adults, stimulus control and sleep restriction form the core of the first-line psychological treatment for insomnia, contingency management produces some of the largest effects in the treatment of drug use, and behavioural activation, once dismissed as a mere component of cognitive therapy, matched antidepressant medication for more severely depressed patients in a 2006 trial led by Sona Dimidjian. In India the Healthy Activity Program, a brief form of behavioural activation delivered by lay counsellors in Goa, showed in 2017 that the approach can be taken to people who will never see a clinical psychologist (Chapter 9).

Table 4.29 Where behaviour therapy is strongest.
ProblemBehavioural treatmentThe evidence in briefSection
Specific phobiasGraded in vivo exposure; one-session treatmentMost patients much improved, often within a few sessions4.2, 4.3
Panic disorder and agoraphobiaInteroceptive and in vivo exposureAmong the best-supported psychological treatments for panic4.3
Obsessive-compulsive disorderExposure and response prevention60 to 75 percent improve; about a quarter symptom-free4.3
Post-traumatic stress disorderProlonged exposureOne of the best-supported trauma treatments4.3
Nocturnal enuresisBell and padThe most effective single treatment; some children relapse4.4
AutismApplied behaviour analysis; early intensive behavioural interventionLovaas (1987): 47 versus 2 percent; later replications more modest4.6
Tics and other habitsHabit reversal trainingA 2010 trial of a habit-reversal package helped about half of children with Tourette's disorder4.9
Disruptive behaviour in childrenParent management training; classroom contingenciesAmong the best-supported child treatments4.10
InsomniaStimulus control; sleep restrictionCore of first-line psychological treatment4.9
DepressionBehavioural activationMatched medication for more severe depression (2006); effective when delivered by lay counsellors4.9

The pattern also predicts where behaviour therapy is weaker. Diffuse problems with no clear behavioural target, such as a sense of emptiness, a confused identity or long-standing difficulties in every relationship, gave classical behaviour therapy little to grip; personality disorders resisted it until dialectical behaviour therapy combined behavioural methods with acceptance (Chapter 5). In schizophrenia, token economies, social learning programmes and social skills training improve self-care, social and vocational functioning (Paul and Lentz's study of 1977 is described in Section 4.5 and again in Section 9.8), but they do not replace antipsychotic medication, which remains the front-line treatment; behavioural methods are added to it, not substituted for it. And in conditions such as depression, where several bona fide therapies have been compared directly, the differences between behavioural and other well-conducted treatments are usually small.

Exam trap

Match the problem to the behavioural treatment with the strongest record. OCD: exposure and response prevention (IHBAS 2020; RML 2024), not systematic desensitisation alone. Tics: habit reversal training (Real PYQ 2025); habit reversal has also been adapted for stuttering, but the keyed "primary" use is tics, and it is not the treatment of obsessive-compulsive rituals or aggression. Autism: the Lovaas programme (RML 2022), not aphasia, depression or alcoholism. Depression: behavioural activation, which encourages clients to engage in rewarding, meaningful activity (JEMAS 2025), not childhood analysis, relaxation or hypnosis. And the front-line treatment for schizophrenia is antipsychotic medication (RML 2021); social skills training, stress management training and CBT are valuable additions, not the front line.

The symptom substitution debate

Of all the predictions made about behaviour therapy, the most confident came from its psychoanalytic critics. If a phobia, a tic or a bed-wetting habit is only the visible expression of an unconscious conflict (Section 3.2), then removing it directly is like plugging one hole in a leaking pipe: the pressure is still there and must find another way out. symptom substitution is the psychodynamic prediction that if a symptom is removed without the underlying conflict being resolved, the conflict will re-emerge in the form of a new, perhaps worse, symptom. The prediction followed naturally from Freud's hydraulic model of psychic energy, and it was the analysts' main objection to every technique in this chapter. Desensitising a phobia, on this view, was at best cosmetic and at worst dangerous, because it removed the patient's defence without touching the conflict it defended against.

Behaviour therapists answered in two ways. The first was Eysenck's flat denial, quoted in Section 4.1: there is no neurosis underlying the symptom, but merely the symptom itself, so getting rid of the symptom eliminates the neurosis. The second was subtler. In 1958 Yates pointed out in a paper on symptoms and symptom substitution that learning theory could itself predict the appearance of a new response in some circumstances. Clark Hull had described a habit-family hierarchy: an animal that has learned several ways of reaching a goal uses the strongest, and if that one is blocked, the next strongest appears. If a behaviour therapist suppressed one response while the drive and the reinforcer behind it remained, a different response serving the same purpose might well take its place. Whether new symptoms appear was therefore an empirical question, and its answer would not automatically confirm either theory. So the behaviour therapists went and looked.

The answer, study after study, was that symptom substitution rarely happened. In Gordon Paul's comparison of desensitisation and insight therapy (Section 4.2), the students whose public-speaking anxiety had been desensitised were followed up two years later (Paul reported the follow-up in 1967): their gains had held, there was no sign of substitute symptoms, and if anything some improvement had spread to other anxieties. The bed-wetting alarm offered an especially clean test, because enuresis was a favourite example of a symptom with a hidden emotional meaning, and analysts warned that a child made dry by conditioning would become anxious or disturbed in some other way. When the psychologist Bruce Baker compared children treated with the bell and pad with untreated children in 1969, those who became dry showed no new problems, and on measures of adjustment they seemed, if anything, happier and more confident than before. In 1982 Alan Kazdin reviewed the whole literature for the Psychological Bulletin. His conclusions have been the standard answer ever since. Evidence for symptom substitution is weak. When behaviours that were not treated change after treatment, they change for the better much more often than for the worse, a process he described as response covariation: treating one problem tends to send ripples of improvement through others, as when a child who stops wetting the bed starts going on school trips. And the hypothesis, as the analysts stated it, could hardly be tested at all, because it specified neither what the new symptom would be nor when it would appear; any new difficulty, years later, could be counted as a substitute, and its absence explained as "not yet". Figure 4.15 sets the two predictions beside what the evidence showed.

That qualification matters, and it is where the behaviourists' own logic did the most useful work. Sometimes a new problem really does appear. A boy whose head-banging is suppressed by a reprimand begins to bite his hand instead; a woman who stops checking the gas tap starts asking her husband whether it is off. Behaviour therapists explain such cases without any appeal to unconscious conflict. Behaviours that produce the same consequence form a response class, a set of different behaviours that serve the same function for the person. If the head-banging served to escape from difficult tasks (the kind of function Iwata's experimental analysis identified, Section 4.1), suppressing it while leaving the escape contingency intact simply makes room for another member of the response class. Reassurance-seeking is a member of the same response class as checking, which is exactly why exposure and response prevention blocks both (Section 4.3). The remedy is therefore not to dig for a hidden conflict but to ask what the behaviour was for, and to teach an acceptable alternative that serves the same function, as functional communication training, a form of differential reinforcement of alternative behaviour (Section 4.6), does. In this sense the symptom substitution debate ended with a draw that both sides could learn from: the analysts were right that a symptom often "means" something, and the behaviourists were right that what it means can be found in its present consequences.

Exam trap

Symptom substitution is a psychoanalytic prediction about what happens when symptoms are treated directly; it is not a behavioural technique, and it is not the defence mechanism of substitution or displacement. If it appears as an option for "practising relaxation while visualising situations of increasing intensity" (RML 2020), reject it: that is systematic desensitisation. The key names: Yates (1958) argued that learning theory could itself predict new responses through the habit-family hierarchy; Kazdin (1982) reviewed the evidence and found substitution rare and positive side effects (response covariation) more common. A truly new problem after treatment is best explained by a shared function (a response class), and prevented by functional analysis.

The limits: generalisation, maintenance and the cognitive critique

If symptom substitution was the critics' wrong prediction, generalisation was behaviour therapy's real weak point, and behaviour therapists were the first to say so. A change produced in one setting does not automatically appear in others. generalisation in this applied sense is the appearance of a treated behaviour change in settings, with people and in behaviours other than those in which it was trained; maintenance is its persistence over time after the treatment contingencies have been withdrawn. A token economy that transforms behaviour on a hospital ward may have little effect once the patient goes home, because home has no tokens. A child taught to greet people in the clinic may not greet anyone at school. In a follow-up published in 1973, Lovaas and his colleagues found that autistic children who had been discharged to institutions lost much of what they had gained, whereas children whose parents had been trained to carry on the treatment kept their gains and in some cases improved further. The lesson was that the natural environment has to be part of the treatment.

In 1977 Trevor Stokes and Donald Baer reviewed the applied literature and found that the commonest "technique" for producing generalisation was what they called train and hope: teach the behaviour in one setting and hope that it spreads. They described better strategies, and their list is still the backbone of good practice. Teach behaviours that the natural environment will go on reinforcing (praise from classmates, for example), so that the new behaviour is "trapped" by ordinary consequences. Train with several different examples, people and settings rather than one. Train loosely, varying the conditions, so that the behaviour does not become tied to a single cue. Make the contingencies indiscriminable, by moving from continuous to intermittent and delayed reinforcement, so that the client cannot tell when reinforcement has stopped. Include stimuli from the real setting in training (the teacher comes to the clinic, or training moves to the classroom). And teach the client to manage his own behaviour (Section 4.9), because a client who carries the contingencies with him takes them everywhere. Mrs Shobha's unannounced games and Arjun's grandmother putting the stars on the chart are both examples.

Maintenance raises a related problem. Several of the successes in this chapter are followed by a predictable proportion of relapses: fears return after exposure (spontaneous recovery, renewal and reinstatement, Section 4.3), some children start wetting again after the bell and pad, and many people treated for addictive behaviour relapse within months. Behaviour therapy's answers are practical rather than theoretical. Overlearning continues treatment beyond the first success; booster sessions are planned in advance; reinforcement is thinned gradually instead of being stopped; and in the addictions the cognitive behavioural method of relapse prevention teaches the client to anticipate high-risk situations and to treat a single lapse as a mistake to learn from rather than a collapse (Section 9.9).

The deepest criticism concerned the theory. Behaviour therapy had advertised itself as applied learning theory, and in 1965 the psychologists Louis Breger and James McGaugh argued in the Psychological Bulletin that this was largely a figure of speech: the "learning theory" invoked in the clinic was a loose borrowing of terms from animal laboratories, the laboratory principles themselves were disputed, and the techniques could not be derived from them in any strict sense. Some of the evidence in this chapter supports them. Systematic desensitisation works, but not because of reciprocal inhibition (Section 4.2); paradoxical intention works, but its learning account was written after the fact. Noam Chomsky's famous 1959 review of Skinner's Verbal Behavior had already argued that human language, and much else, cannot be explained by a history of reinforcement alone. And as early as 1967 Gordon Paul had reframed the whole outcome question in a sentence still quoted in every research methods course: what treatment, by whom, is most effective for this individual with that specific problem, and under which set of circumstances? (Chapter 8.)

The criticism that changed the field, however, was cognitive. Behaviour therapists themselves noticed that their clients' expectations, interpretations and self-statements kept getting in the way of, or doing the work of, the conditioning procedures. Albert Bandura showed that people learn by watching, without being reinforced themselves, and in 1977 he argued that all effective treatments may work by raising self-efficacy (Section 4.7 and Section 5.1). Arnold Lazarus, one of the founders, broadened his practice into "broad-spectrum" behaviour therapy in Behavior Therapy and Beyond (1971) and later into multimodal therapy (Chapter 5). Thoughts began to be treated as covert behaviours that could be conditioned, as in covert sensitisation (Section 4.4), and in the 1970s Michael Mahoney and Donald Meichenbaum announced the merger of cognition and behaviour modification. The result was cognitive behaviour therapy, the story of Section 5.1. It is worth noticing what did not happen. Behavioural techniques were not abandoned; they were absorbed. Exposure, activity scheduling, reinforcement, behavioural experiments and skills training are the working parts of almost every cognitive behavioural treatment, and the third-wave therapies of the 1990s (behavioural activation, acceptance and commitment therapy, dialectical behaviour therapy) brought radical behavioural ideas back to the centre.

A last criticism, that behaviour therapy is mechanical and cold, has fared worst of all. The Temple study found behaviour therapists at least as warm and empathic as psychotherapists, and every technique in this chapter depends on a client willing to do frightening or tedious things because he trusts the person asking him to. Exposure without a working alliance is a recipe for drop-out.

Exam trap

"Train and hope" is the problem Stokes and Baer (1977) named, not their recommended strategy. Generalisation is spread across settings, people and behaviours; maintenance is persistence over time; do not swap them. The famous question "What treatment, by whom, is most effective for this individual with that specific problem, and under which set of circumstances?" is Gordon Paul's (1967). Self-efficacy (1977) is Bandura's, not Skinner's or Wolpe's, and it is the bridge from behaviour therapy to CBT (Section 5.1).

The ethics of aversive methods and of control

Every therapy raises ethical questions, but behaviour therapy raises some of them in an unusually sharp form, for three reasons (the first two are stressed by Richard Sharf). First, the public has long believed that behaviour modification is something done to people against their will. Second, behaviour therapy is used with a wider range of people than any other approach, including infants, people with severe intellectual disability, autistic children and severely psychotic patients, many of whom cannot give informed consent; for them it is often the only suitable psychological treatment, and the therapist must decide on behalf of someone who cannot fully decide for himself. Third, behaviour therapy works by deliberately arranging consequences, sometimes unpleasant ones, in settings such as hospitals, residential schools and prisons where the client has little power. The ethical safeguards that grew up around it address each of these points.

The first safeguard is informed consent: agreement to a specific intervention, given voluntarily by a person who is competent to decide, after he has been told in a language and manner he understands what the procedure involves, its likely benefits and risks, and the alternatives, including the alternative of no treatment, and who knows that he may withdraw. Consent matters for every technique in this chapter (Section 4.3 showed why flooding is offered as a choice, never imposed), but it is essential for aversive methods. Where a client cannot consent, behaviour therapists seek partial consent where possible (a patient with schizophrenia who agrees to a programme during a period of lucidity), the consent of a parent or guardian, and in institutions the approval of an ethics or human rights committee that reviews the programme independently.

The second safeguard is the least restrictive alternative: the principle that a client should receive care and treatment in the least confining and least intrusive way that can achieve the goal. It came into mental health law through American court decisions of the 1960s and early 1970s, beginning with Lake v. Cameron (1966), which required courts to consider less restrictive alternatives to committing a person to hospital, and it was written into the standards for institutions by Wyatt v. Stickney (decided 1971; standards ordered 1972), the Alabama case that set minimum standards for state institutions and declared that residents had a right to the least restrictive conditions necessary to achieve the purpose of their habilitation or treatment. Applied to behaviour change, it produces a ladder of procedures (Figure 4.16; compare the four-level version in Figure 4.9, Section 4.6). Reinforcement-based methods come first; extinction, response cost and time-out come next; overcorrection and other mildly aversive consequences come later; and aversive stimulation such as electric shock sits at the top, where it is rarely if ever justified. A therapist may move up a rung only when the methods below have been tried properly and have failed, and each rung requires more safeguards: written consent, committee review, careful data, and a time limit after which the programme must be reviewed. The same case had a direct effect on token economies: the minimum standards it set turned many basic amenities (a comfortable bed, adequate meals, privacy, one's own clothes) into rights, which could no longer be used as reinforcers that a patient had to earn.

The least restrictive principle has a rival, and the tension between them is real. In 1988 a task force of the Association for Behavior Analysis, whose report was written by Ron Van Houten and his colleagues, published a statement on the right to effective treatment, arguing that clients are entitled to the most effective treatment procedures available, not merely the least intrusive ones. Imagine a girl with severe intellectual disability whose head-hitting has detached a retina. A slow reinforcement programme that takes months to work is less restrictive, but it may cost her the other eye. Some behaviour analysts argued that, in such rare cases, a brief, well-monitored aversive procedure that works quickly is the more ethical choice. From the late 1980s the positive behaviour support movement answered that the dilemma is usually false: when a thorough functional analysis (Section 4.1) identifies what the behaviour is for, and the child is taught another way of getting it, aversive procedures are almost never necessary. That view has largely prevailed. In 2020 the United States Food and Drug Administration banned electrical stimulation devices used to reduce self-injurious or aggressive behaviour, a ban aimed chiefly at one residential school in Massachusetts that had used contingent skin shock for decades; the ban has since been fought over in the courts.

The darkest chapter in the history of aversive methods was their use to change sexual orientation, described in Section 4.4. From the 1950s into the 1970s many people, most of them gay men, were given electric shocks or nausea-inducing drugs while looking at pictures of people of their own sex. Three ethical lessons came out of it. First, consent given under the pressure of stigma, family shame and criminal law is not free consent, however willingly the form is signed. Second, the goal itself was defined by social prejudice rather than by any harm to the client. Third, the methods did not do what was claimed: at best they suppressed arousal for a while, and they left many people depressed and ashamed. In 1973 the American Psychiatric Association removed homosexuality from its diagnostic manual, and in his presidential address to the Association for Advancement of Behavior Therapy, published in 1976, Gerald C. Davison argued that behaviour therapists should stop offering sexual orientation change programmes altogether, even to clients who ask for them, because offering them confirms the prejudice that homosexuality is a disorder. In India the Supreme Court decriminalised consensual same-sex relations in Navtej Singh Johar v. Union of India (2018), the Indian Psychiatric Society, which had stated in 2014 that there is no evidence that homosexuality is a mental illness, reaffirmed that position the same year, and after a 2021 order of the Madras High Court the National Medical Commission declared conversion therapy to be professional misconduct in 2022.

Behaviour therapists did not leave these questions to their critics. In 1977 the Association for Advancement of Behavior Therapy published "Ethical issues for human services", a set of questions that any therapist, of any school, can ask before starting treatment. Paraphrased, they are:

  1. Have the goals of treatment been adequately considered, and has the client taken part in choosing them?
  2. Has the choice of treatment methods been adequately considered, including less intrusive alternatives?
  3. Is the client's participation voluntary?
  4. When another person or agency (a parent, a court, an institution) has arranged the therapy, have the interests of the client been sufficiently considered?
  5. Has the adequacy of the treatment been evaluated, with data?
  6. Has the confidentiality of the treatment relationship been protected?
  7. Does the therapist refer the client to another therapist when necessary?
  8. Is the therapist qualified to provide the treatment?

Behind the particular questions about aversive methods lies a larger one: should psychologists be in the business of controlling human behaviour at all? The question was argued most famously by Skinner and Carl Ransom Rogers (1902 to 1987) at a symposium at the American Psychological Association's convention in 1956, published in Science the same year under the title "Some issues concerning the control of human behavior". Skinner's position was that behaviour is always controlled, by parents, teachers, employers, governments and advertisers, whether we admit it or not. Refusing to plan that control does not abolish it; it merely leaves it to accident and to people with worse motives. A science of behaviour, he argued, as he had imagined in his utopian novel Walden Two (1948), could design a culture in which people behave well because good behaviour is reinforced. Rogers granted the growing power of the behavioural sciences but asked two questions Skinner could not answer from inside his science. Who chooses the goals, given that the choice of goals is a matter of values that science itself cannot settle? And who controls the controllers? Rogers proposed instead that science could be used to free people: his own research suggested that when a therapist provides certain conditions, clients become more self-directing, more open and more responsible, and less in need of control.

Neither man converted the other, and they argued again at greater length in 1962. But the debate had a postscript that delighted behaviourists. In 1966 Charles B. Truax analysed recordings of one of Rogers's own therapy cases and found that Rogers did not respond to everything his client said with equal warmth: he gave more empathy and acceptance to some kinds of statement than to others, and those kinds of statement became more frequent as therapy went on. Even the most non-directive therapist, it seemed, was reinforcing selectively. The fair conclusion is not that all therapy is manipulation, but that all therapy involves influence, and the ethical question is whether the influence is open, agreed and in the client's interest. Skinner himself pointed out that the controlled person exerts countercontrol over the controller: clients drop out, complain, refuse and vote. He developed his views further in Beyond Freedom and Dignity (1971), which was a best-seller and an object of fierce attack.

The public image of behaviour therapy was shaped less by these arguments than by a novel. In Anthony Burgess's A Clockwork Orange (1962), filmed by Stanley Kubrick in 1971, the violent young Alex is "cured" by the Ludovico Technique: he is injected with a drug that makes him violently sick and, with his eyes held open, forced to watch films of brutality, until the mere thought of violence nauseates him (and, by an accident of the soundtrack, so does Beethoven's Ninth Symphony). In technical terms this is aversion therapy, a form of classical conditioning (Section 4.4), not operant behaviour modification. The prison chaplain's objection is the moral of the book: goodness that is not chosen is not goodness, and a person who can no longer choose has stopped being fully a person. The image fixed behaviour therapy in the public mind as brainwashing, and a few coercive American prison programmes of the early 1970s, which called themselves behaviour modification and were stopped after public protest and lawsuits, did nothing to dispel it. Yet the reality was close to the opposite. Aversive procedures were always a small and shrinking part of behaviour therapy. Most of the methods in this chapter rely on reinforcement and exposure, chosen with the client, and behaviour therapy's habit of writing down its goals, describing its procedures exactly and recording what happens makes it more open to scrutiny, and so to correction, than almost any other form of treatment.

Indian context

The Mental Healthcare Act 2017 (Chapter 1) turns many of these principles into law. It defines informed consent as consent for a specific intervention, given without force, undue influence, fraud, threat, mistake or misrepresentation, after the person has been told the risks, benefits and alternatives in a language and manner he understands; it deems a person to have capacity to make his own treatment decisions if he has the ability to understand the relevant information, to appreciate the reasonably foreseeable consequences of a decision, or to communicate it (Section 4; the Act joins the three abilities with "or"), and it allows advance directives (Section 5). Section 20 gives every person with mental illness the right to live with dignity and to protection from cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment, and lists among the rights of people in mental health establishments adequate food, privacy, proper clothing and facilities for leisure and recreation, so none of these can be turned into a reward to be earned in a token economy. Section 95 prohibits chaining "in any manner or form whatsoever", and Section 97 forbids seclusion and solitary confinement outright and allows physical restraint only when it is the only means of preventing imminent harm, never as a punishment or deterrent, which leaves no room for a locked, seclusionary "time-out" used as a behavioural consequence on an Indian ward. The Act also defines the least restrictive alternative (Section 2) and makes it a condition of supported (involuntary) admission that hospital care be the least restrictive option available (Sections 89 and 90). The Act defines a clinical psychologist as a mental health professional with a qualification recognised by the Rehabilitation Council of India, and the RCI's registration and code of ethics (Chapter 1) bind every practising clinical psychologist to these standards.

Exam trap

The least restrictive alternative came from American mental health law (Lake v. Cameron, 1966) and was written into the standards for institutions by Wyatt v. Stickney (1971 to 1972); the right to effective treatment statement is Van Houten and colleagues (1988). The Skinner and Rogers debate on the control of human behaviour was a 1956 APA symposium published in Science; Walden Two is 1948 and Beyond Freedom and Dignity 1971, both Skinner's. The Ludovico Technique in A Clockwork Orange is aversion therapy (drug-induced nausea paired with images of violence: classical conditioning), not token economy, shaping or systematic desensitisation. Davison (1976) argued against offering sexual orientation change, and India decriminalised same-sex relations in 2018 (Navtej Singh Johar).

Behaviour therapy in the balance

How, then, should we judge behaviour therapy? It helps to separate three verdicts, because the approach won one argument, lost another and changed the rules of a third.

On the evidence, behaviour therapy won. Eysenck asked whether psychotherapy could show that it did better than the passage of time, and behaviour therapists answered with the first psychological treatments that could be shown, in controlled trials, to beat placebo and alternative treatments for specific problems: exposure for phobias and panic, exposure and response prevention for obsessive-compulsive disorder, the bell and pad for bed-wetting, parent management training for defiant children, habit reversal for tics, applied behaviour analysis for autism. These methods have not been superseded. They have been absorbed, and they are the working parts of most of the evidence-based treatments in the chapters that follow.

On the theory, behaviour therapy lost several battles. Reciprocal inhibition was not the reason desensitisation worked. "Learning theory" in the clinic was often a vocabulary rather than a derivation, as Breger and McGaugh complained. Human beings turned out to learn by watching and to be changed by what they expect and believe, so that cognition had to be readmitted, and the merger produced cognitive behaviour therapy. Even the symptom substitution debate, which behaviour therapists won on the facts, ended with them conceding that a symptom has a function, and that its function, not its form, is what treatment must address.

On values, behaviour therapy changed the profession, and that may be its largest legacy. It insisted that a problem be defined so clearly that two observers could agree on it, that it be measured before treatment and during it, that treatment be described exactly enough for someone else to repeat it, and that a method which does not work be dropped, however elegant its theory. That discipline is now expected of every clinical psychologist, whatever his orientation, and it is the core of the scientist-practitioner identity described in Chapter 1. The ethical debates that behaviour therapy provoked, about aversive methods, about consent and about who controls whom, produced safeguards (the least restrictive alternative, the right to effective treatment, explicit questions about goals and methods) that now protect clients of every kind of therapy.

Its weaknesses are just as clear. Changes made in one setting do not travel to others unless they are deliberately taught to; problems without a clear behavioural target slip through its fingers; and, as Rogers warned, a technology of change says nothing by itself about which changes are worth making or who should decide. The best behaviour therapists answer these questions with the client, not for him. Behaviour therapy's great contribution was a single, practical question: what is keeping this problem going now, and what would change it? It is one of the most useful questions in clinical psychology. The cognitive therapists of Chapter 5 kept it, and added a second: what does the person make of what is happening to him?

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. Frankl's technique in which the client deliberately tries to produce the symptom he fears is called intention.
  2. In Turner and Ascher's 1979 trial, paradoxical intention for insomnia was as effective as progressive relaxation and control.
  3. Deliberately repeating a voluntary habit, over and over, until fatigue weakens it is Dunlap's method of practice.
  4. Patterson's account of how parents and children train each other to use aversive behaviour is called theory.
  5. In the coercive cycle, both the child's escalation and the parent's giving in are maintained by reinforcement.
  6. Parent-Child Interaction Therapy, with its child-directed and parent-directed phases, was developed by Sheila .
  7. A teacher whose frequent "Sit down!" makes children stand up more often is caught in the trap.
  8. The Good Behaviour Game (Barrish, Saunders and Wolf, 1969) is an example of an group contingency.
  9. The psychoanalytic prediction that a symptom removed without resolving its conflict will be replaced by a new one is called symptom .
  10. The principle, written into the standards for American institutions by Wyatt v. Stickney (1972), that treatment should be no more intrusive than necessary is the least alternative.

Answers: (1) paradoxical, (2) stimulus, (3) negative (or massed), (4) coercion, (5) negative, (6) Eyberg, (7) criticism, (8) interdependent, (9) substitution, (10) restrictive.

The case of Peter and the rabbit, revisited

Go back to the playroom of the Heckscher Foundation and to Peter, two years and ten months old, eating at his high chair while a caged rabbit crept a little closer each day. You can now name everything Jones did. Her first method, letting Peter play beside three children who handled the rabbit happily, was live modelling (Section 4.7). Her second, feeding him while the rabbit came closer, was counterconditioning (Section 4.2): eating was the response incompatible with fear, the kind of response Wolpe would later call antagonistic to anxiety, and Wolpe's shocked cats recovered in just the same way, fed first in a room unlike the laboratory and then in rooms more and more like the cage. Keeping the rabbit close enough to be noticed but never so close that Peter stopped eating was a graded hierarchy climbed in vivo, with each step taken only when the last was calm, which is the logic of systematic desensitisation. No wonder Wolpe called Jones the mother of behaviour therapy. The way Peter's fear had spread from a rat to a fur coat, feathers and cotton wool was stimulus generalisation, and the way his recovery spread to the same objects shows that treatment can generalise too. Even the setback fits the chapter: when a large dog jumped at him on his way home from hospital and his fears came flooding back, that was reinstatement, the return of an extinguished fear that the inhibitory learning model predicts (Section 4.3).

The rest of the chapter shows how far that playroom reached. Jones treated a fear as learned behaviour, watched what Peter actually did, and changed the conditions that kept the fear alive instead of searching for a hidden conflict; these are the assumptions set out in Section 4.1. Exposure, aversive methods, reinforcement programmes, modelling, relaxation, habit reversal and parent training each elaborate one of her moves, and the psychoanalytic prediction about cases like Peter's, symptom substitution, turned out to be rarely true (Section 4.11). What behaviour therapy added to her insight was careful measurement, controlled trials and an ethic of consent and the least restrictive alternative. What it learned along the way, from Bandura above all, was that what people expect and believe matters as well as what happens to them, and that lesson is where Chapter 5 begins.

Chapter summary

4.1 Where Behaviour Therapy Came From and What It Assumes. Behaviour therapy applies experimentally established principles of learning to psychological problems. Its forerunners were Watson and Rayner (1920), Jones (1924), the Mowrers' bed-wetting alarm (1938) and Dunlap's work on habits, but the spur was Eysenck's 1952 claim that about two-thirds of neurotic patients improved within two years with or without psychotherapy (spontaneous remission). It grew from three roots: Wolpe, Lazarus and Rachman in South Africa; Eysenck and Monte Shapiro at the Maudsley; and Skinner's operant tradition in the United States, which became applied behaviour analysis. It treats the problem behaviour as the disorder, explained by learning models such as Mowrer's two-factor theory, refined by Seligman's preparedness and Rachman's three pathways to fear. Every case begins with a functional analysis (the ABC model, Kanfer and Saslow's SORKC, Iwata's experimental functional analysis), a target behaviour and a baseline.

4.2 Counterconditioning and Systematic Desensitisation. Counterconditioning attaches a new response, incompatible with the old one, to the same stimulus, as when Jones fed Peter while the rabbit came closer. Wolpe produced anxiety in cats with shock alone, removed it by feeding them in steps, and stated the principle of reciprocal inhibition: a response antagonistic to anxiety, evoked in the presence of anxiety-provoking stimuli, weakens their bond with anxiety. His systematic desensitisation (1958) has three steps: relaxation training, an anxiety hierarchy rated on the 0 to 100 subjective units of distress scale (SUDS), and desensitisation proper, moving up only when an item evokes no anxiety. It can be done in vitro (imagined) or in vivo, and emotive imagery (Lazarus and Abramovitz, 1962) adapts it for children. Paul (1966) showed that it works, but dismantling studies pointed to exposure, not relaxation, as the active ingredient.

4.3 Exposure Therapies: Flooding, Implosion and Response Prevention. Exposure therapy is repeated or continuous confrontation with a feared object, situation, thought, memory or bodily sensation, without escape or avoidance, until the fear declines. It varies in intensity (graded or intense), medium (imaginal or in vivo) and duration; special forms are interoceptive exposure in Barlow and Craske's panic control treatment, virtual reality exposure (Rothbaum, 1995) and Öst's one-session treatment for specific phobias (1989). Four accounts explain why it works: habituation, extinction, Foa and Kozak's emotional processing theory (1986) and the inhibitory learning model, which advises dropping safety behaviours. Flooding (Malleson, 1959) starts at full intensity with no hierarchy and no relaxation; Stampfl and Levis's implosive therapy (1967) is imaginal, exaggerated and psychodynamic. Exposure and response prevention, introduced by Victor Meyer (1966) and refined by Foa, is the first-line psychological treatment for OCD, and Foa's prolonged exposure treats PTSD.

4.4 Aversive Methods, the Bell and Pad, and EMDR. Aversion therapy pairs an attractive but unwanted stimulus with an unpleasant one by classical conditioning: chemical aversion with emetine for alcoholism (Voegtlin and Lemere) or electrical aversion, which works poorly for drinking because, as Garcia and Koelling (1966) showed, tastes are linked readily to illness but not to shock. Aversion therapy for homosexuality, abandoned by its pioneer Kurt Freund and opposed by Davison (1974), is now condemned. Cautela's covert sensitisation (1966, 1967) pairs the behaviour with an imagined aversive consequence. The Mowrers' bell and pad (1938) treats bedwetting by classical conditioning, or by avoidance in Lovibond's account (1964), and is a first-line treatment beside desmopressin. Francine Shapiro's EMDR (noticed 1987, first study 1989) is a guideline-recommended treatment for PTSD, but dismantling studies suggest that its eye movements add little to exposure (McNally: what is new is not effective).

4.5 Operant Techniques That Build Behaviour. Reinforcement increases the behaviour it follows: positive reinforcement adds a stimulus and negative reinforcement removes one. Reinforcers are primary, secondary or generalised, and work best when immediate, contingent and consistent, with the schedule thinned later. Shaping reinforces successive approximations (Isaacs, Thomas and Goldiamond, 1960); chaining links the steps of a task analysis forwards, backwards or as a total task; prompts help a response occur and are then faded, as in Terrace's errorless learning (1963). The Premack principle (1959) uses a more probable behaviour to reinforce a less probable one.
