Ayllon and Azrin's token economy (from 1961) pays generalised reinforcers exchangeable for backup reinforcers, and Paul and Lentz (1977) found a social learning programme superior to milieu therapy. Contingency management (Higgins; Petry) rewards verified abstinence. Stimulus control and programmed generalisation, not what Stokes and Baer (1977) called train and hope, make change last.

4.6 Operant Techniques That Reduce Behaviour, and Applied Behaviour Analysis. Punishment weakens behaviour: positive punishment adds something unpleasant and negative punishment removes something pleasant, whereas escape and avoidance are negative reinforcement. Extinction withholds the reinforcer that maintains a behaviour (planned ignoring, escape extinction, Rincover's sensory extinction) and brings an extinction burst, a gradual decline and spontaneous recovery. Differential reinforcement (DRO, DRI, DRA, DRL) includes Carr and Durand's functional communication training (1985). Time-out and response cost are negative punishment, and Foxx and Azrin's overcorrection (1972) is restitutional or positive practice. Punishment's side effects make the least restrictive alternative the governing rule; Ayllon's towel hoarder illustrates satiation. Baer, Wolf and Risley (1968) defined applied behaviour analysis by seven dimensions, and Lovaas's Young Autism Project (1987) reported 47 percent of intensively treated children reaching normal functioning, against 2 percent of controls, a claim critics disputed.

4.7 Modelling, Rehearsal, Assertiveness and Social Skills. Bandura made observation central to learning: a model produces observational learning, inhibitory and disinhibitory, and response facilitation effects, helped by vicarious reinforcement, and Meichenbaum (1971) found coping models better than mastery models. Modelling may be live (Bandura, Grusec and Menlove, 1967), symbolic (Melamed and Siegel's Ethan Has an Operation, 1975), participant, covert (Cautela) or self-modelling (Dowrick). In Bandura, Blanchard and Ritter's study (1969) participant modelling beat symbolic modelling and desensitisation for snake phobia, which Bandura explained by self-efficacy (1977). Behavioural rehearsal practises new behaviour in role play (Wolpe and Lazarus, 1966). Assertiveness training runs from Salter (1949) through Wolpe (1958) and Lazarus to Alberti and Emmons (1970), who separated passive, assertive and aggressive behaviour; Manuel Smith (1975) named the broken record and fogging. Social skills training rests on Argyle's social skill model (1967) and, for schizophrenia, on Liberman's UCLA modules.

4.8 Relaxation and Biofeedback. Edmund Jacobson's progressive muscle relaxation (1929; 1938) teaches deep relaxation by tensing and releasing muscle groups; Bernstein and Borkovec (1973) wrote the abbreviated version most clinicians use, and some clients experience relaxation-induced anxiety. Johannes Heinrich Schultz's autogenic training (1932) uses passive concentration on six formulae: heaviness, warmth, heart, breathing, solar plexus and a cool forehead. Breathing retraining corrects hyperventilation (Clark, Salkovskis and Chalkley, 1985), though Schmidt (2000) found that it added nothing to CBT for panic. Benson's relaxation response (1975) is the opposite of Cannon's fight-or-flight response. Öst's applied relaxation (1987) trains clients to relax within 20 to 30 seconds and apply the skill at the first signs of anxiety. Biofeedback, foreshadowed by Kegel's perineometer (1948) and by Neal Miller's curarised rats (later unreplicated), feeds back muscle tension (EMG, for tension-type headache), skin temperature (migraine), skin conductance, heart rate variability or brain rhythms (neurofeedback).

4.9 Habit Change and Self-Management. Azrin and Nunn's habit reversal training (1973) combines awareness training, a competing response that is physically incompatible with the habit and held for about a minute, habit control motivation and generalisation training; it is the first behavioural treatment for tics, hair-pulling and nail-biting, and regulated breathing (1974) adapts it for stuttering. CBIT adds function-based interventions (Piacentini and colleagues, 2010). Dunlap's negative or massed practice (1928, 1932), given a Hullian rationale by Yates (1958), and thought stopping, now discouraged because suppression produces a rebound (Wegner, 1987), are older methods. Self-monitoring uses reactivity, and Kanfer's self-regulation runs from self-monitoring to self-evaluation to self-reinforcement. Behavioural activation, from Lewinsohn's low response-contingent positive reinforcement (1974) to Martell, Addis and Jacobson (2001), schedules rewarding activity for depression. For insomnia, Spielman's 3P model (1987) targets perpetuating factors with Bootzin's stimulus control (1972) and sleep restriction.

4.10 Paradoxical Intention, Parents and Classrooms. In Tharp and Wetzel's triadic model (1969) a consultant works through a parent or teacher to change a child's behaviour. Frankl's paradoxical intention (1939) asks the client to wish for or try to produce the feared symptom, removing anticipatory and performance anxiety; Turner and Ascher (1979) tested it for insomnia. It differs from Dunlap's negative practice and from the paradoxical directives of strategic family therapy. Patterson's coercion theory shows how the coercive cycle negatively reinforces both child and parent, and his Oregon work led to parent management training (praise, point charts, planned ignoring, time-out, response cost) and to descendants such as Eyberg's PCIT, Webster-Stratton's Incredible Years and Sanders's Triple P. Becker's classroom group described the criticism trap and showed praise to be the active ingredient (Madsen, Becker and Thomas, 1968); the Good Behaviour Game (1969), token systems and daily report cards complete the kit.

4.11 Evaluating Behaviour Therapy. Behaviour therapy has been tested more than any other school: most well-established entries on the first list of empirically validated treatments (1995) were behavioural or cognitive behavioural, and Sloane and colleagues (1975) found it at least as effective as psychotherapy for outpatients. It is strongest for phobias, panic, OCD, PTSD, enuresis, autism, tics, disruptive behaviour, insomnia and depression, and it does not replace antipsychotic medication in schizophrenia. The psychoanalytic prediction of symptom substitution rarely came true (Baker, 1969; Kazdin, 1982), though a shared response class can explain new problems. Its real weak points were generalisation and maintenance (train and hope) and its theory (Breger and McGaugh, 1965; Bandura's self-efficacy). Aversive methods are governed by informed consent, the least restrictive alternative (Wyatt v. Stickney) and the right to effective treatment (Van Houten, 1988), and Skinner and Rogers debated the control of behaviour in 1956.

From the past papers

  1. Behavioural therapies applying learning principles to: (RML 2020)

    (a) identify and treat the underlying causes of dysfunction (b) improve learning and insight (c) eliminate the unwanted behaviour (d) improve communication and social sensitivity

    Answer: (c) Eliminate the unwanted behaviour. Behaviour therapy applies learning principles to remove problem behaviour and build adaptive behaviour, treating the behaviour itself as the problem. Identifying and treating underlying causes is the psychodynamic aim, improving learning and insight describes the insight therapies, and improving communication and social sensitivity describes encounter and communication groups.

  2. The process of recording a behaviour causes the behaviour to change, even before any treatment is implemented. This is known as: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Reactivity (b) Shaping (c) Applied Behaviour Analysis (d) Chaining

    Answer: (a) Reactivity. Observing or recording a behaviour alters the behaviour itself, which is why a baseline can shift and why self-monitoring works as a treatment. Shaping and chaining are procedures for building behaviour, and applied behaviour analysis is a whole discipline, not an effect of recording.

  3. Counter conditioning is an exposure therapy technique that involves: (RML 2022)

    (a) Learning that one negative event may be linked to another (b) Exposing the client to the feared stimulus (c) Learning that an event or situation is no longer threatening (d) Helping the client to see that their behaviour is counterproductive

    Answer: (c) Learning that an event or situation is no longer threatening. Counterconditioning attaches a new, calm response to the feared stimulus, as when Peter ate while the rabbit came closer, so the situation stops signalling threat. Exposure (b) is part of it but describes every exposure therapy and leaves out the incompatible response; (a) is how a fear is acquired in the first place; (d) is an insight or motivational aim.

  4. The behavioural technique for reducing anxiety in which patients practice relaxation while visualizing provoking situations of increasing intensity refers: (RML 2020)

    (a) Symptom substitution (b) Flooding (c) Systematic desensitization (d) Token economy

    Answer: (c) Systematic desensitization. Relaxation paired with imagined scenes of increasing intensity is Wolpe's systematic desensitisation. Flooding starts at full intensity with no relaxation, a token economy is operant, and symptom substitution is not a technique at all but the psychoanalytic prediction that a symptom removed without resolving its conflict will be replaced by another.

  5. Subjective unit of distress (SUD) is mostly used under: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Exposure & response prevention (b) Multi-model therapy (c) Mindfulness-based stress management (d) Systematic desensitization

    Answer: (d) Systematic desensitization. Wolpe introduced the 0 to 100 SUDS scale to rate the items of the anxiety hierarchy in systematic desensitisation. Modern exposure therapies, ERP included, also use SUDS ratings, which makes (a) tempting, but the scale was built for desensitisation; multimodal therapy is Arnold Lazarus's BASIC ID approach and mindfulness-based stress reduction is Kabat-Zinn's.

  6. A client with a phobia of heights (acrophobia) is encouraged by their therapist to stand on a balcony for an extended period without any gradual exposure or relaxation techniques until their anxiety subsides. This intervention is an example of: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Systematic Desensitization (b) Flooding (c) Modeling (d) Cognitive Restructuring

    Answer: (b) Flooding. Prolonged exposure to the most feared situation at full intensity, with escape prevented and no hierarchy or relaxation, until the anxiety subsides, is flooding. Systematic desensitisation climbs a hierarchy while relaxed, modelling is learning by watching someone else, and cognitive restructuring changes beliefs rather than exposing the client to anything.

  7. Exposure and response prevention procedure is based on behaviour therapy principal of : (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Counter conditioning (b) Habituation and extinction (c) Reciprocal inhibition (d) Graded exposure

    Answer: (b) Habituation and extinction. In ERP the anxiety provoked by an obsessional trigger is allowed to fall by itself while the rituals are blocked, so the method rests on habituation and extinction. No incompatible response such as relaxation is trained, so counterconditioning and reciprocal inhibition are wrong, and graded exposure is the format of ERP hierarchies, not their principle.

  8. Which behavioral technique involves imagining a scene where an undesirable behavior is paired with an unpleasant consequence to reduce the behavior? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Flooding (b) Implosion (c) Covert sensitization (d) Systematic desensitization

    Answer: (c) Covert sensitization. Cautela's covert sensitisation pairs an imagined unwanted behaviour with an imagined aversive consequence, to create an aversion. Flooding, implosion and systematic desensitisation are exposure methods that remove a fear; implosion is also imaginal and unpleasant, which makes it the tempting option, but it aims to extinguish a fear, not to build an aversion.

  9. Eye movement desensitisation and reprocessing (EMDR) is sometimes used to treat PTSD. This technique involves: (RML 2021)

    (a) The administration of an antidepressant. (b) The client focusing on the memory, emotions and thoughts of the traumatic events while engaging in therapist-guided eye movements (c) Training the client in relaxation techniques. (d) The client being asked to relieve the traumatic event in their imagination

    Answer: (b) Focusing on the traumatic memory while making therapist-guided eye movements. In EMDR the client holds the traumatic memory, with its emotions and thoughts, in mind while following the therapist's fingers back and forth, Francine Shapiro's bilateral stimulation. Reliving the event in imagination, (d), is the imaginal exposure of Foa's prolonged exposure; EMDR involves no antidepressant, and relaxation training alone is not EMDR.

  10. In operant conditioning, the process of reinforcing successive approximations of a desired behavior until the target behavior is achieved is known as: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Chaining (b) Shaping (c) Fading (d) Prompting

    Answer: (b) Shaping. Reinforcing responses that resemble the target more and more closely, until the target itself appears, is shaping. Chaining links a sequence of separate steps, prompting adds a cue before or during the response, and fading gradually removes that cue.

  11. According to the Premack Principle, a high-probability behavior (for example, playing video games) can be used to: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Reinforce a low-probability behavior (for example, completing homework). (b) Punish a low-probability behavior. (c) Reinforce another high-probability behavior. (d) Extinguish a maladaptive behavior.

    Answer: (a) Reinforce a low-probability behaviour. David Premack's principle (1959) says that a more probable behaviour reinforces a less probable one when access to it is made contingent on the less probable one: video games only after homework. It is a principle of reinforcement, not of punishment or extinction, and the reversed or same-level pairings are the standard bait.

  12. Proponent of the Token Economy as a psychosocial therapeutic technique: (Amity 2023)

    (a) B.F. Skinner (b) Pavlov (c) Ayllon and Azrin (d) None

    Answer: (c) Ayllon and Azrin. The printed key gives (a) B. F. Skinner, and that key is wrong when Ayllon and Azrin are offered: Teodoro Ayllon and Nathan Azrin developed the token economy as a therapeutic technique at Anna State Hospital from 1961 and described it in their 1968 book. Skinner supplied the operant principles behind it, and Pavlov's work is classical conditioning.

  13. Which of the following is not a treatment developed from classical conditioning principles? (RML 2022)

    (a) Flooding (b) Token economies (c) Systematic desensitisation (d) Aversion therapies

    Answer: (b) Token economies. A token economy is operant: tokens are secondary (generalised) reinforcers earned for target behaviours and exchanged for backup reinforcers. Flooding, systematic desensitisation and aversion therapy all change what a stimulus elicits, which is classical conditioning.

  14. A parent deliberately ignores a child's temper tantrums in an attempt to discourage them. The parent's strategy is in accordance with: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Negative reinforcement (b) Extinction (c) Operant escape (d) Operant avoidance

    Answer: (b) Extinction. The printed key gives (c) operant escape, and it is wrong; the site has corrected it to extinction. The tantrums were maintained by attention, and withholding that attention weakens them. Operant escape and operant avoidance are both forms of negative reinforcement, which strengthens behaviour, so neither they nor (a) can be a way of discouraging tantrums.

  15. A therapist uses "time-out" to manage a child's aggressive outbursts. Time-out is technically classified as a form of: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Positive Punishment (b) Negative Reinforcement (c) Negative Punishment (d) Positive Reinforcement

    Answer: (c) Negative Punishment. Time-out removes access to play and attention (something pleasant is taken away) and makes the outbursts less frequent, which is negative punishment. Positive punishment adds something unpleasant, and either kind of reinforcement would make the outbursts more frequent.

  16. The behavioral technique that involves removing a desirable stimulus following an undesirable behavior in order to reduce the frequency of that behavior is called: (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Negative reinforcement (b) Response cost (c) Positive punishment (d) Extinction

    Answer: (b) Response cost. Losing a reinforcer the person already has, contingent on a problem behaviour, is response cost, a form of negative punishment. Negative reinforcement increases behaviour, positive punishment adds an unpleasant stimulus, and extinction stops delivering the reinforcer that used to follow the behaviour rather than taking away something already held.

  17. A child with autism is taught to request a "break" using a picture card instead of engaging in a tantrum. This intervention is a key component of: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Cognitive-Behavioral Therapy (CBT) (b) Applied Behavior Analysis (ABA) (c) Play Therapy (d) Psychoanalytic Psychotherapy

    Answer: (b) Applied Behavior Analysis (ABA). Teaching a communicative response that earns the same reinforcer as the tantrum (here, a break) is functional communication training (Carr and Durand, 1985), a form of differential reinforcement of alternative behaviour and a core ABA procedure. CBT, play therapy and psychoanalytic psychotherapy do not work by reinforcing a replacement behaviour.

  18. Over-correction is a behavioural technique to manage: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Bed wetting in children (b) Repetitive aggressive behaviour in autistic children (c) Temper tantrum in children (d) Alienation in adolescent

    Answer: (b) Repetitive aggressive behaviour in autistic children. Foxx and Azrin developed overcorrection (1972, 1973) for aggressive, disruptive and self-stimulatory behaviour in people with intellectual disability and autism. Bedwetting is treated mainly with the bell and pad (Azrin's dry-bed training borrowed overcorrection components, which makes (a) tempting), ordinary temper tantrums with extinction and time-out, and adolescent alienation is not a target behaviour.

  19. The "Lovaas programme" utilises behaviour modification techniques for the treatment of : (RML 2022)

    (a) Autism (b) Aphasia (c) Acute depression (d) Alcoholism

    Answer: (a) Autism. Ole Ivar Lovaas's Young Autism Project at UCLA gave young children with autism intensive behavioural teaching, mainly discrete trial training at about 40 hours a week, and reported in 1987 that 47 percent reached normal functioning against 2 percent of controls. Aphasia is a language disorder after brain injury, depression is treated behaviourally with behavioural activation, and alcoholism was the target of aversion therapy.

  20. In behaviour therapy, the patient is taught the methods of progressive deep relaxation based on the work of : (RML 2022)

    (a) Wolpe (b) Skinner (c) Jacobson (d) Ivan Pavlov

    Answer: (c) Jacobson. Edmund Jacobson developed progressive muscle relaxation (Progressive Relaxation, 1929; second edition 1938). Wolpe borrowed and shortened it as the first step of systematic desensitisation, which makes (a) the tempting wrong answer; Skinner and Pavlov had no part in it.

  21. The technique that is based on the idea that the autonomic nervous system can come under voluntary control through operant conditioning is known as (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Biofeedback. (b) Hypnosis. (c) Sublimation. (d) Systematic desensitization.

    Answer: (a) Biofeedback. The claim that autonomic responses can be operantly conditioned, linked to Neal Miller, underlies biofeedback, in which an instrument feeds back a bodily process so that the person can learn to control it. Hypnosis works by suggestion, sublimation is a Freudian defence mechanism, and systematic desensitisation is classical counterconditioning.

  22. Habit reversal training (HRT) is primarily used as a behavioral intervention to reduce which type of repetitive behavior? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Tics (b) Stuttering (c) Obsessive-compulsive behaviors (d) Aggressive behaviors

    Answer: (a) Tics. Azrin and Nunn's habit reversal training (1973) is primarily a treatment for tics and other repetitive habits such as hair-pulling and nail-biting. Stuttering tempts because they adapted the method for it (regulated breathing, 1974), but that is not its main use; compulsions are treated with exposure and response prevention, and aggression with operant methods.

  23. Which of the following best describes "behavioural activation" in psychotherapy? (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Encouraging clients to engage in meaningful activities to counteract depression (b) Analysing early childhood experiences (c) Relaxation training for anxiety (d) Hypnosis for symptom relief

    Answer: (a) Encouraging clients to engage in meaningful activities to counteract depression. Behavioural activation schedules rewarding and meaningful activity to restore response-contingent positive reinforcement and reduce avoidance (Lewinsohn's theory; Martell, Addis and Jacobson's manual). Analysing childhood is psychodynamic, relaxation training belongs to the treatment of anxiety, and hypnosis plays no part in it.

  24. An intervention in which the person deliberately does, or wishes for, the very opposite of the therapeutic goal in order to reach that goal is: (NIMHANS 2026 recall)

    (a) Paradoxical intention (b) Dereflection (c) Negative practice (d) Reciprocal inhibition

    Answer: (a) Paradoxical intention. Viktor Frankl's paradoxical intention asks the client to intend or wish for the feared symptom, which removes the anticipatory anxiety that keeps it going. Dereflection, Frankl's other technique, turns attention away from the self; negative practice repeats a voluntary habit to weaken it; reciprocal inhibition is Wolpe's principle of pairing an incompatible response with the feared stimulus.

  25. Which of the following is not a part of Parent Management Training? (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Attending positive behaviour (b) Rewarding positive behaviour (c) Ignoring undesirable behaviour (d) Negative reinforcement

    Answer: (d) Negative reinforcement. Parent management training teaches parents to attend to and reward positive behaviour and to ignore minor misbehaviour. Negative reinforcement, as when a parent gives in to end a tantrum and the child learns that screaming removes demands, is the coercive trap the training is designed to break, so it is the odd one out.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. Joseph Wolpe (1915 to 1997) developed systematic desensitisation (Psychotherapy by Reciprocal Inhibition, 1958), used mainly for phobias: relaxation training, an anxiety hierarchy, then desensitisation proper, in which the relaxed client imagines (or meets) each item from least to most feared, moving up only when calm. Not Rogers, Bandura, Freud, Skinner, Pavlov or Beck, and cognitive restructuring is not one of its steps. asked 14 times
  2. EMDR was developed by Francine Shapiro (effect noticed 1987, first study 1989) for post-traumatic stress disorder: the client holds the traumatic memory, with its emotions and thoughts, in mind while following therapist-guided eye movements (bilateral stimulation). Not Foa, Linehan, Beck, van der Kolk or Ogden, and not for phobia, OCD or eating disorders. asked 7 times
  3. Exposure and response prevention (introduced by Victor Meyer, 1966; operationalised by Edna Foa) is the first-line psychological treatment for obsessive-compulsive disorder, and it rests on habituation and extinction, not counterconditioning or reciprocal inhibition. asked 7 times
  4. Systematic desensitisation rests on Wolpe's principle of reciprocal inhibition: a response incompatible with anxiety (deep relaxation), evoked in the presence of the feared stimulus, weakens the bond between the stimulus and anxiety. Not operant extinction, the law of effect, habituation alone or two-factor theory. asked 7 times
  5. Shaping is the reinforcement of successive approximations to a target behaviour, so that simple responses are reinforced in sequence until the complex one appears; chaining links steps, prompting adds a cue and fading removes it. asked 5 times
  6. The token economy is operant: tokens are secondary (generalised) reinforcers exchanged for backup reinforcers. In "which is NOT from classical conditioning?" it is the odd one out, because aversion therapy (pairing an unwanted behaviour with an unpleasant stimulus), flooding and systematic desensitisation are classical. asked 6 times
  7. The Premack principle (David Premack, 1959): a high-probability behaviour reinforces a low-probability one (video games allowed only after homework); a technique of behaviour modification used especially with children, and never the reverse. asked 5 times
  8. Flooding is prolonged exposure to the most feared stimulus at full intensity, with escape prevented and no hierarchy and no relaxation, until the anxiety subsides (the acrophobic kept on a balcony); it can be in vivo or imaginal. asked 4 times
  9. Extinction is the weakening of a behaviour when the reinforcer that maintained it no longer follows: deliberately ignoring tantrums (the Amity 2023 key "operant escape" is wrong), "habit weakening through the elimination of any reward", "a previously reinforced behaviour fades". asked 3 times
  10. Biofeedback rests on the idea, linked to Neal Miller, that autonomic processes can be brought under voluntary control by operant conditioning: the client controls a physiological process using real-time feedback from an instrument. Kegel's perineometer (1948) was an early form, and EMG biofeedback is the classic treatment for tension-type headache. asked 4 times
  11. The token economy as a therapeutic technique was developed by Teodoro Ayllon and Nathan Azrin (Anna State Hospital, from 1961; book 1968) for chronic psychiatric inpatients. Skinner supplied the principles; the Amity 2023 key that gives Skinner is wrong when Ayllon and Azrin are offered. asked 3 times
  12. The 0 to 100 subjective units of distress scale (SUDS) is Wolpe's rating for the items of the hierarchy in systematic desensitisation; not the Fear Survey Schedule, the STAI or the Hamilton scale. asked twice
  13. Behaviour analysis (applied behaviour analysis) is based on operant conditioning; its seven dimensions were set out by Baer, Wolf and Risley (1968). asked twice
  14. Habit reversal training (Azrin and Nunn, 1973) is primarily for tics and other repetitive habits; its competing response is physically incompatible with the habit, inconspicuous and held for about a minute or until the urge passes. asked twice
  15. Paradoxical intention (Viktor Frankl): the person deliberately does or wishes for the very thing he fears, as when an insomniac is told to lie in the dark and try to stay awake; not dereflection, negative practice, stimulus control or sleep restriction. asked twice
  16. Time-out (from positive reinforcement) is negative punishment: it removes access to reinforcement and so reduces the behaviour; it is not positive punishment or negative reinforcement. asked twice
  17. Behavioural activation encourages depressed clients to engage in rewarding, meaningful activities (Lewinsohn's theory, 1974; Martell, Addis and Jacobson, 2001). asked once
  18. Covert sensitisation (Joseph Cautela, 1966 and 1967): the client imagines the unwanted behaviour followed by an unpleasant consequence, to reduce it; not flooding, implosion or desensitisation. asked once
  19. Response cost is the removal of a reinforcer the person already has after an undesirable behaviour, to reduce it; a form of negative punishment. asked once
  20. Counterconditioning (Mary Cover Jones, 1924) attaches a new, incompatible response to a feared stimulus, so the client learns that the situation is no longer threatening. asked once
  21. Progressive (deep) muscle relaxation is Edmund Jacobson's (Progressive Relaxation, 1929; second edition 1938), not Wolpe's, Skinner's or Pavlov's. asked once
  22. The Lovaas programme (Ole Ivar Lovaas, UCLA) treats autism with intensive discrete trial training; his 1987 report claimed normal functioning in 47 percent of treated children against 2 percent of controls. asked once
  23. Teaching a child with autism to ask for a break with a picture card instead of having a tantrum is functional communication training (Carr and Durand, 1985), a core procedure of applied behaviour analysis. asked once
  24. Reactivity: recording a behaviour changes it, even before treatment begins; it is what makes self-monitoring therapeutic. asked once
  25. Overcorrection (Foxx and Azrin, 1972) manages repetitive aggressive, disruptive or self-stimulatory behaviour in autistic children and others with developmental disability; its forms are restitutional and positive practice. asked once
  26. Negative reinforcement is not part of parent management training: parents are taught to attend to and reward good behaviour and to ignore minor misbehaviour. asked once
  27. Habituation in exposure therapy is the reduction in anxiety through repeated encounter with the feared stimulus; an increase in fear is sensitisation. asked once
  28. Behavioural therapies apply learning principles to eliminate the unwanted behaviour (and build adaptive behaviour), not to treat underlying causes or to build insight. asked once
  29. The front-line treatment for schizophrenia is antipsychotic medication; social skills training, stress management and CBT are additions to it. asked once
  30. The variable-ratio schedule produces the highest rate of responding and the greatest resistance to extinction. asked once
